<meta>This page introduces the biography and intellectual significance of the philosopher Zhu Xi (Choo-foo-tsze), establishing his role as the definitive interpreter of Confucian thought.</meta>

# LIFE OF CHOO-FOO-TSZE.

<image-desc>An ornamental typographical divider consisting of a central leaf motif with symmetrical horizontal flourishes and scrolls.</image-desc>

The writings of Choo-tsze <note>Zhu Xi, 1130–1200 AD, the most influential Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> come next to the Classics in the opinion of the Chinese. “As regards the learning of Confucius, Choo alone,” say the Chinese historians, “fully comprehended its true import; and has transmitted it to future generations so perfect and immaculate, that were Confucius himself, or any of the ancient Sages to come back to life, they would not alter what he has written.”<sup>1</sup> “In discussing the meaning of <term>Spirits and Gods</term> <note>original: "鬼 神 Kwei Shin"; refers to the dual nature of spiritual beings or ancestral forces</note>,” says Dr. Medhurst, “we shall be greatly aided by the analyzation of a treatise on the subject by <term>Master Zhu</term> <note>original: "朱 夫 子 Choo-foo-tsze"</note>, the learned commentator on the Four Books, and the elucidator of the five Classics, who, by fixing the sense of the standard writings of the Chinese, has created, as it were the mind of China, and established a system from which all subsequent writers have borrowed, and according to which all modern essayists must be conformed, or they cannot succeed at the literary examinations, through which alone distinction and power can be attained. The opinions of Choo-foo-tsze, therefore, constitute the orthodoxy of China, and all who differ from him are considered heterodox, insomuch that some modern writers, who have dared to dissent from his views, have not only failed in obtaining office, but have also been prevented, through fear of persecution, from publishing their lucubrations <note>laborious studies or writings</note>.”<sup>2</sup>

This philosopher flourished during the <term>Sung Dynasty</term> <note>960–1279 AD, a period of great cultural and intellectual achievement</note>. He was born <term>A. D. 1130</term>, in the reign of <term>Kaou-tsung</term> at <term>Hwuy-chow</term>, in the province of <term>Ngan-hwuy</term>; and died <term>A. D. 1201</term> at the age of 71. His father was a Member of the Board of Office. It is related that when Choo-tsze was only four years of age, his father, pointing to the sky, pronounced the word “Heaven,” whereupon the child, to the father’s astonishment, asked the question, “What is there above it?” At eight years of age he mastered

It highlights his childhood precocity and his unrivaled authority in interpreting the Confucian Classics.</summary>

### LIFE OF ZHU XI (CHOO-FOO-TSZE)

...prefect of <term>Zhangzhou</term> <note>original: Chang-chow; a city in Fujian province</note>. The manners and customs of the people under his jurisdiction there had significantly declined. Some neglected to wear mourning clothes upon the death of their parents. The women frequented Buddhist monasteries to perform religious rites, and some even left their homes to become nuns. <term>Zhu Xi</term> <note>original: Choo-tsze</note> strictly prohibited all these practices. He also printed copies of the <term>Five Classics</term> <note>The classic texts of Confucianism: the Books of Odes, Documents, Rites, Changes, and the Spring and Autumn Annals</note> and the <term>Four Books</term> <note>The canonical works selected by Zhu Xi: the Great Learning, the Doctrine of the Mean, the Analects, and the Mencius</note>, and circulated them among the people. Addressing one of his pupils on a certain occasion, he remarked, “When I commenced the study of philosophy, there were many principles which I never expected to master; but now I find that every doubt regarding them has gone.” So great was the progress which Zhu Xi had made in the study of the writings of <term>Confucius</term> <note>The Latinized name of Kong Fuzi, the 6th-century BCE founder of the Confucian tradition</note>.

On receiving the appointment of Imperial Essayist and Reader to the <term>Emperor Ningzong</term> <note>reigned 1194–1224; he was the fourth emperor of the Southern Song Dynasty</note>, one of his disciples remarked that His Majesty administered the government with a pure heart, and asked his Master what he considered as requiring the most urgent attention? Zhu Xi replied, “Such is the state of affairs at present, that nothing short of a great and thorough reform will <unclear>suffice</unclear> to move the <term>Mind of Heaven</term> <note>an important Neo-Confucian concept suggesting a moral resonance between the ruler's heart and the cosmic order</note>, or to rejoice the hearts of men. As to myself, I know it to be my duty to act with the utmost degree of sincerity and assiduity. For anything further than this, it is not my province to be concerned.”

“Under ordinary circumstances, Zhu was accustomed,” as his biographer states, “to rise before day, dress in plain clothes, a broad scholar's cap, and square-toed shoes, and then to worship at the domestic shrine, and at that of the ancient sages; then, to repair to his study, where his chairs, tables, etc., must all be in order and his books and writing utensils in their proper places. At his meals, he ordered that the table furniture, and the dishes of soup and rice should all be arranged in a certain order, and that his chopsticks and spoons should have their fixed places. When fatigued by study he would rest himself, closing his eyes and sitting erect; and when refreshed, he would rise and with measured steps walk about for relaxation. At midnight he would retire, and if he chanced to awake in the night, he would wrap himself up in a quilt and sit in bed, sometimes until daylight. His countenance was grave and manly; his speech loud and distinct, his gait easy and dignified; he sat straight and erect; and

<summary>This page describes Zhu Xi's efforts to enforce Confucian social norms in Zhangzhou and his later role as an imperial advisor, concluding with a detailed account of his highly disciplined daily routine and physical comportment.</summary>

### LIFE OF <term>ZHU XI</term> <note>original: Choo-foo-tsze; the preeminent Neo-Confucian scholar of the Song Dynasty (1130–1200)</note>

...his whole manner and bearing were impressive. From youth to old age, in summer and in winter, and in all the vicissitudes <note>The changes of circumstances or fortune, typically those that are unwelcome or unpleasant</note> of time and place, he never for a moment departed from this manner of life. In the third month of the year 1201, <term>Zhu Xi</term> <note>original: Choo-tsze</note> fell sick; and on the day on which he died, having ordered his attendants to place his bed in the central hall <note>The formal reception area of a Chinese home, used here to show his adherence to ritual propriety even in death</note>, he rose about noon and sat erect; and then, adjusting his hat and dress, he quietly lay down and died.

A list of the philosopher's works is given by his biographer <term>Gao Yu</term> <note>original: Kaou-yu</note>; see the *Chinese Repository* <note>An influential 19th-century English-language periodical published in Canton/Macau documenting Chinese history and culture</note>, vol. xviii, p. 206, from which the above sketch of his life is abridged.

<meta>This page continues the introduction's exploration of ancient cosmogony, moving from Platonic and Neoplatonic views of a "world-soul" to a comparative analysis of the Chinese Confucian system.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

framer of the <term>Universe<note>Original: "Kosmos," the Greek term for the world seen as an ordered, harmonious system.</note></term>. This Mind or second God is he whom “the generations of men take for the First, they looking up no higher than to the immediate architect of the world.”$^1$ This idea of an animated <term>Universe</term> is confessedly taken from Man. <note>Ralph Cudworth, whose work is cited here, was a 17th-century philosopher who compared Western and Eastern thought.</note> Plotinus, for instance, thus states the opinion of the ancient philosophers on this point: “It is absurd to affirm that Heaven (or the World) is inanimate or devoid of life and soul, when we ourselves, who have but a part of the mundane body in us are endued with soul. For, how could a part have life and soul in it, the whole being dead and inanimate?”$^2$

The Confucian system as given in the text of the Classics, is precisely similar to this. In the *Yih King* <note>The *I Ching* or Book of Changes, an ancient manual of divination and philosophy.</note>, the great authority on <term>Cosmogony<note>The branch of science or philosophy dealing with the origin of the universe.</note></term>, and the oldest Chinese book in existence, the Universe or “Heaven” is declared to be a Great Man, and its eight portions <note>The Eight Trigrams, or *Bagua*, which represent the fundamental principles of reality.</note> are stated to correspond to eight parts of the human body; *for example*: *Khëen* (Heaven) is the head; *Khwăn* (Earth) is the bowels and womb; *Ching* (Thunder) is the feet; *Seuen* (Wind) is the thighs; *Kan* (Water) is the ears; *Le* (Fire) is the eyes; *Kăn* (Mountain) is the hands; and *Tuy* (Lake) is the mouth.”$^3$

Man, according to the Confucianists, is a compound of Mind and Body, Mind being the Ruler; secondly, the body they regard as twofold, the head being the superior portion, and the feet and lower part the inferior portion; thirdly, the Mind or Soul is also twofold, partly **Rational** (<gloss>hún; the spiritual soul</gloss>) and partly **Irrational or Sentient** (<gloss>pò; the physical soul</gloss>), the former ruling chiefly in the head and upper portion of the body, yet pervading the whole being. Lastly, inherent in this Mind is the Divine Reason, which “makes Mind to be Mind.” Mind is pure ether, while the sentient portion of this soul is grosser Air; and the Divine Reason is designated the **First God** (<gloss>zhì shén; the Supreme Divinity</gloss>); the “God who adorns all things” (of the *Yih King*) and unites with the Rational Soul, the subtle Ether, which is therefore styled **God** (<gloss>shén; spirit or deity</gloss>); and this latter, in the Universe, is the second God or “Mind” or *Shang-te* “the **Supreme Emperor**;” the <term>Demiurgic<note>A term from Greek philosophy referring to an artisan-like figure responsible for fashioning and maintaining the physical universe.</note></term> Ruler and framer of all things. All this is transferred from Man to the Universe which is designated “Heaven,” and is declared to be a “Great Man,” while Man is regarded as “a little Heaven.”

3. Book iv., chapter ix.

It further explains the Confucian division of the soul into rational and sentient components, paralleling the structure of the divine hierarchy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s argument that the Confucian understanding of the universe parallels Western classical philosophy, specifically focusing on the identity of the "First God" and the "World-Soul."</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

Hea-ven or <term>Microcosm<note>From the Greek "mikros kosmos," meaning "little world"—the idea that a human being reflects the structure of the entire universe.</note></term>. This parallel is exact, and the animated ether or <term>Supreme Ruler<note>Original: "Shang-te" (上帝), the highest deity in the ancient Chinese classics.</note></term> is precisely the same in every respect as the animated ether or Jupiter. Both these Gods are the Light, the firstborn from chaos; both are designated "Mind," and both are the Rational soul of the <term>Universe<note>Original: "Kosmos," a Greek term for the world as an ordered system.</note></term>; the <term>Supreme Ruler</term> being "the <term>God<note>Original: "神" (Shen), the term used for divinity or spirit.</note></term> of Heaven," and Jupiter being the <term>God<note>Original: "θεος" (theos) or "deus" in Greek and Latin respectively.</note></term> of Heaven; the three terms <term>God<note>The author is referring to the Chinese "Shen," the Greek "Theos," and the Latin "Deus."</note></term>, God, and God, being thus alike applied to the *soul of the Universe*.

The Greeks designated their First God (<term>God<note>Original Greek: "θεος" (theos).</note></term>) Fate, Reason, Nature &c., and regarded Him as an Indivisible Unity; and the Confucianists designate their First God (<term>God<note>Original Chinese: "神" (Shen).</note></term>) Fate, Reason, Nature, &c., and regard Him as an Indivisible Unity. Plato designates this God "the Adorner," and the <term>Book of Changes<note>Original: "Yih King" (I Ching), one of the oldest Chinese texts, used for divination and philosophy.</note></term> designates Him "He who <term>adorns<note>Original: "妙" (miao), implying a subtle, mysterious, or exquisite creation.</note></term> the myriad of things."$^1$ Mind or the second God, the Greeks designated <term>Divine-Spirit<note>Original: "Δαιμων-θεος" (Daimon-theos). In ancient Greek thought, a 'daimon' was a guardian spirit or lesser deity, not necessarily an evil entity.</note></term>, and this same Mind the Confucianists also designate <term>Spirit-god<note>Original: "鬼神" (Gui-shen), referring to the spirits of the dead and the gods of the natural world.</note></term>. Amongst the Greeks and Romans the Rational soul was derived from this ethereal Mind and was therefore designated <term>God<note>Original: "θεος" (theos) or "deus".</note></term>, or God: and in the Confucian Classics the Rational soul is said to be derived from the same ethereal Mind, and is therefore designated <term>God<note>Original: "神" (Shen).</note></term> or God. In a word, every part of the *Universe* and every thing which the Greeks and Romans respectively designated "<term>Demon<note>Used here in the sense of 'daimon'—a supernatural being or power.</note></term>," that the Confucianists also designate "Demon;" and *every thing* which the former designated <term>God<note>Original: "θεος" or "deus".</note></term> or God, that the latter designate <term>God<note>Original: "神" (Shen).</note></term>. Any argument, therefore, supposed to prove that the term <term>God<note>Original: "神" (Shen).</note></term> means "spirit" and not "God," must also be admitted to prove that the terms <term>God<note>Original: "θεος" and "deus".</note></term> mean "Spirit" and not "God." These three terms must necessarily bear precisely the same meaning for the reasons stated. The fact that the Chinese term <term>God<note>Original: "神" (Shen).</note></term>, (like <term>God<note>Original: "θεος" or "deus"</note></term>) is the designation of that which pervades and animates <term>the all<note>Original: "το παν" (to pan), a Greek philosophical term for the totality of the universe.</note></term> must be regarded as proof that this term means "God" in the sense of that term as used by all pagan philosophers, and not pure "Spirit," of which latter, in fact, *they know nothing whatever*.

Plate I will assist the student to understand what has been stated as to the animated *Universe*.

The author maintains that in both traditions, these terms describe the "World-Soul" that animates the universe rather than a "pure spirit" in the modern Christian sense.</summary>

### EXPLANATION OF PLATE I.

Figures 1, 2, and 3, placed one over the other (meaning 3 is atop 2, and both are atop 1) so as to form one globe, represent the Greek <term>Kosmos<note>A term derived from the Greek word for "order" or "world," used here to describe the universe as an organized, living system.</note></term> or Man in his entirety. Likewise, Figures 4, 5, and 6, arranged in the same way, represent the Confucian Universe or Man in his entirety.

Figure 3, placed over figure 2, represents the Mind (fig. 2) and Body (fig. 3) of the Universe or Man according to the Greeks—and the Romans, if one substitutes God <note>original Latin: *Deus*</note> for God <note>original Greek: θεὸς</note>. Figure 6, placed over figure 5, represents the Mind and Body of the Universe or Man according to the Confucianists.

In both systems, the Mind (figs. 2 and 5) is twofold, consisting of a Rational part and a sentient <note>feeling or perceiving</note> part.

Figures 3 and 6 represent the visible world, which both systems define as "Heaven." The Earth is represented by a mountain because, in both systems, the highest mountain—the dwelling place of the Gods—is believed to be the first land to appear as each flood of chaos subsides.

In both cases, the living Universe (fig. 2 dwelling within fig. 3, and fig. 5 dwelling within fig. 6) is simply the First Man in his worldly or divine character. He is made divine by treating his soul as the Deity Himself, or the Divine Reason (figs. 1 and 4 dwelling within figs. 2 and 5 respectively); the <term>Hegemonikon<note>The "ruling faculty" or commanding part of the soul in classical philosophy, responsible for reason and judgment.</note></term> serves as the Rational portion of the soul.

"In attempting to define the terms used by Chinese authors, we must also keep in mind their theory that man is a microcosm <note>original Chinese: 人為小天地 (*rén wéi xiǎo tiān dì*), literally meaning "Man is a small Heaven and Earth."</note>, or heaven and earth in miniature."*

---

* The Theology of the Chinese, p. 2.

Inherent in this “Mind” is the Divine Reason. See Plate I., Fig. 4.

Fig. 2 represents this mass of Primordial Air divided into <term>Subtile<note>The refined, light, and heavenly energy.</note></term> and <term>Gross<note>The heavy, dense, and earthly energy.</note></term>; or Light and Darkness.

# INTRODUCTION.

### EXPLANATION OF PLATE III.

->*The Complete Lord on High <note>original: "Shang-te," 上帝 (Shàngdì). Historically used to refer to the Supreme Deity or Celestial Emperor in Chinese theology.</note>.*<-

**Fig. 1** represents the First God who is styled **Fate**, **Incorporeal Reason** <note>This likely refers to *Li* (理), the principle or logic governing the universe.</note>, **The Infinite** <note>original: *Wuji* (無極), the state of non-being before creation.</note>, **The Good**, **The (Incorporeal) Great Extreme** <note>original: *Taiji* (太極), the ultimate source of all things.</note>, **Nature the Adorner**, etc. He is the **Great Vacuum**, an **Immovable Mover** <note>A reference to Aristotelian philosophy used here to explain Chinese concepts.</note>, **Incomprehensible**, **Omnipresent**, an **Indivisible Unity**, etc.

**Figs. 2 and 3** represent the whole mass of the **Primordial Air** <note>original: *Qi* (氣), the vital energy or substance of which all things are made.</note>, the subtle and the gross in the arranged **Universe** <note>original: *Kosmos* (Greek: κόσμος). In this context, it refers to the ordered world.</note>, in which the first God is inherent, the light being His **Ruling Principle** <note>original: *Hegemonikon* (Greek: ἡγεμονικόν). A Stoic term for the "governing part" of the soul.</note>. These two circles form the animated universe. **Fig 2** is the **Mind** or twofold soul, partly **Rational**, and partly **sentient** (**Light and Darkness** <note>Refers to *Yin* and *Yang*.</note>), the **Great Spirit-god** <note>The original text uses "Demon-god," likely using the older sense of the word *daemon* (a guiding spirit), rather than a malevolent entity.</note> **Lord on High** (*Shang-te*), who is said to be “material” compared with fig. 1, but “Spiritual” compared with fig. 3, which is the grosser Air or visible world, the **Body** of fig. 2. The **Lord on High** (*Shang-te*) or the animated universe therefore is composed of figs. 2 and 3, united in one as **Body and Soul**, and this complete **Being** or **Divinity** <note>original: *Numen* (Latin). Refers to the divine power presiding over a place or entity.</note> is called “**Heaven**,” “**The Great Monad**,” “**The (corporeal) Great Extreme**,” and (the corporeal portion of) “**Nature**.”

**Fig. 4.** represents these three circles or globes placed one within the other in order to complete the **Being** **Lord on High** (*Shang-te*) or the animated universe. The entire description of this **Great Divinity** is as follows: “His bodily form is designated **Heaven** (fig. 3), the **Ruling power** is designated **Sovereign** <note>original: *Di* (帝).</note> (fig. 2), the **Adorning Function** <note>original: *Yung* (用). In Chinese philosophy, *Ti-Yong* (substance and function) describes how a principle manifests in the world.</note> is designated **God** (or **Spirit**, *Shin* <note>original: *Shen* (神).</note> fig. 1), the essence is designated ***Khëen*** (**The Creative/Hard**) <note>original: *Qián* (乾), the first hexagram of the I Ching, representing Heaven and the male principle.</note>.” “Divided and treated of, then, his bodily form is designated **Heaven**, the **Ruling power** is designated **Sovereign** (*Di*), the **Working Function** (*Kung Yung*) is designated **Spirit-god** (fig. 2), the **Adorning Function** is designated **God** (*Shen*), and the essence is designated ***Khëen***. ***Khëen*** (Rational soul fig. 2), is the **Beginning of the Myriad of things**, and hence he is called **Heaven**, and **Light**, and **Father**, and **Prince**.” —*Classic of Changes <note>original: "Yih King," or *I Ching* (易經).</note>, Book iii, chapter xi, commentary (by the Emperor **Kang-he** <note>The Kangxi Emperor (r. 1661–1722).</note>).* “***Khëen-khwăn*** <note>The hexagrams for Heaven and Earth (*Qián* and *Kūn*).</note> (Fig. 2) is the **Sovereign** (*Shang-te*) who governs the **Myriad of things** and pervades the midst of the **Six Children**” (three sons, and three daughters—their three wives: —*Classic of Changes, Book iv, chapter x, text*). —*Ibid. Book iv, chapter vi, commentary.*

# THE STUDIO OF DEEP REFLECTION
# VOLUME 49 OF THE IMPERIALLY COMMISSIONED COMPLETE WORKS OF MASTER ZHU
# PRINCIPLE AND VITAL FORCE
## GENERAL DISCOURSE

In all the world, there has never been vital force <note>original: *Qi* (氣), the material energy or substance that constitutes all things.</note> without principle <note>original: *Li* (理), the underlying pattern, law, or logic of the universe.</note>, nor has there ever been principle without vital force.

Once this principle exists, it gives rise to this vital force. From the premise that "the alternation of the passive and active forces <note>original: *Yin* and *Yang*</note> constitutes the Way," we can infer that this inherent nature naturally possesses benevolence and righteousness.

First, there is the Heavenly Principle; then, there is vital force. As vital force accumulates, it becomes physical substance, and the inner nature is contained within it.

**Question:** Does principle come first, or does vital force?
**Answer:** Principle is never separate from vital force. However, principle is that which is "above form," while vital force is that which is "below form." <note>The terms *Xing’er-shang* (above form) and *Xing’er-xia* (below form) are used to distinguish the abstract, metaphysical realm from the concrete, physical world.</note>

If we speak in terms of being "above" or "below" form, how could there not be a sense of "before" and "after"? Principle has no physical shape. Vital force, by contrast, is coarse and contains dregs <note>original: *zhazi* (渣滓), meaning sediment or impurities; a metaphor for the relative "heaviness" or "opacity" of matter compared to the "transparency" of universal law.</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental Neo-Confucian duality of Principle (Li) and Vital Force (Qi), arguing that while they are inseparable, Principle is logically prior and belongs to the realm of metaphysical formlessness.</summary>
<keywords>Zhu Xi, Principle (Li), Vital Force (Qi), Metaphysics, Yin and Yang, Above Form, Below Form, Heavenly Principle, Benevolence and Righteousness</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Neo-Confucian cosmology of Zhu Xi (Choo-tsze). Following the previous page’s distinction between the "above form" (metaphysical) and "below form" (physical), this section explores the inseparable yet distinct relationship between Principle (Li) and Vital Force (Qi).</meta>

# THE COMPLETE WORKS OF MASTER ZHU

### IMPERIAL EDITION

## SECTION 49

---

# PART I

### PRINCIPLE AND VITAL FORCE—THE GENERAL DISCOURSE

**Paragraph 1.** In the whole Universe there is no such thing as vital force <note>original: *Qi* (氣), the breath or energy that makes up all things.</note> without principle <note>original: *Li* (理), the fundamental law or pattern of the universe.</note>, nor is there principle without vital force.

**2.** Once this principle exists, then this vital force is generated. This follows from the statement in the *Book of Changes* <note>original: *Yih King*</note> that, "The alternating rhythm of the passive and active forces <note>original: *Yin* and *Yang*</note> constitutes the Way." This nature (that is, the principle inherent in vital force) is spontaneously benevolent and upright.

**3.** This Heavenly Principle <note>original: *Tianli*</note> first existing, then most certainly the vital force exists. As the vital force accumulates, it forms physical substance, and the inner nature is then complete.

**4.** When asked whether principle or vital force is prior in point of time, he replied: Principle is never separate from the vital force, but principle is incorporeal <note>literally "above form"</note>, while the vital force is corporeal <note>literally "below form"</note>. In this respect, therefore, must there not be a priority and posteriority? Principle is incorporeal, while the vital force is coarse and contains dregs <note>meaning it has physical impurities or "sediment" compared to the purity of universal law.</note>.

---

Master Zhu’s commentary on this passage is: "The revolving passive and active forces are the vital force; the inherent principle is that which is designated as the Way <note>original: *Dao*</note>." The passive and active forces are merely the two divisions of the Primordial Vital Force. The vital force establishes the inherent principle by acting as a receptacle for it; and the inherent principle (also called destiny or nature) gives a name to the whole compound. Hence, the Primordial Vital Force is merely the ethereal vehicle of the inherent principle—the better nature <note>original: *Melior Natura*</note>.

<summary>This page defines the metaphysical relationship between Principle (Li) and Vital Force (Qi), arguing that while they are inseparable in the physical world, Principle is logically prior and incorporeal.</summary>
<keywords>Zhu Xi, Principle (Li), Vital Force (Qi), Yin and Yang, Book of Changes (I Ching), Metaphysics, Primordial Air, Neo-Confucianism</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Neo-Confucian cosmology of Zhu Xi. Following the previous page’s distinction between the "above form" (metaphysical) and "below form" (physical), this section explores the inseparable yet distinct relationship between Principle (Li) and Vital Force (Qi), specifically addressing the logical priority of Principle and the cycle of movement and stillness in the Supreme Ultimate (Taiji).</meta>

# THE COMPLETE WORKS OF MASTER ZHU

## SECTION 49

---

# PART I (CONTINUED)

### PRINCIPLE AND VITAL FORCE—THE GENERAL DISCOURSE

**Paragraph 5.** Principle and vital force <note>original: *Li* (理) and *Qi* (氣)</note> cannot essentially be spoken of in terms of priority or posteriority. However, if one must trace their origin, one must say that Principle existed first. Yet, Principle is not a separate thing; it exists within the vital force. Without the vital force, Principle would have no place to attach itself. The vital force constitutes the physical elements like metal, wood, water, and fire, while Principle manifests as the moral virtues of benevolence, righteousness, propriety, and wisdom.

**6.** When asked about the relationship between Principle and the vital force, Master Zhu replied: Master Yi Chuan <note>Cheng Yi (1033–1107), a foundational Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> explained it well when he said, "Principle is one, but its manifestations are many." <note>original: *Li yi fen shu* (理一分殊). This is a core concept suggesting that while there is one universal truth, it expresses itself uniquely in every individual object or person.</note> Speaking of Heaven, Earth, and the myriad things collectively, there is only one universal Principle. But when it comes to human beings, each person possesses their own individual expression of that Principle.

**7.** Where there is this Principle, there is this vital force. But Principle is the root. For now, let us speak from the perspective of Principle. For instance, it is said that the Supreme Ultimate <note>original: *Taiji* (太極), the ultimate design or source of all existence</note> moves and generates the active force <note>original: *Yang* (陽)</note>; having moved to its limit, it becomes still. In stillness, it generates the passive force <note>original: *Yin* (陰)</note>. It is not the case that before movement there was no stillness. Master Cheng said: "Movement and stillness have no beginning." This is because we usually begin our descriptions from the point of movement. If we discuss what comes before movement, there is stillness; and before that stillness, there was movement. 

**8.** As it is said in the *Book of Changes*, "The alternating rhythm of the passive and active forces constitutes the Way <note>original: *Dao*</note>; that which continues it is goodness." This word "continues" is the very beginning of movement. If there were only an opening and a closing without a continuation, the cycle would simply cease and be "dead." When asked if "continuation" represents the point between movement and stillness, Master Zhu replied: "It is the end of stillness <note>implying that the transition to movement begins at the very moment stillness reaches its conclusion.</note>."

### TERMINOLOGY GUIDE
*   **Principle (Li):** The fundamental law, pattern, or "reason" behind the universe.
*   **Vital Force (Qi):** The physical energy, breath, or matter that gives form to things.
*   **Yi Chuan:** The honorary name for Cheng Yi, a major influence on Master Zhu.
*   **One Principle, Many Manifestations:** The idea that all things share the same universal law but express it differently.
*   **Benevolence, Righteousness, Propriety, Wisdom:** The four cardinal virtues of Confucianism.
*   **Supreme Ultimate (Taiji):** The source of all things, containing both movement and stillness.
*   **Movement and Stillness:** The cyclical nature of the universe.
*   **Passive and Active Forces (Yin and Yang):** The two complementary energies of the natural world.

<summary>This page explores the codependency of Principle (Li) and Vital Force (Qi), asserting that while logically Principle is the "root," it cannot exist without the physical energy of Qi. It also discusses the cyclical nature of the Supreme Ultimate (Taiji) through the lenses of movement and stillness.</summary>
<keywords>Zhu Xi, Principle and Vital Force, Li and Qi, Taiji, Supreme Ultimate, Yin and Yang, Cheng Yi, Li yi fen shu, Four Virtues, Neo-Confucianism</keywords>

# THE COMPLETE WORKS OF MASTER ZHU

## SECTION 49

---

# PART I (CONTINUED)

### PRINCIPLE AND VITAL FORCE—THE GENERAL DISCOURSE

**Paragraph 5.** Strictly speaking, we cannot say whether Principle <note>original: "Fate" (理, *Li*). Older translations often used "Fate" or "Law" for Principle.</note> or the Vital Force <note>original: "Air" (氣, *Qi*). Here referring to the material substance of the universe.</note> came first in terms of time. Yet, if we are discussing their logical origins, we must say that Principle has priority. Nevertheless, Principle is not a separate, independent entity; it is preserved within the Vital Force. If the Vital Force did not exist, Principle would have nowhere to reside. The Vital Force manifests as the physical elements—Metal, Wood, Water, and Fire—while Principle manifests as the moral virtues: Benevolence, Righteousness <note>original: "Rectitude" (義, *Yi*)</note>, Propriety, and Wisdom.

**6.** When asked about the relationship between Principle and the Vital Force together, Master Zhu said: Master Yi Chuan <note>Cheng Yi (1033–1107)</note> expressed the matter well when he said that Principle is essentially one, but its manifestations are diverse <note>original: *Li yi fen shu* (理一分殊)</note>. Looking at Heaven, Earth, and the myriad of things collectively, they are all part of one single Principle. Regarding individual human beings, each person possesses their own specific expression of that Principle within them.

**7.** Where Principle exists, the Vital Force also exists, yet Principle is the "root." Now, if we speak of Principle as the starting point—for instance, when it is said, "The Supreme Ultimate <note>original: "The Great Extreme" (太極, *Taiji*)</note> moved and generated the Light <note>original: *Yang* (陽)</note>; having moved to the utmost, it rested, and in resting, it generated the Darkness <note>original: *Yin* (陰)</note>"—was there no rest before that first movement? Master Cheng <note>referring to either Cheng Yi or his brother Cheng Hao</note> says that movement and rest have no beginning; this is also a way of speaking about the moment movement begins. If we say that there was rest before movement, and movement before that rest—as it says in the *Book of Changes* <note>original: "Yih King" (*I Ching*)</note>, "The alternation of *Yin* and *Yang* is the Way <note>original: "Reason" (道, *Dao*)</note>; that which continues it is Goodness"—then this term "continues" <note>original: "pivot" (繼, *Ji*)</note> refers to the very beginning of movement. If there were only a single instance of the universe opening and closing, without a "pivot" of perpetual cycles, the universe would simply perish when it closed. When asked whether this "pivot" exists in the center between movement and rest, Master Zhu replied that it is the point where rest ends and movement begins.

<summary>Master Zhu explains that while Principle and Vital Force are co-dependent, Principle is the logical "root" of all things. He explores the cyclical nature of the Supreme Ultimate, where movement and stillness (Yang and Yin) alternate eternally through a continuous "pivot" of creation.</summary>

<keywords>Principle (Li), Vital Force (Qi), Five Elements, Four Virtues, Cheng Yi, Li Yi Fen Shu, Taiji (Supreme Ultimate), Yin and Yang, I Ching, movement and rest</keywords>

# PART I (CONTINUED)

...is the beginning of movement. Take, for instance, the four seasons. When the winter months arrive, the myriad things all return to their "nests" <note>resting places or roots</note>. If there were no further growth, then in the following year, all life would cease. This is because **Persistence returns to become the Origin** <note>original: 貞復生元 (*zhen fu sheng yuan*). This refers to the cycle of the four virtues from the *Book of Changes* (I Ching): Origin, Penetration, Advantage, and Persistence, which correspond to Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter.</note>. It is an infinite cycle, just like this.

**Question:** If there is this Principle <term>Li: the underlying blueprint or moral order of the universe</term>, and afterward there is this Vital Force <term>Qi: the physical substance or energy that makes up all things</term>—before humanity existed, where did this Principle reside?

**Answer:** It was simply right here. It is like the water of the ocean. Whether one takes a single ladleful, a bucketful, or a bowlful, it is all the same ocean water. However, the ocean is the "host," while we are the "guests." The ocean is relatively enduring; we possess a portion of it, but only for a short time.

**Question:** If Principle exists within the Vital Force, how is its manifestation revealed?

**Answer:** Consider the **Yin and Yang** and the **Five Elements** <note>Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</note>. Their intricate interweaving without losing its proper order *is* Principle. If the Vital Force did not condense and gather together, Principle would have nothing to attach itself to.

**Question:** Regarding the theory that "Principle comes first, and Vital Force comes after."

**Answer:** One need not speak of it in those terms. How can we truly know today which came first? Whether Principle was first and Vital Force was later, or vice versa—these are things that cannot be definitively investigated. However, if we judge by logic <note>original: 意度 (*yidu*), meaning to estimate or infer by the mind's intent</note>, we might suspect that this Vital Force moves by **relying upon** <note>original: 依傍 (*yibang*)</note> this Principle. When the Vital Force gathers, Principle is present within it. For the Vital Force has the capacity to condense and create...

<summary>Master Zhu explains that Principle and Vital Force are inseparable in practice, using the metaphor of ocean water to show how individual life partakes in a universal essence. He notes that while Principle is logically prior, it requires the physical condensation of Vital Force to manifest in the world.</summary>

# FATE AND THE VITAL FORCE

Take, for instance, the Four Seasons. In the winter, the myriad things return to their resting places. If there were no further generation, the following year could not exist. But because **Persistence** <note>original: *Ching* (Zhen), the fourth stage of the cycle in the Book of Changes, associated with Winter</note> again generates the **Origin** <note>original: *Yuen* (Yuan), the first stage of the cycle, associated with Spring</note>, there is no end to the present state of things.

8. When asked about the statement, “If Principle exists, then the Vital Force exists,” and where this Principle was before humanity appeared, he replied: It was already in existence even then. It resembles the water of the sea. Whether we take up a single ladleful, two buckets, or a bowlful, it is all the same sea water. However, this Principle is the "host" while I am merely his "guest." Compared to me, Principle is eternal, while I obtain a portion of it and am mortal.

9. When asked how this Principle manifests its presence in the midst of the Vital Force <note>original: "the Air"</note>, he replied: It is this Principle which prevents the **Light and the Darkness** <term>Yin and Yang</term> and the **Five Elements** from becoming so tangled together that they lose their distinctness in chaos. If the Vital Force did not accumulate together, then Principle would have nothing to rest upon.

10. When asked whether Principle exists first and the Vital Force exists afterwards, he replied: It is not necessary to state the matter in those terms. To understand whether, in the **Universe** <note>original: *το παν* (Greek: the all/the whole)</note>, Principle exists first and Vital Force afterwards, or the Vital Force first and Principle afterwards, is something we cannot truly search out. However, if I may offer a conjecture, I suppose that the Vital Force depends upon Principle for its action, and that wherever the Vital Force accumulates, Principle is also there. Now, the Vital Force has the power of condensing and forming physical things; but Principle neither devises, nor plans, nor makes anything. It is simply that wherever the Vital Force condenses and accumulates, Principle is in the midst of it. And, since

...action. Principle, however, possesses no human emotion or intent. It does not calculate, nor does it perform "work" or "fabrication" <note>original: *zao zuo*, referring to conscious or deliberate creation</note>. It is simply that where the Vital Force <term>Qi: the material energy or "air" that makes up all things</term> accumulates, Principle is found within it. Take, for example, the people, plants, and animals within the Universe. In their birth, none are without a seed; it is impossible for something to be born without a seed, appearing out of nowhere. All of this is the work of the Vital Force. As for **Principle**, it is merely a pure, empty, and vast world, without physical form or trace. It cannot act or fabricate. It is the Vital Force that is able to ferment and condense, and thus bring living things into being.

**Question:** Since the existence of this Principle implies the existence of this Vital Force, it seems they cannot be distinguished as coming "before" or "after" one another.

**Answer:** Ultimately, Principle comes first. However, we cannot say that today there is Principle and tomorrow there is Vital Force. Nevertheless, there must be a sequence of priority.

**Question:** Before the Universe <note>original: *Tiandi*, literally "Heaven and Earth"</note> existed, was Principle certainly there first?

**Answer:** Before the Universe existed, there was certainly only Principle. Because this Principle existed, the Universe could then exist. If this Principle did not exist, then there would be no Universe, no humanity, and no objects; there would be nothing to contain or carry them. Once Principle exists, the Vital Force flows and circulates, nourishing and developing the myriad things.

**Question:** Is it Principle itself that performs this "nourishing"?

**Answer:** Because this Principle exists, the Vital Force flows and nourishes. Principle itself has no physical form.

**Question:** Is what we call "Form" <note>original: *Ti*, referring to the substance or body of a thing</note> merely a **forced name** <note>original: *qiang ming*, a reference to the *Dao De Jing*, suggesting that human language is inadequate to describe the ultimate reality, so we "force" a name upon it</note>?

**Answer:** Yes.

Humans and objects, plants and trees, birds and beasts, existing within the Universe <note>original: *Tiandi*, literally "Heaven and Earth"</note>, are all generated from seeds. **11.** Being asked whether Principle and the Vital Force are so intertwined in the formation of things that one cannot say which comes first and which comes last? Because Principle existed, the Universe could then exist. If Principle had no existence, then there also could not be any Universe, or humanity, or objects; none of these would have a vessel to contain them. Once Principle exists, then the Vital Force exists, flowing forth, pervading all, generating, and nourishing the Myriad Things <note>A traditional term for all things in existence</note>. When asked whether the word "bodily" was perhaps too strong an expression to use here, he replied that it was. Another person inquired whether Principle is infinite and the Vital Force is finite. He replied: With regard to limits, what boundary could be assigned to either of them?

It was asked: "Fate <term>Li: traditionally 'Principle,' here translated as 'Fate' to denote the cosmic blueprint</term> is infinite. Does the Air <term>Qi: the 'Vital Force' or material energy of the universe</term> have a limit or not?"

Someone asked whether Fate is prior and the Air is posterior.
He replied: "Essentially, Fate and the Air cannot be spoken of in terms of priority or sequence. However, when we trace their origins upward, it appears as if Fate is prior and the Air is posterior."

Keji asked: "Regarding the 'Great Potter’s Wheel' <note>original: *Dajun*, a metaphor for the creative force of Nature</note> which molds all things—does a thing simply end once it departs? Or is there a principle of departing and then returning?"
He replied: "Once it departs, it simply ends. How could there be 'Air' that, once dispersed, gathers itself back together again?"

Being asked about the expansion and contraction of the Air, he replied: "It is like putting water in a pot to boil. Once the water has boiled away into dryness, new spring water comes as it did before. One cannot take the water that has already dried up and use it again to make more."

A question was asked: "The Lord on High <note>original: *Shangdi*, the personified supreme deity of ancient Chinese texts</note> bestows a moral sense upon the people; Heaven is about to confer a great responsibility on a man; Heaven protects the people and provides them with a ruler; Heaven produces things and treats them according to their inherent capacity; Upon those who do good, Heaven sends down a hundred blessings; upon those who do evil, it sends down a hundred calamities; When Heaven is about to send an extraordinary disaster upon this world, it must first produce an extraordinary person to withstand it. In all these instances, does the vast blue sky <note>original: *Cang-cang*, a poetic term for the heavens</note> above truly possess a Sovereign <term>Zhu-zai: a Master, Governor, or conscious Will</term> who acts in this way?"

Once that water has evaporated away, you would not attempt to take that same steam to refill the cauldron in order to restore the water as it was before, would you? You would need fresh water."

->### THE COSMIC CYCLE AND THE NATURE OF HEAVEN<-

Or perhaps Heaven has no conscious mind; it is simply that if we trace the matter back to its origin, its Principle <term>Li: the underlying law or pattern of all things</term> is naturally so. He said: "These three sections carry only a single meaning. This, too, is simply because the Principle is as it is. The movement of the Vital Force <term>Qi: the material energy or 'breath' of the universe</term> has always consisted of a period of flourishing followed by a period of decline; then decline followed again by flourishing. It simply continues to cycle in this manner. There is never a period of decline that does not eventually lead back to flourishing."

When a person breathes out, the belly actually swells; when one breathes in, the belly actually contracts. By logic, one might expect the belly to contract during exhalation and swell during inhalation, yet it is currently as described. This is because when the breath is exhaled, although that specific breath goes out, the next breath is already being generated, which causes the belly to swell. When one breathes in, that newly generated breath is pushed out from within, and thus the belly contracts. Laozi <note>The legendary founder of Taoism</note> said: "The space between Heaven and Earth, is it not like a bellows? It moves but is never exhausted; the more it is worked, the more it brings forth." A "bellows" <note>original: 橐籥, 'tuoyue'</note> is simply what we today call a forge-fan or wind-box.

Principle hitches a ride upon the Vital Force in order to operate.

If we speak in terms of the Vital Force, we have Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter. If we speak in terms of cosmic Virtues, we have Origination, Penetration, Advantage, and Persistence <note>original: 元亨利貞, 'Yuan, Heng, Li, Zhen'—the four stages of a cycle described in the I-Ching</note>. In human beings, these manifest as Benevolence, Righteousness, Propriety, and Wisdom. Regarding Origination, Penetration, Advantage, and Persistence, the Principle contains these four stages, and the Vital Force also contains these four stages. Principle exists right within the Vital Force; the two can never be separated from one another.

When asked about Principle and Number <term>Shu: numerical proportion or destiny</term>, he said: "Where this Principle exists, there is this Vital Force. Where this Vital Force exists, there is this Number." For "Number" is simply the point where boundaries and limits are defined.

-># FATE AND THE VITAL FORCE<-

Such statements have but one interpretation: it is merely that the Mandate of Heaven <note>The text uses "Fate" to refer to *Ming* (命), the overarching cosmic order or "Mandate."</note> is naturally so. The revolving Vital Force <term>Qi: the "Air" or material energy that makes up all things</term>, from the beginning, has experienced fullness after decline and decline after fullness, ever revolving in a circle. There has never been a period of decline without a period of fullness following it.

17. When a person breathes out, their stomach expands, and when they draw their breath in, it contracts. Regarding the exhalation and contraction of the stomach, and the inhalation and expansion of the stomach, the rule is as follows. Now, since this is the case, when one exhales, although that portion of breath goes out, a succeeding portion is already being generated, and therefore the stomach expands. Upon inhalation, this portion of breath which has been generated is expelled from within <note>This refers to the internal movement of energy rather than the physical air in the lungs</note>, and therefore the stomach contracts. Laozi <note>The legendary founder of Taoism</note> says that the space between Heaven and Earth resembles a bellows; when in motion, it never runs out of air, and when it is emptied, even more is brought forth. The *Fŭh-yŏ* <note>original: 橐籥 'tuoyue'</note> is simply what we call a bellows today.

18. The Mandate <note>Fate</note> unites with the Vital Force and spreads through everything.

19. The original text uses the Latin *virtus* to describe their power.</note> constitute the inherent virtue of the Vital Force. In Man, this is expressed as Benevolence, Rectitude, Propriety, and Wisdom. The Mandate has these four parts—Origin, Prosperity, Utility, and Potential—and the Vital Force has its four corresponding parts. The Mandate is located right in the midst of the Vital Force, and these two can never be separated from each other.

20. Being asked as to the relation between the Mandate and "Number," he replied that where this Mandate exists, the Vital Force exists; and when the Vital Force exists, Number exists. For calculation <note>Number</note> is simply the way we assign limits and boundaries to things.

# CHINESE TEXT

This body is merely a physical shell. Inside and out, it is nothing but the **Vital Force** <term>Qi: the material energy or "breath" of the universe</term> of the universe’s *Yin* and *Yang*. It is like a fish in water: the water outside is the same as the water inside its belly. The water inside the belly of a grass carp is exactly the same as the water inside a common carp.

When the Vital Force gathers, it forms a physical shape. When the **Principle** <term>Li: the underlying cosmic pattern or reason</term> combines with this Vital Force, consciousness arises. This is like a fire fueled by oil; only then does it produce a great deal of light. That which is perceived is the Principle of the mind, while that which is capable of perceiving is the spiritual essence of the Vital Force.

The reason a human being is a human being is that their Principle is the Principle of the universe, and their Vital Force is the Vital Force of the universe. Principle leaves no physical trace and is invisible; therefore, we must observe it through the Vital Force. <gloss>The above section consists of twenty-three entries from the *Classified Conversations* <note>original: *Yulei* (語類), referring to the *Zhuzi Yulei*, a collection of sayings by the philosopher Zhu Xi</note></gloss>

Between Heaven and Earth, there exist both Principle and the Vital Force. Principle is the "Way" that exists **above the world of physical forms** <note>original: *Xing’er shang* (形而上), meaning metaphysical or abstract</note>; it is the root from which all living things arise. The Vital Force is the "vessel" that exists **within the world of physical forms** <note>original: *Xing’er xia* (形而下), meaning concrete or material</note>; it is the instrument by which things are produced. Therefore, when humans and things are born, they must receive this Principle before they can possess a Nature, and they must receive this Vital Force before they can possess a physical form. <gloss>From a reply to Huang Daofu</gloss>

What we call Principle and the Vital Force are definitely two distinct things. However, when viewed from the perspective of created things, the two are blended together and cannot be separated or placed in different locations. Yet, this does not prevent them from each being a distinct entity. If we look from the perspective of Principle, then even before any physical things existed, there already existed...

# FATE AND AIR

21. My body is just a shell; within and without there is nothing but the **Vital Force** <term>Qi: the life-energy or "Air" of the universe</term> of Heaven and Earth, divided into its light and dark aspects <note>original: "Light and Dark Air," referring to the duality of *Yin* and *Yang*</note>. This resembles a fish in the water; the water outside is the same as that inside its stomach. The water inside a whale is the same as the water inside a carp.

22. Accumulated Vital Force produces form; **Universal Principle** <term>Li: often translated here as "Fate," meaning the inherent reason or laws of the cosmos</term> unites with it, and then the being possesses the powers of Understanding and Sensation. This is just as when oil is poured upon fire; only then is there a great flame. That which causes Sensation to exist is the Principle inherent in the Mind, and that which possesses the power of Sensation is the spiritual part of the Vital Force <gloss>here, "Air" is used to denote the Mind's energetic substance</gloss>.

23. That which makes a human being a human being is that their Principle is the Principle inherent in Heaven and Earth, and their Vital Force is the Vital Force of Heaven and Earth. Principle has no traces and is invisible; therefore, we must look at the Vital Force in order to discern its presence.

24. In the midst of the universe, there is nothing but Principle and the Vital Force. Principle is **Incorporeal Reason** <note>original: *Xing’er shang* (形而上), the metaphysical realm "above form"</note>, the Origin of Life; the Vital Force is the **Corporeal Vessel** <note>original: *Xing’er xia* (形而下), the physical realm "below form"</note>, the Receptacle of Life. Hence both humans and things, at their generation, must receive this Principle—and then they have a "Nature"; they must receive this Vital Force—and then they have "Form."

25. With regard to Principle and the Vital Force, these are certainly two different things; yet in looking at any thing, the two are blended together and cannot be so separated as to occupy different places. Nevertheless, this does not prevent them from being totally distinct things. Although the Principle of a thing exists before the thing itself has any existence, it is merely the Principle <note>original: "the Fate (*i. e.* the idea)"</note> which exists and nothing more; the...

# THE PRIORITY OF PRINCIPLE AND THE VITAL FORCE

The Principle of things. However, it is only that Principle alone; the actual thing does not yet truly exist. Generally, when examining these matters, one must distinguish between them clearly. One must also consider the beginning and the end together to avoid error. <gloss>Reply to Liu Shuwen</gloss>

Only after this Principle exists is there this **Vital Force** <term>Qi: the energetic "Air" or substance that makes up the physical world</term>. Once this Vital Force exists, then this Principle has a place to be anchored. From the vastness of Heaven and Earth to the minute scale of mole crickets and ants, all life is produced in this way. Why then worry that the creation of Heaven and Earth had no source of transmission? In short, the single word "Principle" cannot be discussed in terms of "being" or "non-being" <note>This refers to the metaphysical debate on whether Principle "exists" as a physical object does</note>. Before Heaven and Earth existed, it was already so. <gloss>Reply to Yang Zhiren</gloss>

**Question:** Between Heaven and Earth, there is Principle and there is the Vital Force. Principle is eternal and unchanging, while the Vital Force is not constantly fixed. The *Doctrine of the Mean* <note>One of the Four Books of Confucianism, focusing on harmony and the "Mandate of Heaven"</note> says: "He who possesses great virtue will surely obtain his title, his position, and his longevity." In Principle, it should certainly be so. However, **Confucius** <note>The Great Sage who, despite his wisdom, never attained high political power</note> held no high position, and **Yan Hui** <note>Confucius’s favorite disciple, known for his virtue</note> died young. There are scholars living in humble dwellings who indeed grow old and die without their names ever being known. Is this not caused by the Vital Force? 

Therefore, the **Superior Person** <term>Junzi: the idealized ethical man</term> speaks of the constant laws and not the exceptions. Yet I secretly wonder: if Principle comes first and the Vital Force follows, and yet Principle is currently unable to overcome the Vital Force, then the common saying about "blessing the good and bringing calamity to the wicked" <note>original: *fushan huoyin* (福善禍淫)</note> is often proven false. How then can this be considered the constant law of Heaven and Earth? It is thought that although the Vital Force varies, it also changes with the age. When the **Sage-Kings** **Yao**, **Shun**, and **Yu** ruled from above, the world was in a state of peace and order. Because harmony was summoned by harmony, the Vital Force was also pure and upright, and—

26. When this **Principle** <term>Li: the fundamental order or "Fate" of the universe</term> is established, then this **Vital Force** <term>Qi: the physical "Air," energy, or matter</term> follows. When this Vital Force is obtained, then Principle has a place to rest. The generation of great things such as Heaven and Earth, and of small things such as ants, follows this same pattern. Why should Heaven and Earth not have had these conferred upon them at their creation? As for Principle, we need not debate its existence, for it existed even before Heaven and Earth.

27. A question was asked as follows: "In the midst of Heaven and Earth, there is nothing but Principle and the Vital Force. Principle never changes, but the Vital Force is ever-changing. The *Doctrine of the Mean* <term>Chung Yung: one of the Four Books of Confucianism</term> says that 'Great merit must obtain reputation, distinguished rank, and long life.' According to Principle, this should certainly be true. Yet, **Confucius** <note>The most famous Chinese philosopher (551–479 BCE), who never achieved the high political office his wisdom deserved</note> held no high rank; **Yan Hui** <note>original: "Yen-tsze"; Confucius's most brilliant disciple, who lived in poverty and died at the age of 29</note> died young; and many poor scholars remain unknown until their deaths. In the golden age of the Sage-Emperors **Yao, Shun, and Yu** <note>Legendary rulers of ancient China held up as perfect examples of virtue and wisdom</note>, because Sages were on the throne, the empire was governed in peace, and peace bred more peace. Wherever there is this **Principle** <term>Li: the fundamental moral and natural order</term>, there is this **Vital Force** <term>Qi: the physical energy or matter that constitutes all things</term>. Within the Vital Force, there is nothing that is not dual <note>referring to the complementary forces of Yin and Yang</note>. Therefore, the **Book of Changes** <term>I Ching: an ancient foundational text of Chinese philosophy</term> says: "The **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji: the supreme, undifferentiated source of all existence</term> produced the two Modes." However, **Laozi** <note>The legendary founder of Taoism</note> claimed that the **Way** <term>Dao</term> first produced the One, and only then did the One produce the Two; thus, his observation of Principle was not precise. <gloss>Reply to Cheng Kejiu</gloss>

If we discuss the single origin of all things, then Principle is the same while the Vital Force differs. But if we observe the differences in the physical forms of all things, then their Vital Force appears somewhat similar, while their Principle is absolutely different. The difference in the Vital Force lies in the unevenness of its purity or coarseness. The difference in Principle lies in its <insert>complete</insert> difference of partiality or wholeness. If you examine this more closely, you will naturally find no cause for doubt. <gloss>Reply to Huang Shangbo</gloss> <gloss>Complete</gloss>

Wherever the Vital Force gathers, Principle is present within it. Yet, Principle always remains the master. This is what is meant by the **Mysterious Union** <term>Miaohe: the subtle and perfect integration of the physical and the metaphysical</term>. <gloss>Reply to Wang Zihe</gloss>

Regarding your doubt about the partiality of Principle and the Vital Force: if we speak of the original source, then there is Principle, and then there is the Vital Force. Thus, Principle cannot be described in terms of being "partial" or "complete." However, if we speak of the **physical endowment** <term>Bingfu: the specific genetic and energetic makeup an individual receives at birth</term>, then there is this Vital Force first, and Principle subsequently follows to be fully present within it. Therefore, if there is this Vital Force, there is this Principle. If there is no such Principle, then...

27. During the times of Confucius and Mencius, there were executions and oppressions; the **Vital Force** <term>Qi</term> followed the times and shifted so that **Principle** <term>Li</term> could not subdue it. Do all these things refer to human affairs? He replied that he has already treated of this in a previous section on Nature. Although what is received is not the same in all things, yet the **Primal Force** <term>Yuanqi: the original energy of the universe</term> was corrupted in the cases instanced.

28. Where Principle exists, the Vital Force exists. The Vital Force is always of two types <note>referring to Yin and Yang</note>, and hence the **Book of Changes** <term>I Ching</term> states that "The **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji</term> generated the **Two Modes** <note>original: "Two E," referring to the Liangyi (Yin and Yang)</note>." **Laozi** <note>The founder of Taoism</note> also says that the **Way** <term>Dao</term> first generated the One, and afterwards the One generated the Two; thus, his investigation of Principle is not precise.

29. With regard to the **Single Origin** of all things, Principle is uniform, but the Vital Force varies. With regard to the varied bodies of the myriad things, there is a degree of similarity in the Vital Force of which they are formed, but their Principle is very diverse. The distinction in the Vital Force is that of pure and mixed; and the probable distinction in Principle is whether it is received partially or completely. We have, happily, further investigated this point; we should not remain in doubt about it.

30. Wherever the Vital Force accumulates, Principle is also inherent within it; but Principle is undoubtedly the Master. This is what is meant by his "adorning the One Whole"—that is, **the Universe** <note>original: *το παν* (Greek: *to pan*), meaning "the all" or the totality of existence</note>.

31. But if we treat of the bestowing of these two—Principle and the Vital Force—upon all things with a view to their existence, then...

# PRINCIPLE AND THE VITAL FORCE

That is not how **Principle** <term>Li: the fundamental order or pattern of the universe</term> works. If there is a great deal of **Vital Force** <term>Qi: the physical substance or energy of which all things are made</term>, then there is a great deal of Principle; if there is little Vital Force, then there is little Principle. How then can one not discuss this in terms of being "partial" or "complete"? <gloss>Reply to Zhao Zhidao</gloss>

Principle certainly cannot be described in terms of being partial or upright, or being open or blocked. However, since the **endowment of Vital Force** <term>Qibin: the specific physical and spiritual makeup a person is born with</term> is already diverse, then those whose Vital Force is "partial" only obtain the partiality of Principle; those whose Vital Force is "blocked" are naturally separated from Principle. Thus, when Principle exists within human beings, it too cannot be without partiality or blockage. **Hengqu** <note>The courtesy name of Zhang Zai (1020–1077), a foundational philosopher who used the metaphor of a mirror or light to explain how our physical nature affects our moral clarity</note> discussed how the reception of light varies in size, dimness, or clarity, yet the light's ability to illuminate and the object's ability to receive it are not two different things. His explanation is quite complete. <gloss>Reply to Du Renzhong</gloss>

Your argument—that the endowment of Vital Force is partial while the unified body of Principle has never differed—is correct. **Mingdao** <note>The courtesy name of Cheng Hao (1032–1085); he argued that even though water may be muddy, it does not stop being water, just as a person's "clouded" nature does not destroy their inherent goodness</note> also stated that one cannot claim turbid water is not water; this carries the same meaning. <gloss>Reply to Du Renzhong</gloss>

For Principle is pure and perfectly good, while Vital Force is mixed and uneven. To internalize the "gentleman" <note>The morally superior person</note> and externalize the "lesser man"—and all such efforts to suppress the **Yin** <note>The dark, passive, or negative force</note> and bolster the **Yang** <note>The bright, active, or positive force</note>—is to follow Principle in order to refine and assist, thereby compensating for the deficiencies in how the Vital Force is distributed. What harm is there in this? <gloss>Reply to a certain person</gloss>

**Nature** <note>Original: *Xing*. In this tradition, human nature is viewed as the Principle present within a person</note> is simply Principle. It cannot be spoken of in terms of gathering together or dispersing. What are called the **spirit and soul** <term>jingshen hunpo: the various facets of the human psyche and life-force</term>, which possess knowledge and awareness, are entirely the products of the Vital Force.

# PRINCIPLE AND THE VITAL FORCE

Once **Vital Force** <term>Qi: the physical substance and energy of which all things are made</term> is established, **Principle** <term>Li: the fundamental order or "Fate" of the universe</term> follows and is inherent within it. Therefore, where Vital Force exists, Principle exists. Where Vital Force is absent, Principle is absent. When Vital Force is abundant, Principle appears abundant; when Vital Force is deficient, Principle appears deficient. Can we not then describe them in terms of being "complete" or "incomplete"?

32. Intrinsically, we cannot describe **Principle** itself as having perfection or imperfection, nor can we say it is free or obstructed. However, because the **endowment of Vital Force** <term>Qibin: the specific physical and spiritual makeup a person is born with</term> varies among things, those with imperfect Vital Force receive Principle imperfectly. When the Vital Force is "obstructed" (thick or clouded), a separation from Principle occurs. When this Principle dwells within Man, it cannot avoid being affected by these physical incompleteness and obstructions. **Hengqu** <note>The courtesy name of Zhang Zai (1020–1077). He famously used the "Clear Void" to describe the state of the universe</note> says that in the reception of light, there are differences of scale, dullness, or brilliance, and yet we are not receiving two different kinds of light. This illustration is perfect.

33. This explains the idea that while the Vital Force we receive is diverse, the entire body of Principle remains the same. **Mingdao** <note>The courtesy name of Cheng Hao (1032–1085), who often emphasized the unity of all things</note> also says that which cannot be made muddy is not water; this expresses the exact same idea. <note>original: "That which is not muddied is not water" refers to how Principle is like the purity of water, while Vital Force is the sediment that can cloud it, though they are inseparable in the stream of existence.</note>

34. **Principle** is perfectly pure and superlatively good, but the **Vital Force** is mixed and variable. Within every person is the **Exemplary Person** <term>Junzi: the morally superior man</term>, while without is the **Petty Person** <term>Xiaoren: the man driven by base instincts</term>. All who suppress the **Darkness** <note>Yin: the passive, cold, or dark principle</note> and uphold the **Light** <note>Yang: the active, bright, or warm principle</note> are obedient to Principle. They complete, support, and remedy the deficiencies in their physical endowment. What blemish can remain in such a person?

35. **Human Nature** is simply this Principle, and we cannot speak of it as something that accumulates or disperses. # THE PERSISTENCE OF PRINCIPLE

Therefore, when the <term>Vital Force: Qi, the material energy or "Air"</term> assembles, there is existence; when it disperses, there is non-existence. As for <term>Principle: Li, the fundamental moral order or "Fate"</term>, it does not depend on this assembly or dispersal for its existence or non-existence. However, wherever this Principle exists, this Vital Force must also exist. If the Vital Force assembles in a certain place, its Principle is likewise mandated to that place. <gloss>Reply to Liao Zihui</gloss> <note>Liao Zihui (Liao De-ming) was a prominent disciple of the philosopher Zhu Xi.</note>

# FATE AND THE VITAL FORCE

As the <term>Vital Force: Qi, the material energy or "Air"</term> accumulates, these life-powers come into being; when it scatters—as it does at death—then they no longer exist. With regard to <term>Principle: Li, the fundamental law or "Fate"</term>, we cannot say that it originally accumulates or disperses, nor that it begins or ceases to be. It is simply that where this Principle exists, the Vital Force must also exist; and wherever the Vital Force gathers, the Principle is also received there.

36. Principle possesses both movement and stillness; therefore, the Vital Force also possesses movement and stillness. If Principle were devoid of movement and stillness, from what source would the Vital Force derive its own movement and stillness? To illustrate this through what is clearly visible: <term>Humaneness: Ren, the active, creative impulse</term> constitutes movement, and <term>Righteousness: Yi, the firm, moral boundary</term> constitutes stillness. How could the Vital Force be independent of these moral qualities? <note>original: "what has the Air to do with these?" — This rhetorical question implies that the physical "Air" or Vital Force is the necessary medium through which these abstract virtues are expressed in the world.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the general relationship between Principle (Li) and Vital Force (Qi) to a specific inquiry into the "Supreme Ultimate" (Taiji). ## THE SUPREME ULTIMATE

The <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji, the source of all existence</term> is simply the word "<term>Principle: Li, the fundamental law</term>."

**Question:** Is it not true that before <term>Heaven and Earth: the physical universe</term> existed, there was a chaotic, completed "thing"? And is the "Supreme Ultimate" not the collective name for the Principle of all things in the universe?

**Answer:** The Supreme Ultimate is simply the Principle of all things in the universe. If we speak of Heaven and Earth, then the Supreme Ultimate is within Heaven and Earth. If we speak of the <term>Myriad Things: all existing entities</term>, then each of the myriad things possesses its own Supreme Ultimate. Before Heaven and Earth existed, this Principle certainly existed first. Movement producing <term>Yang: the active, bright force</term> is also simply this Principle; stillness producing <term>Yin: the receptive, dark force</term> is also simply this Principle.

The myriad things, the four seasons, and the <term>Five Elements: Wu Xing; the phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term> all come from that Supreme Ultimate. The Supreme Ultimate is just a single <term>Vital Force: Qi, the material energy</term>, which gradually divides into two forces. The part within that moves is Yang, and the part that is still is Yin. These then divide into five types of Vital Force and scatter to become the myriad things.

**Question regarding the "Explanation of the Supreme Ultimate":** Why is movement placed before stillness? Why is <term>Function: Yong, the external application</term> placed before <term>Substance: Ti, the inner essence</term>? Why is <term>Stimulus: Gan, the outward response</term> placed before <term>Quiescence: Ji, the inner silence</term>? <note>The questioner is likely referring to the "Taiji Tu Shuo" (Explanation of the Diagram of the Supreme Ultimate), a foundational text by Zhou Dunyi which begins with the phrase "The Supreme Ultimate moves and produces Yang."</note>

**Answer:** In terms of Yin and Yang, function resides in the Yang, while substance resides in the Yin. However, movement and stillness have no beginning; Yin and Yang have no starting point. One cannot strictly divide them into "before" and "after." We only speak this way based on where we begin our description. Ultimately, before movement there is always stillness; before function there is always substance; before stimulus there is always quiescence; and before Yang there is always...

## PART II.

### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE.

1. The <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji, the source of all existence</term> is exactly the same as <term>Principle: Li, the fundamental law or order of the universe</term>. <note>The original translator used the word "Fate" here to represent the Chinese concept of Li.</note>

2. When asked whether the Supreme Ultimate is the collective name for the Principle inherent in <term>Heaven and Earth: the physical universe</term> and the <term>Myriad Things: all existing entities</term>—rather than a chaotic physical mass existing before the world began—he replied: it is simply the Principle inherent in Heaven, Earth, and all things. Regarding its presence in the cosmos, the Supreme Ultimate is at the center of Heaven and Earth; regarding its presence in all things, the Supreme Ultimate is at the heart of each individual thing. Before Heaven and Earth existed, this Principle must have first existed. That which moved and produced <term>Yang: the active, bright force</term> is simply this Principle; and that which rested and produced <term>Yin: the receptive, dark force</term> is also simply this Principle.

3. All things, the Four Seasons, and the <term>Five Elements: Wu Xing; the cosmic phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term> emerge from the center of the Supreme Ultimate. The Supreme Ultimate is a single <term>Vital Force: Qi, the material energy of the universe</term> which divided into two forces; the part that moves is Yang, and the part that rests <note>original: "vis inertia"; Latin for the force of inactivity</note> is Yin. It also divided and became five types of Vital Force; these scattered and became all things.

4. When asked: In your explanation of the Supreme Ultimate, why do you say that first there was motion and then rest; first activity and then inertia; first the exertion of influence and then its cessation? He replied: regarding Yang and Yin, activity belongs to Yang and inertia to Yin. However, motion and rest have no beginning; the...

...Yin. Yet, before silence there is a response; before stillness, there is already motion. Which should we consider to come first and which second? We cannot simply say that today’s motion is the beginning while ignoring yesterday’s stillness. Consider the breath: when we speak of <term>Respiration: huxi, the cycle of breathing</term>, the words flow naturally in that order <note>original: "呼吸则辞顺"; literally "exhale-inhale then the words are smooth"</note>. We do not say "inhale-exhale." Nevertheless, before an exhale there must have been an inhale, and before that inhale, there was an exhale.

The <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji</term> is not a separate, distinct object. It exists within <term>Yin and Yang: the fundamental dualities</term>; it exists within the <term>Five Elements: the cosmic phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term>; and it exists within the <term>Myriad Things: all of creation</term>. It is simply a single <term>Principle: Li, the underlying law of the universe</term>. Because it is the absolute peak of all things, it is named the "Supreme Ultimate."

If the Supreme Ultimate did not exist, Heaven and Earth would not have been brought forth.

<insert>Introduction</insert>
The Supreme Ultimate is Principle. Motion and stillness are the <term>Vital Force: Qi, the material energy of the universe</term>. When the Vital Force moves, the Principle also moves. These two are always dependent upon one another and have never been separated. In the very beginning, there was not a single thing—there was only this Principle. Because this Principle existed, it could move and produce <term>Yang: the active force</term>, and it could rest and produce <term>Yin: the receptive force</term>. When stillness reaches its limit, it returns to motion; when motion reaches its limit, it returns to stillness. They circulate in a perpetual loop. In truth, Principle is infinite, and the Vital Force is infinite along with it. Ever since Heaven and Earth began, it is this very process that has been circulating here. A day has its cycle, a month has its cycle, and a year has its cycle. It is simply this one process rolling ever forward.

# THE SUPREME ULTIMATE <note>The source uses "The Great Extreme," an older translation for the *Taiji*.</note>

<term>Yang: the active force, associated with light</term> and <term>Yin: the receptive force, associated with darkness</term> have no beginning; we cannot say which comes first and which comes second. Now, if we speak only of the point of beginning, then certainly stillness comes before activity; inertia comes before action; non-influence comes before influence; and Yin comes before Yang. However, before non-influence there is influence, and before stillness there is activity. Which of these, then, should we consider to be prior to the other? We cannot say today that activity is first while ignoring yesterday's stillness. For example, in speaking correctly of <term>Respiration: huxi, the cycle of breathing</term>, we must say "exhalation and inhalation," not "inhalation and exhalation." Yet, assuredly, before an exhalation there must be an inhalation, and before an inhalation there must be an exhalation.

It is <term>Principle: Li, the underlying law of the universe</term> <note>original: "Fate"</note> and nothing else. Because it is the absolute peak and final point of all things, it is designated the Supreme Ultimate.

6. If the Supreme Ultimate did not exist, would Heaven and Earth not lose their order and turn upside down?

In the beginning, before a single physical thing existed, there was only this Principle. When this Principle existed, it moved and generated Yang; it rested and generated Yin. Having reached the utmost limit of stillness, it moved again; having reached the utmost limit of activity, it rested again, revolving thus in a perpetual circle. This Principle being really...
