# CHINESE TEXT.

Before the **Supreme Ultimate** <note>The *Taiji*, representing the ultimate source of all existence.</note> begins to move, it is characterized by **Yin**. Within the stillness of Yin, the root of **Yang** naturally exists. Within the activity of Yang, the root of Yin is likewise present. The reason that activity must inevitably lead to stillness is that it is rooted in Yin; the reason that stillness must inevitably lead to activity is that it is rooted in Yang.

The fact that the Supreme Ultimate possesses both activity and stillness is the "flowing" of the <term>Mandate of Heaven: Tianming, the natural order and cosmic will</term>. Some might wonder: how can there be a "flow" during a state of stillness? The answer is: it is precisely through the alternation of movement and stillness that the flow occurs. Consider the seasons of autumn and winter—could we say that the natural flow of the world has stopped during these quiet times? If we claimed the flow could not exist in stillness, how could we say that "stillness generates Yin"? The meaning is revealed in that single word: "generate" <note>original: *sheng*, meaning to give birth to, produce, or grow</note>.

From the Supreme Ultimate down to the transformation and birth of the <term>Myriad Things: wanwu, all creatures and objects in the universe</term>, there is only one <term>Principle: Daoli, the fundamental truth or logic of the way things are</term>. This encompasses everything; it is not a case where one thing exists first and then another follows. Rather, it is all unified as one Great Origin, moving from <term>Substance: Ti, the internal essence</term> to <term>Function: Yong, the external expression</term>, and from the subtle to the manifest.

A question is asked regarding the reality of this single Principle: If the myriad things each share in it to form their own substance—meaning each individual thing possesses its own Supreme Ultimate—does this mean the Supreme Ultimate is split into pieces? The answer is: Originally, there is only one Supreme Ultimate. However, as each of the myriad things has its own <term>Endowment: bingshou, the specific nature and vital force received at its beginning</term>, each thing also fully possesses the Supreme Ultimate within itself. It is like the moon in the sky: there is only one moon. Yet, when its light is scattered across the rivers and lakes, it is seen everywhere. One cannot say that the moon itself has been divided <note>This refers to the famous "Moon Reflected in Ten Thousand Rivers" metaphor, illustrating how one universal truth can be present in many individual forms without being diminished.</note>.

**Supreme Ultimate** (*Taiji*), **Yin and Yang**, **Mandate of Heaven** (*Tianming*), **Flow/Circulation** (*Liuxing*), **Principle/Truth** (*Daoli*), **Substance and Function** (*Ti-Yong*), **Principle** (*Li*), **Endowment** (*Bingshou*), **The Myriad Things** (*Wanwu*), **The Moon reflected in ten thousand rivers** (*Yue yin wan chuan*).

# THE SUPREME ULTIMATE

7. Since the <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji, the ultimate source of all existence</term> is eternal, the <term>Vital Breath: Qi, the energy and matter that forms the universe</term> is also eternally united with it. From the moment that the universe existed, there was this constant cycle revolving. A day has its own revolution; a month has its own revolution; and a year has its own revolution. This is simply the constant turning of this cosmic principle.

8. Before the Supreme Ultimate moved, there was only <term>Stillness: Yin</term>. In the midst of that stillness and rest, the root of <term>Activity: Yang</term> spontaneously exists. Likewise, in the midst of activity and motion, the root of stillness is also present. The reason why rest necessarily follows motion is that its root is in the stillness; the reason why motion must succeed rest is that its root is in the activity. <note>The author is describing the "interdependence" of Yin and Yang; each state contains the seed of its opposite, ensuring a never-ending cycle.</note>

9. That the Supreme Ultimate should possess both motion and rest is the "flowing forth" of the <term>Mandate of Heaven: Tianming, the cosmic will or natural law</term>. Someone questioned how "rest" could be described as "flowing." The philosopher replied that the very alternation between motion and rest is what is meant by "flowing." For example, can we not say that autumn and winter—seasons of withdrawal—are part of the natural flow of time? If not, how could it be said that the Supreme Ultimate "rested and generated <term>Yin: the dark, receptive force</term>"? The use of the word "generated" proves this point.

10. From the Supreme Ultimate down to the transformation and production of the <term>Myriad Things: wanwu, all individual creatures and objects</term>, a single pattern encompasses the whole. It is not that one thing exists first and another follows later in importance; rather, they together constitute one Great Root. This process moves from *internal essence* to *external activity* <note>original: "inertia" and "activity," referring to Ti (Substance) and Yong (Function)</note>, and from *subtle beginnings* to *clear visibility*.

11. A question was asked regarding the reality of the one Mandate, and how it is divided among the myriad things to pervade them. If each of the myriad things has a Supreme Ultimate within it, does this mean the Supreme Ultimate is divided into separate portions? He replied that, as the Root, there is only one Supreme Ultimate, yet each of the myriad things has received it in its entirety, each...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of the Supreme Ultimate (Taiji), moving from its role in cosmic cycles to its metaphysical definitions. It specifically addresses the relationship between the Supreme Ultimate and the concept of the "Non-Ultimate" (Wuji) as taught by the Neo-Confucian master Zhou Dunyi.</meta>

# THE SUPREME ULTIMATE

When the <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji, the ultimate source and principle of the universe</term> divides, it is simply the two forces of **Yin and Yang**. These encompass every thing and every affair under Heaven.

The reason why the "Supreme Ultimate" received its name is that it takes its meaning from a "pivot" or a "pole" <note>The Chinese word 'ji' (極) refers to the highest point or the axis of a spinning wheel.</note>. The Sages called it the Supreme Ultimate to designate the root of Heaven, Earth, and the ten thousand things. <term>Master Zhou: Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), a central figure in Neo-Confucianism</term> followed this and also called it the <term>Non-Ultimate: Wuji, signifying the formless, infinite state before or within existence</term> in order to magnify the mystery of that which is "without sound and without scent" <note>A reference to the *Classic of Odes* and the *Doctrine of the Mean*, describing the subtle, imperceptible nature of the divine or cosmic law.</note>.

The Supreme Ultimate has no physical location, no form, and no specific place where it can be set down. If we speak of the state before it is <term>Unmanifested: Weifa, the state of stillness before thoughts or actions arise</term>, that state is simply stillness. Activity and stillness, or Yin and Yang, are all categorized as being <term>Below the Level of Form: Xing’erxia, referring to the physical, concrete world of objects</term>. However, activity is the activity of the Supreme Ultimate, and stillness is the stillness of the Supreme Ultimate; yet activity and stillness themselves are not the Supreme Ultimate. 

Therefore, Master Zhou spoke of it only as the Non-Ultimate. While the unmanifested state cannot strictly be called the Supreme Ultimate, it contains within it the seeds of joy, anger, sorrow, and pleasure. Joy and pleasure belong to **Yang**; anger and sorrow belong to **Yin**. Initially, these four emotions have not yet appeared, yet their <term>Principle: Li, the underlying pattern or logic</term> is already fully present. If contrasted with the <term>Manifested: Yifa, the state after emotions or actions have been expressed</term>, perhaps one could call it the Supreme Ultimate, but this remains difficult to express. These descriptions are only approximations; one must experience and realize this truth for oneself.

Activity is not the Supreme Ultimate; rather, activity is the <term>Function: Yong, the external application or expression</term> of the Supreme Ultimate. Stillness is not the Supreme Ultimate; rather, stillness is the <term>Substance: Ti, the internal essence or foundation</term> of the Supreme Ultimate.

***

**Key Vocabulary:**
*   **Supreme Ultimate** (*Taiji*): The ultimate principle of the universe.
*   **Yin and Yang**: The dual forces of shade/stillness and light/activity.
*   **Non-Ultimate** (*Wuji*): The formless aspect of the ultimate principle.
*   **Master Zhou** (*Zhouzi*): The philosopher Zhou Dunyi.
*   **Substance** (*Ti*): The internal reality or essence.
*   **Function** (*Yong*): The external manifestation or use.
*   **Below Form** (*Xing’erxia*): The physical, material realm.
*   **Unmanifested** (*Weifa*): The latent state before expression.
*   **Manifested** (*Yifa*): The active state after expression.

<summary>This page defines the Supreme Ultimate (Taiji) as the formless root of all existence, explaining that while it expresses itself through the activity and stillness of Yin and Yang, it remains a transcendent "Non-Ultimate" (Wuji) that is beyond physical form.</summary>

### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE. 31

...possessing within it an entire and complete <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji, the fundamental principle of the universe</term>. For example, there is only one moon in the sky, but when its light is scattered across the rivers and canals, it can be seen in every body of water. Yet, we cannot say that the moon itself has been divided.

12. When the Supreme Ultimate unfolds, it manifests as the two forces of <term>Yang: Light, activity, and the creative</term> and <term>Yin: Darkness, passivity, and the receptive</term>, which together encompass every thing and every event in the universe.

13. The Supreme Ultimate derives its name from the concept of a "highest pivot" <note>The original term 'ji' (極) refers to the highest point of a roof or a celestial pole—the axis around which everything turns.</note>. The ancient sages called it the Supreme Ultimate to identify it as the root and source of Heaven, Earth, and all things. Because of this, <term>Master Zhou: Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073)</term> also designated it as the <term>Non-Ultimate: Wuji, or the Infinite</term> to describe its subtle, immaterial nature—what the classics call being "without sound or scent" <note>This refers to something that exists beyond the reach of the physical senses.</note>.

14. The Supreme Ultimate is not restricted to any specific location; it has no physical form, and no single place can contain it <note>In other words, it is omnipresent, incorporeal, and infinite.</note>. If we consider it before it was manifested, that state was simply Stillness. Activity and Stillness, or Yang and Yin, are all part of the material world; however, Activity is the activity *of* the Supreme Ultimate, and Stillness is the stillness *of* the Supreme Ultimate. Yet, Activity and Stillness themselves are not the Supreme Ultimate; this is why Master Zhou called it the "Non-Ultimate." 

Before it was manifested, it could not strictly be called the Supreme Ultimate, and yet, within it were already contained the seeds of Pleasure, Anger, Grief, and Joy. Pleasure and Joy belong to the force of Yang, while Anger and Grief belong to the force of Yin. Before these four emotions were expressed, their <term>Principles: Li, the inherent "fate" or patterns of things</term> already existed there. If we speak of these things once they are expressed, then perhaps we may call them the Supreme Ultimate. However, this is a difficult point to explain; it is merely an attempt to illustrate a profound matter. You should investigate this subject with great care.

### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE. 32

Someone asked about the <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji</term>. The Master replied: The Supreme Ultimate is simply the principle of perfect goodness and ultimate excellence. Every person possesses a Supreme Ultimate, and every object possesses a Supreme Ultimate. What <term>Master Zhou: Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073)</term> called the Supreme Ultimate is a descriptive term for the myriad virtues and the supreme excellence found within Heaven, Earth, people, and all things.

As soon as one speaks of the Supreme Ultimate, the forces of <term>Yin and Yang: The passive and active cosmic forces</term> are already involved. As soon as one speaks of <term>Nature: Xing, the inner essence of a being</term>, then <term>Vital Energy: Qi, the material force of the universe</term> is already involved. Without Yin, Yang, and Vital Energy, where would the Supreme Ultimate and <insert>and</insert> Nature find a place to attach themselves? Yet, if we wish to understand this clearly, we must also be able to distinguish them analytically.

**Question:** The Master has said that because the Supreme Ultimate possesses this Nature, it therefore possesses Yin, Yang, and the <term>Five Elements: Wuxing; metal, wood, water, fire, and earth</term>. What is meant by "Nature" in this context?

**Answer:** I suspect that was an older explanation of mine. Upon more recent reflection, it does not seem quite right. This word "Nature" refers to that which is endowed by Heaven. If the Supreme Ultimate is strictly understood as <term>Principle: Li, the fundamental pattern of reality</term>, then it is naturally something that cannot be altered or moved. The <note>original: "Yi" (易)</note> *Book of Changes* says: "One Yin and one Yang: this is called the Way." That which continues this process is called Goodness. As for that which completes it, only then do we call it "Nature." This refers to what Heaven bestows upon people and things, and what people and things receive from Heaven.

I once remarked that the Supreme Ultimate is like something with its "head hidden." When it is in motion, it belongs to the <term>Yang: The active principle</term>; before it has moved, it belongs to the <term>Yin: The still principle</term>.

The Supreme Ultimate naturally contains the principles of both activity and stillness. However, one cannot separate <term>Substance and Function: Ti and Yong; the internal essence and its external expression</term> simply by labeling one as activity and the other as stillness. Rather, stillness is the *Substance* of the Supreme Ultimate, and activity is its *Function*. For example, a fan is just a fan <note>The physical object of the fan represents the "Substance."</note>. When it is being waved, that is its "Function." When it is laid down, it is...

### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE. 33

15. Motion is not the <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji</term> itself, but that which possesses motion is the active phase of the Supreme Ultimate. Rest is not the Supreme Ultimate, but that which possesses rest is the quiet phase of the Supreme Ultimate.

16. In response to an inquiry about the Supreme Ultimate, he replied: The Supreme Ultimate is simply the most excellent and perfectly good <term>Principle: Li, the fundamental pattern of reality</term>. Every person and every object possesses a Supreme Ultimate. What <term>Master Zhou: Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073)</term> means by the Supreme Ultimate is the supremely good and most excellent manifested *virtue* <note>original: "virtus," referring to the inherent power or moral excellence of a thing</note> of Heaven, Earth, Humanity, and all things.

17. When we speak of the "Supreme Ultimate," we must link it to <term>Yin and Yang: The passive and active cosmic forces, here referred to as Darkness and Light</term>; and when we speak of <term>Nature: Xing, the inner essence of a being</term>, we connect it with <term>Vital Energy: Qi, the material force or "air" of the universe</term>. If these principles were not connected to Yin, Yang, and Vital Energy, how could the Supreme Ultimate or Nature be supported? Yet, when we wish to distinguish them clearly, we must treat them as distinct concepts.

18. Being asked: "Sir, in speaking of the Supreme Ultimate, you said that having Nature, we then have Yin and Yang and the <term>Five Elements: Wuxing; wood, fire, earth, metal, and water</term>. What is this 'Nature'?" He replied: "I believe I said so on a former occasion, but I no longer think that is correct. The term 'Nature' refers to our receiving this essence from Heaven. Regarding the Supreme Ultimate, we should call it the <term>Mandate: Ming, often translated as 'Fate'</term>; we certainly must not change this designation." The <term>I Ching: The Classic of Changes</term> states: "The alternation of Yin and Yang is called the <term>Way: Dao</term>. That which continues this process is Goodness, and that which completes it is Nature." This is what Heaven bestows upon humans and things, and what humans and things receive from Heaven.

19. I have already said that the Supreme Ultimate is like one who hides his head <note>A metaphor for the Principle being the hidden source behind visible phenomena</note>: during motion, it belongs to the <term>Yang: The active, light principle</term>, and when there is no motion, it belongs to the <term>Yin: The passive, dark principle</term>.

### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE. 34

When the substance is at rest, it is simply this one Principle. When it is in motion, it is also just this one Principle.

Liang Wenshu <note>A student of the philosopher Zhu Xi</note> said: "The Supreme Ultimate is spoken of as combining both motion and rest." The Master replied: "It is not a matter of 'combining' motion and rest. Rather, the <term>Supreme Ultimate: Taiji</term> inherently *possesses* the capacity for both motion and rest. When the emotions of pleasure, anger, sorrow, and joy have not yet issued forth <note>This refers to the state of 'Wei-fa' or pre-activation mentioned in the 'Doctrine of the Mean'</note>, there is the Supreme Ultimate. When these emotions have already issued forth, there is also the Supreme Ultimate. It is but a single Supreme Ultimate, which flows through the moments of activity and remains concealed during the moments of stillness."

Someone asked about the Supreme Ultimate. The Master said: "Before the emotions are stirred, there is Principle. Once they are stirred, there is <term>Emotion: Qing</term>. Just as the movement of the universe produces <term>Yang: The active, light cosmic force</term>, this activation is the realm of Emotion."

The Supreme Ultimate is like the ridgepole of a house, or the pole of the heavens. When you reach this point, there is no further <gloss>way</gloss> to go. It is the absolute highest point of <term>Principle: Li, the cosmic pattern</term>. The motion of Yang and the stillness of <term>Yin: The passive, dark cosmic force</term> are not the Supreme Ultimate’s own motion and rest; rather, it is that Principle itself contains the capacity for motion and rest. Principle cannot be seen with the eyes; it is known only through the manifestations of Yin and Yang. Principle is mounted upon Yin and Yang, much like a rider straddling a horse. As soon as the <term>Five Elements: Wood, fire, earth, metal, and water</term> are produced, they are shaped and limited by the individual's <term>Qi-disposition: Qizhi, the physical and psychological temperament</term>. Thus, each thing becomes a distinct object and each possesses its own specific nature, yet the Supreme Ultimate is present in every one of them.

A question was asked: "Is the 'Center of Heaven and Earth' spoken of by Liu Zi <note>Likely referring to the philosopher Liu Shao or a similar contemporary scholar</note> the same as what Master Zhou <note>Zhou Dunyi</note> calls the Supreme Ultimate?" The Master replied: "They are generally the same, though the names differ. The 'Center' <note>original: 'Zhong'</note> simply refers to the 'just right' place. In the *Book of History*, where it says, 'The August Lord on High bestowed the inner sense of rightness <note>original: 'Zhong,' often translated as 'the Mean'</note> upon the people,' it also refers to this 'just right' place. However, the 'Ultimate' <note>the 'Ji' in Taiji, meaning the highest point or limit</note> is not exactly the same as the 'Center'."

<summary>This page clarifies that the Supreme Ultimate (Taiji) is the singular Principle underlying both stillness and activity, using the metaphor of a rider and horse to explain how abstract Principle governs material force. It distinguishes between the "Ultimate" as a cosmic limit and the "Center" as a state of perfect balance in human nature.</summary>

# THE SUPREME ULTIMATE. 35

20. The Supreme Ultimate <term>Taiji: The Great Extreme</term> is in reality the Principle <term>Li: The cosmic pattern, referred to here as 'Fate'</term> which contains both Movement and Stillness. We cannot take the Movement and Stillness of the Supreme Ultimate and separate them from its active and inert portions; for Stillness is the inactivity of the Supreme Ultimate, and Movement is its activity. For example, a fan is but one fan; to fan oneself is the fan’s activity, to lay it down is the fan’s inactivity. But, whether it is laid down or used for fanning, it remains the same fan.

21. Liang Wenshu <note>A student of the philosopher Zhu Xi, transcribed in the original as Leang Wăn-sŭh</note> said: "The Supreme Ultimate is connected with Movement and Stillness." The Master replied: "It is not merely 'connected' with Movement and Stillness. The Supreme Ultimate *possesses* Movement and Stillness. Before the emotions of Pleasure, Anger, Grief, and Joy were manifested, the Supreme Ultimate existed; and when these were manifested, the Supreme Ultimate was still in existence. This is simply the one Supreme Ultimate flowing forth into manifestation, or remaining hidden before it is expressed."

22. Someone asking about the Supreme Ultimate, he replied: "Before it is manifested, it is Principle <note>original: 'Fate'</note>; when manifested, it is our natural disposition. For example, the statement 'Movement generated the Yang' <note>original: 'the Light'</note> shows the expression of this natural disposition."

23. The Supreme Ultimate resembles the ridgepole <note>The highest horizontal beam of a roof</note> of a house, and the highest point of Heaven. Arrived at this point, there is no place beyond; it is the absolute ultimate point of Principle. <term>Yang: The active, light cosmic force</term> moves and <term>Yin: The passive, dark cosmic force</term> rests, but the Supreme Ultimate itself does not move or rest; it is merely that this Principle possesses the capacity for Movement and Stillness. Principle is invisible; its existence is known only through the existence of Yang and Yin. Principle rides upon Yang and Yin, just as a man rides upon a horse. When the <term>Five Elements: Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term> are generated, Principle then becomes inherent in their...

# THE SUPREME ULTIMATE. 36

24. The nature of the "Extreme" <term>Ji: the pivot or highest point</term> is simply to be in the center. Consider this candlestick: the central point where the pin is fixed is the "Extreme." When measured from here to there, it is perfectly balanced, without any addition; when measured from there to here, it is perfectly balanced, without any subtraction.

25. The Supreme Ultimate is a thing of immense scale. The four cardinal directions combined with the vertical "up and down" are called *Yu* <term>Space</term>. The progression from the ancient past to the present day is called *Zhou* <term>Time</term>. No physical object is comparable to this. Space is vast: the four directions go on without limit, and the vertical dimensions go on without limit—how great a scale is that? No object has the endurance of Time, which spans from the beginning of antiquity to the present, coming and going without end. Within your own heart, you must constantly recognize this meaning.

26. Yu <note>The student Pan Shijia (courtesy name Ziyu), who is recording this dialogue</note> asked: "Whose words are these?" The Master replied: "These are the words of the ancients. Xiangshan <note>Lu Jiuyuan (1139–1193), a rival philosopher to Zhu Xi who emphasized the 'School of the Mind'</note> often spoke this way. However, for him, the concept was merely *this*—he did not account for the many structured stages <note>original: 'beats' or 'cadences,' referring to the logical intervals of cosmic development</note> within it. Instead, he simply clung to a state of vast emptiness. You should look further into the 'Western Inscription' by Hengqu <note>Zhang Zai (1020–1077). His 'Western Inscription' is a foundational Neo-Confucian text on the unity of the cosmos</note>. At first glance, it seems to have many 'beats' and might appear narrow, but once you fulfill its capacity, you realize how truly vast it is!"

27. If we speak of it in terms of Principle <term>Li</term>, then it cannot be said to "exist" <note>in a tangible, physical form</note>. If we speak of it in terms of things <note>as the source and pattern of all matter</note>, then it cannot be said to be "non-existent."

28. Someone asked: "Kangjie <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a cosmologist famous for his work on the Book of Changes</note> said that the Way <term>Dao</term> is the Supreme Ultimate, and he also said that the Mind is the Supreme Ultimate. Does 'the Way' refer to the natural Principle of Heaven, Earth, and all things, while 'the Mind' refers to a human being attaining this Principle to serve as the master of their own body?" The Master replied: "That is certainly correct. But the Supreme Ultimate is just a single..."

CHINESE TEXT.

...and has no opposite.

The Supreme Ultimate is the Principle that resides within the Five Elements <note>Water, Fire, Wood, Metal, and Earth—the fundamental phases of nature.</note> and the dual forces of Yin and Yang; it possesses them all. It is not an empty or void thing. If it were empty, it would be similar to how the Buddhists <note>original: "Shishi," referring to the followers of Shakyamuni Buddha.</note> speak of "nature" <note>Referring to the Buddhist concept of Sunyata or Emptiness, which Neo-Confucians critiqued as being too detached from physical reality.</note>.

The "Ultimate" is the highest point of Principle. When one sums up the principles of Heaven, Earth, and the myriad things of the world, that is the Supreme Ultimate.

The Supreme Ultimate is simply the absolute peak; there is nowhere further to go. It is the height of the lofty, the subtle, the refined, and the divine. There is no other place beyond it. Master Lianxi <note>The philosopher Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073).</note> feared that people would claim the Supreme Ultimate had a physical form, so he said, "The Non-Ultimate, and yet the Supreme Ultimate!" This means that within the "nothingness" of form, there exists the most ultimate Principle.

**Question:** The Supreme Ultimate is Principle. How can Principle have movement and stillness? Things with physical form have movement and stillness, but since the Supreme Ultimate is formless, I fear one cannot speak of it in terms of movement and stillness.
**Answer:** Principle contains the potential for movement and stillness; therefore, Vital Force <term>Qi: the material energy or substance that constitutes all things</term> also has movement and stillness. If Principle did not have these qualities, from where would Vital Force derive its own movement and stillness?

When observed from the perspective of existing things, then Yin and Yang contain the Supreme Ultimate within them. But when we trace back to the ultimate origin, the Supreme Ultimate generates Yin and Yang.

**Question:** Is the Supreme Ultimate simply the ultimate Principle within the human heart?
**Answer:** Every single object and every single affair possesses an "ultimate"—the highest point of its specific Principle <term>Li: the underlying law or pattern of the universe</term>. 
**Someone said:** For example, the benevolence of a ruler or the reverence of a minister—that is their "ultimate." 
**Answer:** Those are the ultimates of specific things and affairs. But when you total the principles of Heaven, Earth, and the myriad things...

<note>The text concludes mid-sentence, transitioning to the totalizing nature of the Supreme Ultimate across the cosmos.</note>

### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE <note>original: "THE GREAT EXTREME"</note>

(The Supreme Ultimate) is **Being** <note>original Latin: "Ens"</note>; while, speaking with regard to Matter, we must not say that it is **Non-being** <note>original Latin: "non-Ens"</note>.*

27. Some one asked about the statements made by Shao Yong <note>A philosopher of the Northern Song dynasty; original: "Kang-tsëĕ"</note>, that “Principle <note>original: "Reason"</note> is the Supreme Ultimate,” and “Mind is the Supreme Ultimate;” whether “Principle” here refers to the spontaneously existing Mandate <note>original: "Fate," referring to the cosmic order or *Tianming*</note> inherent in Heaven, Earth and all things: and whether “Mind” refers to Man, obtaining this Mandate to act as the Lord of the whole body? He replied, it is just so: but the Supreme Ultimate is just One—a unity without compare.

28. The Supreme Ultimate is the Mandate <note>original: "Fate"</note> which exists in the Five Elements <note>Water, Fire, Wood, Metal, and Earth</note>, in the Light and in the Darkness <note>The dual forces of Yin and Yang</note>. It is not a mere abstraction: for, if it were, it would then resemble what the Buddhists call "Nature" <note>Neo-Confucians often critiqued the Buddhist concept of "Nature" or "Emptiness" as being void of moral principle and reality.</note>.

29. The "Ultimate" means the very highest Principle. The Mandate inherent in Heaven, Earth and all things, as one whole, is just the Supreme Ultimate.

30. The Supreme Ultimate is just the very extreme point beyond which we cannot go; most High, most Beautiful, most Subtile, most Divine; surpassing every thing. Master Zhou Dunyi <note>The influential philosopher also known as Lianxi; original: "Lëen-khe"</note>, lest any one should imagine that the Supreme Ultimate has bodily form, designates it “The Non-Ultimate, and yet the Supreme Ultimate;” that is, this ultimate Mandate exists in the midst of the **Incorporeal** <note>original: "non-Ens"; here the translator uses the term to describe the realm of formlessness</note>.

31. Being asked: If the Supreme Ultimate is the Mandate, how can the Mandate move and rest? That which has bodily form has Motion and Rest; but the Supreme Ultimate is Incorporeal, and hence cannot, I suppose, be said to move and rest? He replied: The Mandate has the power of Motion and Rest, and therefore the Vital Force <term>Qi: the material energy or "air" that constitutes all things</term> has Motion and Rest; if the Mandate had not the power of Motion and Rest, then, how could the Vital Force spontaneously have Motion and Rest?

\* That is, it holds a middle place in the Cosmos.

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the "Supreme Ultimate" (Taiji), transitioning from its definition as the "Highest Principle" to a discussion of its manifestation in the natural cycles of growth, decay, and the relationship between Principle (Li) and Vital Energy (Qi). Note: There is a pencil check mark above the eleventh column from the right in the original manuscript.</meta>

### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE <note>original: "Taiji"</note>

The Principle <note>The underlying logic or cosmic law of the universe; original: "Li"</note> of all things is the Supreme Ultimate. The "Supreme Ultimate" was not originally its name; it is merely a descriptive title used to express its virtuous nature.

The Supreme Ultimate is like a single root. As it grows, it divides into branches and a trunk. It divides again to produce flowers and leaves, generating and regenerating without end. When it reaches the stage of bearing fruit, inside that fruit there is once more the principle of endless generation. As it grows outward again, it becomes an infinite number of Supreme Ultimates, never pausing. It is only when the fruit is fully formed that there is a brief period of rest. It is not that growth stops permanently at this point; rather, it is a natural pause. As the saying goes: "Nothing is more complete for the beginning and end of all things than the Keeping Still <note>original: "Gen," one of the eight trigrams of the I Ching (Book of Changes) representing the mountain and the concept of "Stopping" or "Resting"</note>." This "Resting" implies the meaning of catching one's breath to allow for new life.

It is simply this one thing. Therefore, when all things reach the seasons of Autumn and Winter, they each withdraw and hide away. Then, suddenly, when Spring arrives, they each break forth and grow with ease. This is all just one Vital Energy <note>The life-force or matter-energy of the universe; original: "Qi"</note>—one part waning <note>original: "Xiao"</note> and one part waxing <note>original: "Xi"</note>. It is just like a human being: when they are silent, that is Stillness; when they speak, that is Movement. The mountain full of blue, yellow, and emerald green is nothing other than this Supreme Ultimate manifest.

What we call the Supreme Ultimate cannot be discussed as if it were separate from the dual forces of Yin and Yang <note>The complementary forces of dark/light, passive/active</note>, nor can it be discussed as if it were confused or mixed with Yin and Yang.

**Question:** It is said that all things each possess a Supreme Ultimate. Is this spoken of in terms of Principle <note>original: "Li"</note> or in terms of Vital Energy <note>original: "Qi"</note>?
**Answer:** It is spoken of in terms of Principle.

<vocab>Supreme Ultimate, Yin and Yang, Principle, Vital Energy, Keeping Still, Branches and Trunk, Waning and Waxing</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a fruiting tree to explain how the Supreme Ultimate (Taiji) acts as an infinite, self-replicating principle within all nature. It clarifies that while Taiji is inseparable from the physical world (Qi), it is defined by its underlying cosmic Law (Li).</summary>
<keywords>Taiji, Li and Qi, Neo-Confucianism, I Ching, Gen Trigram, cycles of nature, Yin and Yang, cosmology</keywords>

->### THE SUPREME ULTIMATE <note>original: "The Great Extreme"</note><-

32. Regarding how it manifests in every object and every event, the forces of Yang and Yin <note>original: "Light and Darkness"</note> surround the Supreme Ultimate. However, regarding their source, the Supreme Ultimate is what generated Yang and Yin.

33. When asked if the Supreme Ultimate represents the ultimate Principle <note>original: "Fate"</note> of the human mind, he replied: Every situation and every object has an ultimate point—the source from which it arises. Someone remarked, "Just as, for example, the benevolence of an Emperor or the respect shown by a Minister are ultimate standards?" He replied: These are the ultimate standards for those specific roles and things. However, the Principle inherent in Heaven, Earth, and all things as a single whole is the Supreme Ultimate. The Supreme Ultimate was not its original name; the term is simply used to describe its manifested virtue <note>original Latin: "virtus"; referring to the inherent power or excellence of a thing</note>.

34. The Supreme Ultimate resembles a root that sprouts upward, dividing into branches, which then divide further to produce flowers and leaves, creating life without end. When the fruit is formed, it contains the seed of further infinite generation within itself, ready to sprout again. This is the infinite Supreme Ultimate, which never truly stops; it only pauses briefly when the fruit is fully ripened <note>Original footnote: "That is, when the universe is about to return to a state of chaos before a new cycle begins."</note>. It is not as if the universe reaches a certain point and then simply ends forever. Regarding the beginning and ending of the myriad of things, it is said that they are completed in the Resting Trigram <note>original: "Kăn Diagram"; this refers to "Gen" (艮), one of the eight trigrams of the I Ching, representing "Keeping Still" or "The Mountain"</note>. This "Resting" represents a cessation; specifically, a temporary pause in the process of generation.

35. It is entirely because of this Supreme Ultimate that all things in the world are gathered and stored away during Autumn and Winter. No sooner does Spring...

It clarifies that the end of a cycle, represented by the "Resting" trigram, is merely a temporary pause before life begins again in Spring.</summary>

# CHINESE TEXT.

"The Limitless and yet the Great Ultimate" <term>Wuji er Taiji; a foundational phrase in Neo-Confucianism describing the transition from nothingness to the source of all existence</term>. This simply means that there is no physical form, yet <term>Principle (Li)</term> is present. Master Zhou <note>Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), the philosopher who wrote the "Explanation of the Diagram of the Great Ultimate"</note> feared that people might look for another "Great Ultimate" outside of the one that exists, so he used the term "Limitless" to describe it. Since it is called the Limitless, one cannot use mundane logic to force a search for its physical boundaries.

**Question:** Does the Great Ultimate begin with the movement of Yang?
**Answer:** The stillness of Yin is the root of the Great Ultimate, yet the stillness of Yin is itself generated from the movement of Yang. One stillness and one movement are like a single "opening and closing" <term>Pihe; a metaphor for cosmic expansion and contraction</term>. Tracing back from the greatest instances of this opening and closing, the process is infinite; one cannot speak of a single, absolute beginning.

**Question:** Nanxuan <note>The literary name of Zhang Shi (1133–1180), a close friend and philosophical contemporary of Zhu Xi</note> says, "The substance of the Great Ultimate is utmost stillness." What do you think?
**Answer:** That is not correct.
**Question:** He also says, "This so-called 'utmost stillness' refers to something that runs through both the manifested and the unmanifested states." What do you think?
**Answer:** If it were like that, it would become a skewed and improper Great Ultimate. <gloss>The above consists of thirty-nine entries from the Classified Conversations</gloss> <note>original: "Zhuzi Yulei," the collected records of Zhu Xi's oral teachings</note>

Between Heaven and Earth, there are only the two states of movement and stillness, cycling without end; there is nothing else. This is what is called "Change" <note>referring to the system of the *I Ching* or *Book of Changes*</note>. And within that movement and stillness, there must be a "Principle of movement and stillness." This is what is called the Great Ultimate.

The so-called Great Ultimate exists only within Yin and Yang. The so-called Yin and Yang exist only within the Great Ultimate. People today say that "above" Yin and Yang there is a separate thing, invisible and shadowless, which is the Great Ultimate. This is incorrect.

<summary>This page explores the metaphysical relationship between the Great Ultimate (Taiji) and the Limitless (Wuji), arguing that the Ultimate is not a separate physical entity but a non-physical Principle inherent within the cycles of movement and stillness (Yin and Yang).</summary>

# THE GREAT ULTIMATE.

...return to the source, then each thing emerges and expands again. This is simply the one Vital Energy <term>Qi; the "Air" or material force that animates the universe</term> alternately ceasing and then springing forth once more. It is like a person: when silent, they are at Rest; when speaking, they are in Motion. The blue, yellow, white, and green colors that cover the hills all owe their existence to this **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji; the supreme source and guiding principle of all reality</term>.

36. What is designated as the **Great Ultimate** can neither be separated from, nor confused with, the forces of Yang and Yin <note>Original text: "Light and Darkness"</note>.

37. When asked whether the statement that "each thing has a Great Ultimate inherent in it" refers to Principle <term>Li; translated in the source as "Fate," referring to the inherent laws of nature</term> or to the Vital Energy, he replied: "It refers to Principle."

38. The phrase "*The Limitless and yet the Great Ultimate*" <note>Original text: "Infinite and Great Extreme"</note> simply means that Principle existed before physical forms. **Master Zhou** <note>Chow-tsze, or Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073)</note>, fearing that someone might seek a second Great Ultimate beyond the true one, added the term "Limitless." Since he calls it "The Limitless," we do not need to trouble ourselves by asking whether it has a physical form or not. When asked whether the beginning of the **Great Ultimate** starts with the movement of the Yang, he replied: "The stillness of the Yin is the root of the **Great Ultimate**, but the Rest of the Yin is itself generated from the Motion of the Yang." The alternation of Motion and Rest is just like the opening and shutting of a door. Looking upward from the "Great Opening and Shutting" <note>The cosmic cycle of expansion and contraction</note> of the universe, the process is without limit or end. We cannot claim a specific beginning for the root.

39. When asked regarding the statement by **Nanxuan** <note>Nan-hëen, the literary name of the philosopher Zhang Shi (1133–1180)</note> that the essence of the **Great Ultimate** is extreme Rest, he replied: "That is not correct." When asked further whether this so-called "extreme Rest" includes both manifestation and non-manifestation, he replied: "If it did, we would be creating a lopsided or distorted **Great Ultimate**" <gloss>meaning it would no longer be a perfect, all-encompassing circle</gloss>.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the Great Ultimate, shifting focus to the relationship between the human constitution (Nature and Heart-Mind) and the cosmic principles of the Book of Changes (I Ching). It explores the sequence of creation and the cyclical nature of time.</meta>

CHINESE TEXT.

Human **Nature** <term>Xing; the inherent moral constitution of a person</term> is like the **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji; the supreme source of all reality</term>. The **Heart-Mind** <term>Xin; the seat of both consciousness and emotion</term> is like the forces of **Yin and Yang**. The Great Ultimate exists only within the Yin and the Yang; it cannot be separated from them. However, when we speak specifically of the Great Ultimate, then the Great Ultimate is its own entity, and Yin and Yang are their own entities. This is also true of Human Nature and the Heart-Mind. They are what we call "two in one, and one in two." <gloss>The three entries above are taken from the minor annotations to the *Diagram of the Great Ultimate* in the *Nature and Principle* texts.</gloss>

This word "Way" <term>Dao; the fundamental path or principle of the universe</term> is the same as the Great Ultimate in the *Book of Changes* <note>The *I Ching*</note>. The number One represents the odd number of the **Yang**; Two represents the even number of the **Yin**; and Three is the sum of the odd and the even. When the text says that "Two produces Three," it is like saying that two and one together make three. If we simply considered "One" to be the Great Ultimate itself, we could not then speak of "The Way producing the One." <gloss>From a reply to Cheng Taizhi.</gloss>

Motion and Rest have no starting point, and Yin and Yang have no beginning. Essentially, one cannot speak of them in terms of "before" and "after." However, if we were to cut into the middle of the cycle to describe it, there is no harm in speaking of a sequence. Consider what **Master Zhou** <note>Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), a foundational figure of Neo-Confucianism</note> said: "The Great Ultimate moves and produces the Yang." It follows that before it moved, it must have already been in a state of Rest. He also said: "Rest, when it reaches its limit, returns to Motion." This implies that after a state of Rest, Motion must surely follow.

It is like the four seasons—Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter—or the four cosmic virtues: **Originality, Prosperity, Advantage, and Firmness** <note>The four stages of a cycle (Yuan, Heng, Li, Zhen) from the *I Ching*, representing birth, growth, harvest, and storage</note>. There is certainly a sequence here. Yet, without Winter, how could there be a Spring? And without Firmness <note>the closing of the cycle</note>, how could there be a new beginning in Originality?

<summary>This page uses analogies of the seasons and the I Ching to explain that while the Great Ultimate and Yin-Yang are inseparable, they can be distinguished logically, much like the relationship between human nature and the mind.</summary>

### THE GREAT ULTIMATE.

40. Between Heaven and Earth, there are only the two principles of **Motion** and **Rest** revolving in a perpetual circle; there is nothing else besides these. This is designated "**Change**." This Motion and Rest must have a **Governing Principle** <note>The original text uses "Fate," likely translating *Li*, the fundamental law or logic of the universe</note> which causes them, and this Principle is what is meant by the **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji; the supreme source and sum of all existence</term>.

41. That which is designated the **Great Ultimate** exists within **Yin and Yang** <note>The original uses "Light and Darkness" to describe these dualities</note>, and that which is designated Yin and Yang exists within the Great Ultimate. What some people now assert—that there is a formless and shadowless thing existing outside of Yin and Yang in which the Great Ultimate resides—is incorrect.

42. **Nature** <term>Xing; the inherent moral constitution</term> resembles the Great Ultimate, and the **Heart-Mind** <term>Xin; the seat of consciousness</term> resembles Yin and Yang. The Great Ultimate is right in the midst of Yin and Yang and cannot be separated from them; yet, to speak accurately, the Great Ultimate is the Great Ultimate, and Yin and Yang are Yin and Yang. Nature and the Heart-Mind are also like this. This is what is meant by being "One, and yet Two; Two and yet One."

43. **Reason** <note>Again, the underlying Principle or *Li*</note> is the Great Ultimate of the **Book of Changes** <term>I Ching; the ancient classic of divination and philosophy</term>. One is the **Yang** (the odd number); Two is the **Yin** (the even number); Three is the odd and even numbers added together. When it is said that "Two generated Three," this means that Two and One make Three. If we simply consider the **Monad** <note>The "One" or the primordial unity</note> to be the Great Ultimate, then we do not need to repeat the statement that Reason generated the Monad.

44. Motion and Rest have no beginning, and Yin and Yang have no commencement. Regarding their origin, we cannot say one comes before or after the other; but if we were to divide the cycle in the middle, we can speak of a sequence without detriment to the truth. With regard to the statement of—

# CHINESE TEXT.

Viewed from this perspective, there is naturally a sequence of priority. <gloss>Reply to Wang Zihe</gloss>

What I wrote in my previous letter—that the **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji; the supreme principle of the universe</term> is not outside of **Yin and Yang**—is exactly consistent with what you taught in your message: that it does not depend on Yin and Yang, yet it generates Yin and Yang. However, I believe that while that which is **above form** <term>Xing-er-shang; the metaphysical Principle</term> and that which is **below form** <term>Xing-er-xia; the physical world</term> are actually not mixed at their core, I say it exists *within* Yin and Yang. You, respected elder, argue that because the names of the "above form" and "below form" cannot be confused, it is *not outside* of Yin and Yang. Though the points of departure for our arguments differ, there has never been any fundamental disagreement between us. <gloss>Reply to Cheng Kejiu</gloss>

The meaning of the **Great Ultimate** refers precisely to the ultimate reach of **Principle** <term>Li; the underlying logic or natural law</term>. Where there is this Principle, there is this thing; there is no sequence of "before" and "after" to speak of. Therefore, it is said that the *Book of Changes* <note>original: *Yi*</note> contains the Great Ultimate. This means the Great Ultimate is located right within Yin and Yang, rather than existing outside of them. 

Now, to define it as the "Great Mean" <note>original: *Dazhong*; referring to a central impartiality</note>, or to discuss it as the time before **Heaven and Earth** <note>original: *Qian* and *Kun*; the primary hexagrams representing the creative and receptive forces</note> were decided, or before the **Great Expansion** <note>original: *Dayan*; a numerological concept from the I Ching</note> was divided—I fear this is not quite settled. That which is above form is called the **Way** <term>Dao</term>; that which is below form is called the **Tool** <term>Qi; physical objects or vessels</term>. Now, in discussing the Great Ultimate, to say its substance is called "Spirit," or to speak of it as the state where Heaven and Earth were not yet divided and the **Original Vital Energy** <term>Yuanqi; the primal substance of the universe</term> was merged into one—I also fear this is not quite settled. <gloss>Reply to Cheng Kejiu</gloss>

# THE GREAT ULTIMATE. 47

**Master Zhou** <term>Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), the philosopher who formulated the "Diagram of the Great Ultimate"</term> stated that the **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji; the supreme source of all existence</term> moved and generated the **Light** <note>original: "Light"; referring here to Yang</note>; therefore, before it moved, it must have been at rest. Furthermore, when he says that it rested to its furthest limit and then moved again, it follows that after resting, it must have had motion. For example, Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter, or the cycle of **Sublimity, Potential, Utility, and Firmness** <term>Yuan, Heng, Li, and Zhen; the four fundamental stages of any natural cycle</term>—these cannot help but have a sequence of first and last. If there were no Winter, how could there be Spring? If there were no **Firmness**, how could there be **Sublimity**? Looking at the matter in this way, there is certainly a sequence of priority and posteriority <note>meaning a clear "before" and "after"</note>.

45. What the former letter states—that the **Great Ultimate** is not found outside of the **Light and Darkness** <note>Yin and Yang</note>—is the same as what the present communication says: namely, that while it exists independently of Light and Darkness, it nonetheless generated them. However, I consider that the terms **"Corporeal"** and **"Incorporeal"** <note>referring to "that which has form" (the physical) and "that which is above form" (the metaphysical)</note> mean that these two must not be confused with one another. Hence, I said that the **Great Ultimate** is in the center of the **Light and Darkness**. You, sir, also consider that the terms **"Corporeal"** and **"Incorporeal"** mean they must not be confounded, and thus you say the **Great Ultimate** is not *outside* the **Light and Darkness**. Although our ways of expressing it are different, there is no difference in our meaning.

46. The **Great Ultimate** simply means the **Ultimate Principle** <note>original: "Extreme Fate," a 19th-century rendering of the term *Li*, or natural law</note>. Once this Principle exists, then Matter exists; we cannot truly designate a "first" or "second" between them. The **Book of Changes** <note>original: *Yih King*</note> says, "Change has the Great Ultimate," which means the **Great Ultimate** is right in the center of the **Light and Darkness**, not outside of them. Now, to assert that it is the "Great Center," or to speak of it as existing before **Heaven and Earth** <term>Qian and Kun; the two primary hexagrams representing the cosmic father and mother</term> were divided and the **Great Expansion** <term>The "Great Extension" or "Dayan," a complex system of numerology used in ancient divination</term> commenced—I fear this is not correct. That which

# THE GREAT ULTIMATE. 48

To say that the **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji; the supreme, foundational principle of the universe</term> "contains" activity and stillness is to speak of it in terms of its **Original Substance** <note>original: "benti"; the fundamental essence of a thing as it is in itself</note>. To say that the Great Ultimate "possesses" activity and stillness is to speak of it in terms of its **cyclic flow** <note>original: "liuxing"; the active operation or manifestation of the principle in the world</note>. However, if one were to say that the Great Ultimate *is* simply activity and stillness, then the **Metaphysical** <term>Xing-er-shang; that which is "above form," or the abstract principle</term> and the **Physical** <term>Xing-er-xia; that which is "below form," or the concrete object</term> could not be distinguished. If that were the case, the statement in the *Book of Changes* that "the *Changes* possess the Great Ultimate" would be redundant and unnecessary. <gloss>Reply to Yang Zizhi</gloss> <note>Yang Zizhi, also known as Yang Fang, was a scholar and contemporary of Zhu Xi</note>

The Great Ultimate is the **Principle** <term>Li; the underlying logic or natural law of all things</term> of the **Two Modes** <note>Yin and Yang</note>, the **Four Images** <note>Greater/Lesser Yin and Greater/Lesser Yang</note>, and the **Eight Trigrams** <note>The basic symbols used in divination to represent the fundamental principles of reality</note>. One cannot say that it does not exist; it is only that it has no physical form or image that can be described. Therefore, from this [Ultimate], existence is generated. One Yin and one Yang constitute the Two Modes, and the Four Images and Eight Trigrams are born from these in turn. All of this follows a natural sequence and is not a result of human arrangement. Yet, from the time of **Confucius** until now, not a single person truly grasped this. It was only when **Shao Kangjie** <term>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a Song dynasty philosopher renowned for his mathematical and cosmological theories</term> appeared that this theory was clarified. It is extremely systematic and full of profound meaning; it is worthy of deep contemplation and should not be overlooked. Let us examine it in more detail. <gloss>Reply to Lin Huangzhong</gloss> <note>Lin Huangzhong, or Lin Lize, was a scholar who questioned the traditional diagrams of the Book of Changes</note>

That which is **unexpressed** <note>original: "weifa"; the state of the mind or principle before it is stirred by emotion or external stimuli</note> is the stillness of the Great Ultimate. That which is **already expressed** <note>original: "yifa"; the state of manifestation or activity</note> is the motion of the Great Ultimate. <gloss>Reply to Lyu Ziyue</gloss> <note>Lyu Ziyue, or Lyu Zuheng, was the younger brother of the famous scholar Lyu Zuqian</note>

**Zhu** <note>Zhu Zhu, a student of Zhu Xi</note> asked: "The word **Ultimate** <note>original: "Ji"</note> means the absolute limit, the point at which nothing more can be added. It is a name used to describe the nature of this **Principle**; there is no title in all the world that can surpass it. Therefore, it is always found in the center of all things, acting as their **bullseye** <note>original: "di"; the target or standard by which a thing is measured</note>. Without it, things have no way to—" <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page</note>

# THE GREAT ULTIMATE. 49

...is **Incorporeal** <term>Xing-er-shang; the metaphysical realm of principles</term> is **Reason** <term>Li; the underlying law or principle of the universe</term>, and that which is **Corporeal** <term>Xing-er-xia; the physical realm of objects</term> is the **Receptacle** <note>original: "Qi"; the vessel or physical manifestation of a thing</note>. Now, in speaking of the **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji</term> to say that its Matter is designated **God** <note>original: "Di" or "Shangdi"; Zhu Xi argues that the ultimate principle is not a personified deity with a physical body, but the logic governing creation</note>; or, to speak of it before Heaven and Earth were divided, when the **Original Air** <note>original: "Yuan Qi"; the primordial material force</note> was chaotic and one—I also fear this is not correct.

47. We may say that the Great Ultimate enfolds **Motion and Rest**; this refers to its **Original Substance** <note>original: "Benti"</note>. We may also say that the Great Ultimate has Motion and Rest; this refers to its **active flow** and pervading influence. If we say that the Great Ultimate is *merely* Motion and Rest, then we do not discriminate between the **Physical** and the **Metaphysical**, and the statement that "the *Book of Changes* possesses the Great Ultimate" would be redundant.

48. The Great Ultimate is the **Principle** <term>Li; here translated in the original as "Fate"</term> inherent in the **Two Modes** <note>original: "Liang Yi"; the dual forces of Yin and Yang</note>, the **Four Images**, and the **Eight Trigrams**. We cannot say that it has no existence, and yet, we must not ascribe a physical, corporeal form to it. Therefore the **Light and Darkness** <note>Yin and Yang</note> which are generated from this are the Two Modes; and the Four Images and Eight Trigrams which are generated from it have their order spontaneously, and are not arranged by human power. But, from the time of **Confucius** no one understood this until **Shao Kangjie** <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a philosopher who specialized in the mathematical and cosmological aspects of the I Ching</note> afterwards explained it. His treatise is most connected and interesting; it should be carefully studied and not hastily run over.

49. **Non-manifestation** is the Rest of the Great Ultimate; and **manifestation** is the Motion of the Great Ultimate.

50. **Shu** <note>Shu Cai, a disciple of Zhu Xi</note> asked the meaning of the term "**Ultimate**." Is it the very utmost extreme, so that nothing can be added to it, which is signified by this appellation of **Principle**? That is to say, is it the name for that to which nothing in the whole world can be added? Is this the reason why it is always in the centre of things, being their central...

# THE GREAT ULTIMATE. 50

...is the root and master, yet it cannot be established as a physical entity. Thus, while it is acceptable to consider it as having the **meaning of being in the center**, it is not acceptable to simply define the **Ultimate** <term>Ji; meaning the highest point, pole, or standard</term> as **the Center** <term>Zhong; the middle or the mean</term>. 

If we discuss this in terms of things that possess physical form, then it is like the **ridgepole** <note>original: "jidong"; the highest horizontal timber of a roof that provides structural integrity</note> of a house, or the **central pillar** <note>original: "tongtian zhu"; literally "the pillar that reaches heaven," used here to describe the central support of a granary</note> of a granary. These are always situated in the very center of the object. From all four sides and eight directions, one looks toward these central points to find correct alignment. Amidst a thousand variations and ten thousand differences, all things depend upon this root to have life. 

What the *Book of Rites* <term>Li; a foundational collection of texts describing social forms and rituals</term> calls the **Standard of the People** <note>original: "Minji"; the moral center or ultimate pattern for the populace</note> and what the *Book of Songs* <term>Shi; the oldest existing collection of Chinese poetry</term> calls the **Standard of the Four Quarters**—the meaning is one and the same. I do not know if such an interpretation is correct? 

The Master said: It is. <gloss>Response to Dong Shuzhong. The above consists of eight entries from the Collected Works.</gloss>

# THE GREAT ULTIMATE. 51

...point; and whatever lacks this possesses nothing that can be regarded as the source of its governing power, and thus cannot have any existence. Therefore, we may consider this principle to be *in* the center of all things, yet we must not say that the **Ultimate** <term>Ji; the highest point, pole, or supreme standard</term> of a thing *is* simply the center. 

To illustrate this using things that possess physical form, it resembles the **ridgepole** <note>original: "topmost beam"; the horizontal timber at the apex of a roof</note> of a house, or the **supporting pillar** in a granary. These are always located in the center of these buildings; when they are properly adjusted, the four sides and eight divisions look toward them for their structural integrity.

<meta>This page transitions from the abstract metaphysical principle of the "Great Ultimate" (Taiji) to the physical formation of the universe, explaining how the interaction of Yin and Yang resulted in the creation of Heaven and Earth.</meta>

# HEAVEN AND EARTH

When Heaven and Earth first separated, there was only the **vital energy** <term>Qi; the fundamental vapor or life force of the universe</term> of **Yin and Yang** <note>the complementary forces of dark/passive and light/active</note>. This single energy moved and churned, grinding together. As the grinding grew more intense, many **dregs** <term>sediment or impurities squeezed out from the pure vapor</term> were pressed out. Since they had nowhere to escape from the interior, they congealed to form the Earth in the center. The clear part of the energy became Heaven, the sun, the moon, and the stars, which constantly revolve around the outside. The Earth remains in the center, motionless; it is not simply "at the bottom."

The movement of Heaven never ceases, revolving day and night. Thus, the Earth is held fast in the middle. If Heaven were to stop for even a single breath, the Earth would surely plummet downward. It is only because the rotation of Heaven is so rapid that so many dregs could congeal in the center. Earth is essentially the dregs of this vital energy. Therefore, it is said: "The light and clear became Heaven; the heavy and turbid became Earth."

**Question:** Does Heaven have a physical form or substance?
**Answer:** It is simply a **whirlwind** <note>original: "xuanfeng"</note>. It rolls below and is firm above; the **Taoists** <term>followers of the Way (Dao) who emphasize harmony with the natural world</term> call it the "Hard Wind." People often say that Heaven has **Nine Layers** <note>original: "jiu chong"</note> and give them nine different names, but this is not correct. There are simply nine levels of rotation. The energy below is relatively turbid and dark, while at the highest point, it is perfectly clear and bright.

In the very beginning of Heaven and Earth, when **Chaos** <term>Hundun; the state of the universe before the separation of Yin and Yang</term> was not yet divided, I imagine there were only two elements: water and fire. The sediment of the water became the Earth. Just as when one climbs to a high place...

<gloss>Heaven and Earth, Yin and Yang, Vital Energy (Qi), Dregs (Sediment), Whirlwind, Hard Wind, Nine Layers, Chaos</gloss>

<summary>This page describes a Neo-Confucian cosmology where the rotation of primordial energy (Qi) sifts clear vapors upward to form the heavens and congeals heavy dregs in the center to form the Earth. It explains that the Earth remains suspended in space not by a foundation, but by the sheer centrifugal force of the heavens' constant rotation.</summary>

# HEAVEN AND EARTH

## PART III

### HEAVEN AND EARTH

1. In the beginning, Heaven and Earth were simply the **vital energy** <term>Qi; the fundamental vapor or life force of the universe</term> of the Light and Dark. This single energy moved and churned, grinding round and round. As the grinding grew more intense, a great deal of **sediment** <term>dregs or heavy impurities squeezed out from the pure vapor</term> was compressed. Having no way to escape, these dregs congealed and formed the Earth in the center. The **subtile** <note>refined or delicate</note> portion of the energy then became Heaven, as well as the Sun, Moon, and Stars, which unceasingly revolve on the outside. The Earth remains in the center and is motionless; it does not simply sit at the bottom.

2. Because Heaven revolves without ceasing, Day and Night also revolve, and this motion keeps the Earth exactly in the center. If Heaven were to stand still for even a single moment, then the Earth would surely fall down; however, Heaven revolves so quickly that much sediment is forced to congeal in the center. The Earth is effectively the sediment of this **vital energy** <note>original: "Qi"</note>. Thus, it is said that the light and pure energy became Heaven, while the heavy and turbid energy became Earth.

3. When asked whether Heaven has any physical substance, he replied: It is simply a **spiral wind** <note>original: "xuanfeng" or whirlwind</note>, which is soft below and hard above. The **Taoists** <term>followers of the Way (Dao) who emphasize harmony with the natural world</term> refer to it as "the hard wind." People often claim that Heaven has **nine separate spheres** <note>original: "jiu chong," traditional layers of the sky</note> and give them names as if they were nine distinct locations. This is an error; the spiral ascent* simply has nine levels of rotation. The lower portion of the energy is coarse and dark, while the highest part of the upper portion is perfectly pure and bright.

4. At the beginning of Heaven and Earth, before **chaos** <term>the state of the universe before the separation of elements</term> was divided, I believe there were only two things: Fire and...

---

* This is represented by a <image-desc>small spiral symbol</image-desc>, not by <image-desc>three nested concentric circles</image-desc>. See the **Categorized Compilation of the Five Classics** <note>original: "Wu Jing Lei Bian" (五經類編), a reference work for Confucian literature</note>.

<summary>This page explains the Neo-Confucian theory of cosmogony, where the Earth was formed from the heavy dregs of a spinning whirlwind of energy, while the lighter energy rose to form the heavens.</summary>
<keywords>Qi, Yin and Yang, cosmogony, Nine Layers of Heaven, Taoists, Zhu Xi, Wu Jing Lei Bian, formation of Earth</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical formation of the world, specifically addressing the appearance of the landscape, the cyclic nature of time, and the origins of the first human beings through the interaction of elemental forces.</meta>

...and when one looks at them, the many mountain ranges all have the appearance of waves. This is because they were formed from the flooding of water in just such a way. We simply do not know at what exact moment they congealed.

At the very beginning, matter was extremely light. Only later did it congeal and become firm. When asked if this could be imagined as the tide surging up and leaving sand behind, he replied: Exactly. The most **turbid** <note>original: "zhuo"; heavy, opaque, or impure</note> part of water became the Earth. The most pure part of fire became things such as the wind, thunder, lightning, the sun, and the stars.

When asked: "Since the **Opening and Clearing** <term>Kaipi; the beginning of the current cosmic cycle or 'creation'</term> until today, it has not yet been ten thousand years. We do not know what it was like before then." He replied: Before that, there must have also been a period of clarity such as this. He was then asked if Heaven and Earth could be destroyed. He replied: They will not be destroyed. It is only that when the **Way of man** <note>original: "Dao"; refers here to human morality and social order</note> has been utterly exhausted, everything will collapse together into a state of **Chaos** <term>Hundun; a primordial, undifferentiated mass</term>. Humanity and all things will come to an end, and then everything will start anew.

When asked how the first human was born, he replied: Through the **transformation of vital energy** <note>original: "Qihua"</note>. The **essence of the Two and the Five** <note>The Two refers to Yin and Yang; the Five refers to the Five Elements: Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth</note> joined together to form a shape. The **Buddhists** <note>original: "Shijia," the school of Shakyamuni</note> call this **transformation birth** <term>Huasheng; spontaneous generation from elemental forces</term>. Even today, there are many things born through such transformation, such as lice.

When the universe was still a **primordial mass** <term>Hunlun; the world before it was divided into distinct parts</term> and not yet judged, the energies of the Light and Dark were mixed in deep shadow. Once they had divided, the space between them opened up, becoming vast and bright, and the **Two Poles** <note>original: "Liangyi"; Heaven and Earth</note> were established. **Shao Kangjie** <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a Song dynasty philosopher who used mathematics to explain the structure of the universe</note> used 129,600 years as one **Great Aeon** <term>Yuan; a complete cycle of the universe from beginning to end</term>. Thus, 129,600 years ago, there was another Great Opening and Clearing. If we look even further back, it was the same. It is simply a cycle of movement and stillness without...

<summary>This page discusses the geological formation of mountains from "fluid" origins and details a Neo-Confucian theory of a cyclic universe that resets every 129,600 years. It also explains "transformation birth" as the origin of the first humans and small organisms like lice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Neo-Confucian exploration of cosmology, specifically how the physical world solidified from water and how human life first emerged from the interaction of vital energies. It introduces the concept of recurring cosmic cycles.</meta>

-># HEAVEN AND EARTH<-

Water and its sediment formed the Earth. When we ascend to a high point and look down, the many mountain ranges resemble the waves of the sea in their appearance. This is because the water once flowed in just such a way; I do not know at what specific period it **solidified** <note>The original text uses "coagulated," suggesting a transition from a liquid to a firm state</note>. At first, the matter was very soft, but later it solidified and became hard. Someone asked whether this process resembled sand being thrown up by the tide. He replied: "Exactly so. The coarsest sediment of the Water became the Earth, and the most refined part of the Fire became the wind, thunder, lightning, the sun, and the stars."

5. Someone asked: "From the beginning of Heaven and Earth to the present day is not yet 10,000 years; I do not know how things were before that time?" He replied: "Before this, there was another **opening of clarity** <term>Kaipi; the beginning of a cosmic cycle or the 'opening' of a new world</term> just like the present one." Being further asked whether Heaven and Earth can perish altogether, he replied: "They cannot. However, when mankind becomes totally degenerate, then the whole world shall return to a state of **Chaos** <term>Hundun; a primordial, undifferentiated state where all elements are mixed</term>. Humanity and all things shall cease to exist, and then the world shall begin again." Someone asked how the first human was created, and he replied: "By the **transformation of vital energy** <note>original: "Qihua"; the process by which life is generated from the movement of the atmosphere or spirit-energy</note>. The subtle portions of **Light and Darkness** <note>Yin and Yang</note> and the **Five Elements** <term>Wu Xing; the five phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term> united and produced the human form." The **Buddhists** call this "transformation and generation." At present, things are still transformed and generated in great abundance, much like the spontaneous appearance of lice.

6. Before **Chaos** was divided, the **Atmosphere of Light and Darkness** <note>The primordial Qi</note> was mixed and dark. When it divided, the center formed an enormous and most brilliant opening, and the **Two Principles** <note>original: "Two E" (Liangyi); referring to the fundamental forces of Yin and Yang</note> were established. **Shaou Kang-tsëĕ** <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077 AD), a famous philosopher and cosmologist known for his work on the Book of Changes and cosmic numbers</note> considers 129,600 years to be one **Cycle** <term>Yuan; a major cosmic period often translated as a 'Kalpa'</term>. Therefore, before this current period of 129,600 years, there was another opening and unfolding of the world; and before that again, there was another like the present. Thus, **Motion and Rest**...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on cosmic cycles, shifting from the creation of the first humans to the evidence of geological change and the relationship between Heaven and Earth. -># HEAVEN AND EARTH<-

...ends. **Yin and Yang** <note>original: "Yinyang"; the complementary forces of dark and light, or receptive and active</note> have no beginning. The small is but a shadow of the great. This can be seen simply through the cycle of day and night. This is what **Wufeng** <note>The pseudonym of Hu Hong, a 12th-century philosopher</note> meant when he said: "The single breath of vital energy exhales greatly, vibrating and shaking without limits. The oceans and lands change, mountains rise and rivers are buried. People and things are entirely consumed, and old traces are utterly destroyed." This is called the age of **Great Primal Desolation** <term>Honghuang; a prehistoric, chaotic era before civilization</term>.

I have often seen spiral shells and clam shells on high mountains, sometimes embedded in the stones. This stone was once the soil of olden days, and the shells were once creatures of the water. What was low has changed to be high; what was soft has changed to be hard. If one thinks deeply on this matter, there is proof to be found <note>The author is using the presence of marine fossils on mountains as evidence of prehistoric floods and geological shifts</note>.

Someone asked: "Before Heaven and Earth were divided, were all these many things below already present?" He replied: "It is simply that the **Principle** <term>Li; the underlying pattern or natural law of the universe</term> of them all was present. Heaven and Earth produce things; from ancient times to the present, over thousands and tens of thousands of years, they never depart from these many things."

The Earth has empty gaps or voids, but Heaven surrounds it on all four sides, above and below, completely enclosed without any gap. Everything is tightly packed within the four directions and beneath Heaven and Earth. The Earth rests against Heaven, and Heaven enfolds the Earth. Its **vital energy** <note>original: "Qi"</note> penetrates everywhere. Looking at it this way, everything is simply Heaven. The energy enters and exits from within the Earth, and one can see the vastness of the Earth.

Heaven enfolds the Earth, and the energy of Heaven also moves within the Earth. Therefore, **Hengqu** <note>The pseudonym of Zhang Zai, a pioneer of Neo-Confucianism</note> said: "The Earth cannot contend against Heaven." 

Someone asked about the reason for the height and depth of Heaven and Earth. He replied: "Heaven is simply vital energy. It is not merely that it is high. People today on the Earth only see..."

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Neo-Confucian cosmology, moving from the evidence of geological cycles (fossils) to the spatial relationship between Heaven and Earth. -># HEAVEN AND EARTH<-

**Yin and Yang** <note>original: "Light and Darkness"; the fundamental dual forces of the universe</note> have no beginning. Just as small things reflect great things, this concept can be illustrated by the daily cycle of day and night. This is what **Wufeng** <note>The courtesy name of Hu Hong, a Southern Song dynasty philosopher</note> meant when he described the **Great Cessation** <term>Da jin; the theoretical end of a cosmic cycle where all motion stops</term> of the primal energy. He spoke of a vast and boundless agitation of all things, where the oceans shift their positions, mountains burst apart, and river channels are wiped away. In this state, humanity and all living things come to an end, and every trace of the ancient world is destroyed—this refers to the total destruction of a world cycle by a **Great Flood** <note>original: "Deluge"</note>.

We frequently see the shells of sea snails and pearl oysters on high mountains, seemingly born out of the very stones. These stones were actually part of the soil of a previous world. Because sea snails and oysters belong to the water, their presence on peaks proves that what was once below changed to become high, and what was once soft mud changed to become hard stone. This is a deep subject that deserves careful investigation.

7. Someone asked whether the multitude of things existed before Heaven and Earth divided. He replied: "Only the **Principle** <term>Li; the inherent logic or 'blueprint' of a thing</term> of each thing existed. Heaven and Earth generate all things; throughout all time, ancient and modern, they cannot be separated from the things they have created."

8. The Earth has hollow places and gaps. Heaven, however, completely surrounds it on all sides—above and below—and has no void. Heaven presses inward and fills every space. The four quarters of the Earth slope downward and rest upon Heaven. Heaven embraces the Earth, and its **Vital Energy** <term>Qi; the breath or life-force that animates the universe</term> penetrates every part, so that the whole of existence is within Heaven. This energy is scattered outward from the interior of the Earth, which reveals the Earth's vast capacity to hold and release life.

9. Heaven embraces the Earth, and its energy flows into it. Because of this, **Hengqu** <note>The pseudonym of Zhang Zai, a pioneer of Neo-Confucianism who focused on the study of Qi</note> says that the Earth's scale cannot be compared to that of Heaven.
