<meta>This page continues the discourse on Neo-Confucian cosmology. Having established that the earth's surface changes over vast cycles (evidenced by fossils), the text now turns to the structural mechanics of the universe—specifically how the Earth remains suspended and the relationship between the physical form of the cosmos and the invisible "Qi" that permeates it.</meta>

->### THE STRUCTURE OF THE COSMOS<-

...so high. In short, even the space beneath the earth is part of Heaven. It is also said that in all the world, there is no single thing as vast as the Earth. However, the Earth is merely the **dregs** <term>zhazi; the heavy, physical sediment of energy that settled to form solid matter</term> of the **Vital Energy** <term>Qi; the fundamental substance and life-force of the universe</term>. This is why the Earth is so thick and deep.

Heaven and Earth are but a single manifestation of **Yin and Yang** <note>the dual complementary forces of cosmic order</note>. They are produced, as they always have been, from the energy of these dual forces.

**Kangjie** <note>The courtesy name of Shao Yong (1011–1077), a philosopher famous for his mathematical approach to the universe</note> said: "Heaven relies on form, and Earth is attached to energy." The reason he spoke of this repeatedly and never strayed from this meaning was because he feared people might look for a reality *outside* of Heaven and Earth. Heaven and Earth have no "outside." Therefore, while their physical form has a boundary, their energy is boundless. Because this energy is extremely compressed, it is able to support the Earth and keep it in place; otherwise, the Earth would fall. Outside of this energy, there must be a very thick "outer shell" <note>the furthest reaches of the rotating cosmos</note> that contains and stabilizes this energy. As for the movement of the Earth <note>referring to earthquakes</note>, it is only a localized movement and does not extend to great distances.

The **astronomers** <term>Lijia; specialists in the calendar and celestial observations</term> of ancient and modern times have only been able to calculate the boundaries of the **waxing and waning** <note>original: "xiaozhang"; the cyclic growth and decay of cosmic forces</note> of Yin and Yang. How could they ever match Kangjie’s description of how Heaven relies on Earth and Earth is attached to Heaven? He spoke of how they depend on each other, and how "Heaven relies on form, while Earth is attached to energy."

Someone asked about the meaning of the phrase: "Heaven relies on form, and Earth is attached to energy."

The master replied: "I am concerned that people imagine there is some physical object underneath the Earth supporting it. Heaven moves with great speed, and the broad Earth is positioned in the center. If Heaven itself possessed brightness, then the sun and the moon would not be clearly visible. Heaven has no light of its own; at midnight, it is as black as dregs. This blackness is the true color of Heaven."

It highlights the philosophical contributions of Shao Yong (Kangjie) over purely mathematical astronomical calculations.</summary>

->### HEAVEN AND EARTH<-

10. When asked about the heights and depths of Heaven and Earth, he replied: Heaven is simply **Vital Energy** <term>Qi; the fundamental substance and life-force of the universe, often translated in older texts as "Air"</term>. It is not only the vast expanse we see above us; we must realize that the space beneath the Earth is also part of Heaven. He also remarked that because nothing else in the world is of such immense scale, the size of the Earth is often spoken of with exaggeration. The Earth is merely the dense sediment of the **Vital Energy**, and that is why it is so solid and deep.

11. Heaven and Earth are essentially a single entity composed of **Yin and Yang** <note>original: "Light-Dark"</note>, originally generated by the energy of these dual forces.

12. **Shao Yong** <note>1011–1077, courtesy name Kangjie, a philosopher famous for his mathematical and cosmological theories</note> says that Heaven rests upon physical form, while the Earth is supported by **Vital Energy** <term>Qi</term>. The reason he repeated this idea so often, never wavering from it, was to prevent people from searching for some other realm beyond Heaven and Earth. There is nothing outside of Heaven and Earth; thus, while their physical form has limits, the energy within them is boundless. Because this energy is extremely compressed, it is able to support the Earth; if it were not so, the Earth would fall. Beyond the atmosphere, there must be a very thick "outer shell" that provides strength and stability. Regarding earthquakes, they only agitate one location at a time and do not extend across great distances.

13. Astronomers of both ancient and modern times have merely calculated the cycles of the waxing and waning of **Yin and Yang** <note>original: "Light and Darkness"</note>. How could they ever assert, as **Shao Yong** did, that Heaven rests upon the Earth and the Earth leans upon Heaven? He showed that Heaven and Earth mutually rely on one another—that Heaven rests upon physical form, and the Earth is buoyed by **Vital Energy**.

14. When asked why it is said that Heaven rests upon form and the Earth leans upon **Vital Energy**, he replied that it was to prevent men from claiming there was anything else beneath them.

<summary>This page explains the Neo-Confucian view of the cosmos as a closed system where the Earth is a dense sediment suspended and supported by highly compressed Vital Energy (Qi). It highlights the theories of Shao Yong, who argued that Heaven and Earth are a mutually dependent, singular entity with no "outside."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the cosmos, moving from the physical support of the Earth to the nature of celestial rotation and the philosophical question of whether the universe possesses a conscious "mind." ->### THE COSMOS AND THE DIVINE MIND<-

Heaven is simply one vast entity. One must observe it with an expansive mind to truly grasp it. Speaking of Heaven’s movement, it surely completes one full rotation in a single day. Yet, there is also a cycle of "great rotation"; one cannot seek to understand this through narrow or rigid thinking.

Heaven rotates, but not simply from East to West. It does not spin like a flat grinding wheel; rather, it turns on a slant.

Someone asked about Master **Shao Yong’s** <note>1011–1077, courtesy name Kangjie</note> discussion regarding what lies beyond the **Six Directions** <term>the universe; north, south, east, west, up, and down</term>. Is it likely that there is nothing beyond them?

He replied: **Principle** <term>Li; the underlying pattern or reason of the universe</term> has no distinction between "inside" and "outside." However, the physical form of the universe must have an interior and an exterior. The sun rises from the eastern side and sets in the west; the next day it rises from the east again. There is a vast space above this, and just as much space below us. Is this all not contained within the Six Directions? Calendar-makers <note>astronomers</note> calculate the movement of **Vital Energy** <term>Qi</term>, but they can only calculate as far as the paths of the sun, moon, and stars. They cannot calculate anything further up. How then could there be no "inside" or "outside"?

Someone asked: Does the **Mind of Heaven and Earth** possess consciousness? Or is it merely indifferent and without purposeful action?

He replied: One cannot say that the Mind of Heaven and Earth lacks intelligence, but it does not involve the same kind of deliberation as human thought. Master **Cheng Yi** <note>1033–1107, courtesy name Yichuan</note> said: "Heaven and Earth have no [deliberate] mind yet accomplish their transformations; the Sage has a mind yet practices **Non-action** <term>Wu-wei; effortless action in harmony with the natural order</term>."

Someone asked: Regarding the "Mind of Heaven and Earth" and the "Principle of Heaven and Earth"—does "Principle" refer to the universal path, while "Mind" refers to a **Governing Authority** <term>Zhuzai; the controlling or presiding force</term>?

He replied: The Mind is certainly the governing authority...

->HEAVEN AND EARTH<-

## Heaven revolves quickly, and Earth is placed in the center.

15. If the Heavens were inherently bright, then the Sun and Moon would not be able to provide any visible light. However, the Heavens are not luminous; the darkness we see at midnight is the true color of the Heavens.

16. Heaven is simply one vast entity; we must expand our thinking in order to observe and understand it intelligently. Regarding the revolutions of the Heavens, they complete one full rotation every day. However, there are also cycles of "Great Revolutions" <term>Yuan; vast cosmic cycles of time</term> which cannot be calculated with the same day-to-day precision.

17. The rotation of the Heavens does not move simply from East to West, nor does it spin horizontally like a millstone grinding grain. Instead, it revolves on an oblique <note>tilted</note> axis.

<margin>* North, South, East, West, the Zenith, and the Nadir.</margin>
18. When asked: **Shao Yong** <note>1011–1077, a cosmologist also known as Kangjie</note> speaks of what lies "outside" the **Six Directions** <term>the universe; the four cardinal points plus up and down</term>, but I assume there is no "outside"? 

He replied: **Principle** <term>Li; the fundamental patterns of reality, here referred to as "Fate" or "the Vacuum"</term> has no "inside" or "outside." However, the physical manifestation of the universe—the **Vital Energy** <term>Qi; here referred to as "the Air"</term>—must have an interior and an exterior. The Sun rises in the East and sets in the West, and tomorrow it will rise again in the East; it travels through as much space below us as it does above us. Is this movement not contained within the Six Directions? Astronomers, in calculating the movements of the Vital Energy, only calculate the orbits of the Sun, Moon, and Stars. Their measurements cannot extend beyond these paths. How, then, could they know whether a boundary exists further out?

19. When asked whether the **Mind of Heaven and Earth** <term>the governing intelligence or purpose of the universe</term> is conscious and spiritual, or merely passive and without thought: 

He replied: We must not claim that the Mind of Heaven and Earth is not spiritual. However, it does not "think" or worry about specific matters the way a human being does. **Cheng Yi** <note>1033–1107, a philosopher also known as Yichuan</note> says that Heaven and Earth create and transform without the need for a deliberate "Mind"; the Sage <note>a perfectly realized human</note> uses his mind to act, yet achieves a state of effortless passivity.

He replied: The word "Man" is similar to the word "Heaven," and the word "Mind" is similar to the word "Sovereign."

**Master Cheng** <note>Cheng Yi, 1033–1107, a key philosopher in the development of Neo-Confucianism</note> said: "In terms of governance, it is called the Sovereign; in terms of nature and emotion, it is called the Creative <term>Qian; the hexagram representing Heaven and active energy</term>." These definitions are naturally established. The Mind is simply the seat of governance. Therefore, it is said that Heaven and Earth take the "production of life" as their Mind.

Heaven and Earth have no other business; they simply take the production of things as their Mind. The **Primal Vital Energy** <term>Yiyuan zhi qi; the original, unified breath or life-force</term> revolves and flows through all things, never pausing for even a moment. Just...

### HEAVEN AND EARTH.

20. When asked about the **Mind of Heaven and Earth** <term>Tiandi zhi xin; the underlying consciousness or organizing principle of the universe</term>, and the **Mandate of Heaven and Earth** <term>Tianming; the cosmic decree or destiny</term>; specifically, whether the **Mandate** is the same as our **Inherent Nature** <term>Xing</term>, and if the **Mind** is the **Governing Power** <term>Zhuzai; the presiding or ruling force of the cosmos</term>?

It is not that this Mandate exists apart from the Mind, nor does the Mind exist apart from the Mandate <note>the text emphasizes that these two concepts are eternally united</note>. 

Being further asked whether the Mind and the **Lord on High** <note>original: "Shang-te" or Shangdi, the personified supreme deity or celestial ruler</note> are the same, he replied: Humanity is the same as Heaven, and the Mind is the same as the Lord on High <note>implying a microcosm-macrocosm relationship where the human mind mirrors the divine governing principle</note>.

21. A question was raised regarding the idea of Heaven and Earth "not exercising a mind," contrasted with the statement that "**Benevolence** <term>Ren; the fundamental virtue of life-giving growth</term> is the Mind of Heaven and Earth." If they possess a mind, then they must think, devise, and plan; yet, how can Heaven and Earth plan and devise in the way humans do? 

Moreover, the process by which the **Four Seasons** revolve and all things are generated occurs simply because these things *ought* to be so; they do not wait for human-like planning and calculation. Is this the true **Way of Heaven and Earth**? 

He replied: Just so. Therefore, what the ***Book of Changes*** <note>original: "Yih King" or Yijing</note> says regarding the **Hexagram of Return** <note>original: "Fŭh Diagram" or Fu; representing the return of life-energy</note> manifesting the Mind of Heaven and Earth is strictly correct. Through this, the natural disposition of Heaven and Earth can be observed. 

Furthermore, according to your argument, how can you only speak of them "not exercising a mind"? <note>The speaker suggests that while they don't "think" like humans, they do possess a purposeful order</note>. If, in reality, they never exercised a mind <note>in the sense of a structured principle</note>, then assuredly oxen would produce horses, and peach trees would send forth plum blossoms. But their course is determined spontaneously. 

**Master Zhang** <note>original: "Chang-tsze," referring to the philosopher Zhang Zai</note> says: "Because of the Governing Power, He <note>Heaven</note> is called the **Lord on High**; because of his nature, he is called **The Creative** <note>original: "Khëen" or Qian, the hexagram representing the firm, active principle of Heaven</note>." ...is simply how they bring forth the myriad things. When asked about **Master Cheng’s** <note>Refers to the brothers Cheng Hao or Cheng Yi, pivotal figures in Neo-Confucianism</note> statement that "Heaven and Earth have no mind yet complete their transformations; the Sage has a mind yet practices **non-action** <term>Wu-wei; the principle of acting in harmony with the natural path without forced or artificial effort</term>," 

He replied: This describes the aspect in which Heaven and Earth lack a conscious, deliberate mind. For example, as the four seasons cycle and the hundred things grow—where is there room for Heaven and Earth to harbor a specific intent? As for the **Sage** <term>Sheng-ren; a person of perfect virtue who is in total harmony with the cosmos</term>, he simply follows **Principle** <term>Li; the fundamental laws or patterns of the universe</term> and nothing more. What further "action" would he need to take? 

This is why **Mingdao** <note>original: "Mingdao," the posthumous name of Cheng Hao (1032–1085)</note> said: "The constancy of Heaven and Earth is that their mind pervades the myriad things, yet they have no mind of their own. The constancy of the Sage is that his feelings follow the myriad affairs, yet he has no private feelings." This is the most excellent explanation.

When asked: "Regarding 'pervading the myriad things,' does this mean the Mind is universal and free from selfishness?" 

He replied: Heaven and Earth use this Mind to reach all things. When humans receive it, it becomes the human mind. When objects receive it, it becomes the object's mind. When plants and animals receive it, it becomes the mind of plants and animals. It is all simply the one **Mind of Heaven and Earth** <note>the source suggests a single cosmic consciousness that manifests differently depending on the vessel—human, animal, or plant—that receives it</note>. At present, you must recognize the aspect in which the cosmos *has* a mind, and also see the aspect in which it *has no* mind. You cannot simply define it as one or the other.

The growth of the myriad things is the time when Heaven and Earth have no [deliberate] mind. When things are withered and about to spring back to life, that is the time when Heaven and Earth have a "mind" <note>this implies that the impulse to create life is the most visible "intent" of the universe</note>.

The movement of **Creation** <term>Zaohua; the transformative and productive power of nature</term> is like the upper stone of a mill: it turns constantly without stopping. The birth of the myriad things is like the flour being scattered out from the mill; some grains are coarse, and some are fine <note>this analogy explains how the same cosmic process produces the vast diversity of life, from simple organisms to complex humans</note>.

<summary>This page explores the Neo-Confucian concept of the universe as a spontaneous yet orderly system, comparing the "Mind of Heaven" to a millstone that produces life naturally. It emphasizes that while the universe has no "selfish" intent, its inherent "mind" is found in the constant renewal and growth of all living things.</summary>

<keywords>Master Cheng, Sage, Mind of Heaven and Earth, Non-action, Principle, Mingdao, Creation, Neo-Confucianism, Universal Mind</keywords>

It specifically references the teachings of the Cheng brothers to bridge the gap between cosmic processes and the conduct of the Sage.</meta>

### HEAVEN AND EARTH.

[Formu]lations mean “spontaneously determined” <note>original: "ziran"; the state of being so of itself, often translated as nature or spontaneity</note>. **Mind** <term>Xin; here referring to the cosmic consciousness or governing principle of the universe</term> is just their **Ruling Power**, and hence it is said that Heaven and Earth exert their Mind in generating things.

22. Heaven and Earth have no other occupation than merely to exercise Mind in generating things. The one **Primordial Energy** <term>Yuanqi; the original "breath" or substance from which all things are formed</term> revolves, flows forth, and pervades, without the least cessation, merely generating the **myriad things** <note>original: "wanwu"; the "ten thousand things," representing the infinite variety of life and objects in existence</note> and nothing more. 

Being asked as to the statement of **Master Cheng** <note>original: "Ching-tsze"; likely referring to the philosopher Cheng Yi (1033–1107)</note> that “Heaven and Earth without exerting Mind make and transmute, the Sage exerts Mind but is passive,” he replied: This refers to the period during which Heaven and Earth do not exert Mind. For, when the **Four Seasons** are revolving, and all things are generated, why should Heaven and Earth then exert Mind? With regard to the **Sage** <term>Sheng-ren; a person who has attained perfect harmony with the laws of the universe</term>, he merely obeys **Destiny** <term>Ming; often translated as Fate or the Mandate of Heaven, referring to the inherent path of one's life</term>, and nothing more; what further has he to do? 

Hence **Master Mingdao** <note>original: "Ming-taou," the honorific name for Cheng Hao (1032–1085)</note> says, that “the invariable rule of Heaven and Earth is to exhaust their Mind by pervading all things with it, and the Sage’s invariable rule is to exhaust his natural disposition by adapting it to all circumstances.” This is an excellent statement. 

Being asked whether the pervading all things means thoroughly pervading all things by their Mind without partiality, he replied: Heaven and Earth with this Mind pervade the **myriad things**; Man obtains it, and then it is the Mind of Man; Things obtain it, and then it is the Mind of Things; Grass, Trees, Birds, and Beasts obtain it, and then it is the Mind of Grass, Trees, Birds and Beasts; this is just the one Mind of Heaven and Earth. You should now understand what is meant by their exerting Mind, and also perceive the meaning of their not exerting Mind; only we must not assert the one to the exclusion of the other.

<summary>This page explores the concept that the "Mind of Heaven and Earth" is nothing other than the continuous, spontaneous drive to generate and sustain life. It explains that while the universe has no conscious "intent," its life-giving patterns are universal and shared by humans, animals, and plants alike.</summary>

### HEAVEN AND EARTH (Continued)

Naturally, things are not uniform. He also said: The shape of Heaven and Earth is like two bowls joined together. If you store water inside and keep the bowls spinning constantly with your hands, the water stays inside and does not spill. But the moment you stop your hand, even for a moment, the water leaks out. <note>This famous analogy by Zhu Xi illustrates the idea that the constant rotation of the heavens creates a centrifugal-like force that keeps the Earth and its waters in place.</note>

Heaven surrounds the four sides, while Earth dwells in the center. If you were to remove a foot of earth, a foot of **Energy** <term>Qi; the vital breath, matter, or life-force that constitutes all things</term> would immediately take its place, though people cannot see it. This refers to energy that has not yet taken physical form. Once it floats upward or descends downward, it becomes that which has already taken form. Just as when materials are fused or burned, leaving behind "dregs" or "ashes" <note>original: "zaopo" and "zhazi"; referring to the coarse, material sediment of the creative process</note>, these are the residues of **Energy**. Ultimately, all of these phenomena are intended to reveal **Principle** <term>Li; the fundamental, rational order or "blueprints" of the universe</term> to humanity.

The movement of day and night without ceasing represents the two poles of **Yin and Yang**. The chaotic scattering at the edges is the "Wandering Energy," which gives rise to the infinite variety of people and things. It is very much like a **flour mill** <note>original: "mianmo"</note>: as it turns, it constantly scatters layers of flour from its sides. The **Energy** of Heaven and Earth rotates without end, and in doing so, it constantly produces generations of people and things. Among these, some are coarse and some are fine, just as people and objects may be "biased" or "upright" <note>original: "pian" and "zheng"; these terms describe the quality of one's physical and moral endowment—whether one's nature is balanced (upright) or skewed (biased)</note>.

The **Lord** <term>Di; traditionally referring to a supreme deity or "Shangdi," here interpreted philosophically</term> is simply **Principle** acting as the ruling power.

That which we call "Heaven" is the vast, azure sky. It rotates and flows everywhere without end—that is what it is. If people today say there is a person up there judging <insert>human affairs</insert>... <note>The text here begins to challenge the anthropomorphic view of a god sitting in the clouds, arguing instead for a universe governed by impersonal law.</note>

It redefines the traditional concept of a supreme "Lord" as the governing operation of universal Principle (Li).</summary>

This describes the state before physical forms are finalized. When the heavens float and rise, and the Earth sinks and settles, then their forms are completed. Terms like "coagulation," "dregs," and "residue" <note>original: "zaopo" and "zhazi"</note> refer to the dense sediment of this **Energy**. Truly, these concepts are used solely for the instruction of humanity.

That which is scattered out toward the four sides is the "dispersed energy" from which the vast variety of people and things are created. -># THE NATURE OF HEAVEN AND THE MECHANICS OF THE COSMOS<-

To say that Heaven "judges" sin and evil is certainly not correct. However, to say that there is absolutely no Master <term>Zhuzai; a governing power or "Lord" often discussed in terms of cosmic order rather than a personal deity</term> is also not correct. This is a point where one must see the truth for themselves.

When asked about the use of the word "Heaven" <term>Tian</term> in the Classics and Commentaries, he said: "One must be able to distinguish the meanings clearly. Sometimes it refers to the 'vast blue' <note>original: *Cangcang* (蒼蒼), describing the physical appearance of the sky</note>; sometimes it refers to a Master; and sometimes it is interpreted solely as **Principle** <term>Li; the fundamental moral and natural law of the universe</term>."

Heaven, through its **Energy** <term>Qi; the vital force and material substance of the universe</term>, relies on the physical form of the Earth. Earth, through its form, attaches itself to the Energy of Heaven. Heaven encompasses the Earth; the Earth is merely a single object within the center of Heaven.

Heaven uses its Energy to revolve on the outside; therefore, the Earth is balanced in the middle, remaining still and unmoving. If the movement of Heaven were to stop for even a single breath, the Earth would surely sink and fall.

Jitong <note>Cai Shen (1135–1198), a mathematician and astronomer who was a close disciple of the philosopher Zhu Xi</note> said: "Everything above the ground is simply Heaven."

Heaven and Earth bear no resentment; this refers to the "withering energy" <term>Susha; the 'killing' or chilling air of autumn that causes life to retreat</term> and similar phenomena <note>meaning that the destruction seen in nature is a result of natural cycles, not a display of divine anger</note>.

When asked whether Heaven has a physical form or substance, he replied: "It has none. It is simply Energy rotating with great intensity, like a gale-force wind. At the highest reaches, the rotation is even tighter. If this rotation were to slow down even slightly, the Earth would break loose and plummet."

The Taoists speak of a "Hard Wind of ten thousand miles" <note>original: *Wanli Gangfeng* (萬里剛風)</note> at great heights; this is precisely what they mean. At high altitudes, the Energy is clear and tight; at lower altitudes, the Energy is turbid <term>Zhuo; heavy, dense, or impure</term>, and thus the movement is slower.

<summary>This page discusses the multifaceted definition of "Heaven" in Neo-Confucian thought—as a physical sky, a governing principle, or a cosmic master—and explains the mechanical force (rotating Qi) that keeps the Earth suspended in space.</summary>
<keywords>Heaven (Tian), Principle (Li), Energy (Qi), Master (Zhuzai), Cosmology, Centrifugal Force, Cai Shen, Susha (Withering Energy)</keywords>

28. The vast blue sky is Heaven; it is simply that which revolves and flows outward without ever stopping. Now, to say that Heaven contains a literal person up there who passes judgment on good and evil is an assertion we must not make. However, to say that there is absolutely nothing ruling over it is also an assertion we must not make. People should understand this distinction.

29. When asked about the meaning of the word "Heaven" <term>Tian</term> in the Classics, he replied that people must observe and clearly distinguish the context for themselves. In some passages, the physical blue sky is meant; in others, it refers to the Ruling Power <term>Zhuzai; the governing agency of the cosmos</term>; and in others, it refers to Fate or Destiny.

30. Heaven, through its **Energy** <term>Qi</term>, supports the physical form of the Earth; and Earth, by its form, rests upon the Energy of Heaven. Heaven encompasses the Earth, and the Earth is merely a single object within the center of Heaven. Heaven’s Energy revolves on the outside, and because of this, the Earth remains steady in the center without moving. If Heaven were to cease its revolution for even a single moment, the Earth would surely fall.

31. Jitong <note>Cai Shen (1136–1230), a scholar and disciple of Zhu Xi known for his expertise in cosmic numbers and geography</note> says that everything above the Earth is simply "Heaven."

32. The saying "Heaven and Earth will not forgive" means that all created things are eventually destined for destruction <note>This refers to the "Great Void" or the end of a cosmic cycle where forms dissolve back into Energy</note>.

33. When asked whether Heaven has a physical, bodily substance, he replied: "No; it is merely **Energy** <term>Qi</term> which revolves with great density, much like a whirlwind." At the highest point, the very summit of the cosmos, it revolves with even greater speed and density. If it revolved any more slowly, the Earth would fall.

-># THE LAYERS OF HEAVEN AND THE ORIGIN OF LIFE<-

...scatter. If one imagines climbing higher and higher up a tall mountain, a person would no longer be able to stand. This is because the **Energy** <term>Qi; the vital force and material substance of the universe</term> up there is compressed so tightly. That is the reason. In the *Li Sao* <note>original: "離騷"; an ancient Chinese poem "Encountering Sorrow"</note>, there is a mention of the "Nine Heavens" <gloss>Jiutian</gloss>. Commentators have erroneously explained this by saying there are literally nine separate heavens. From my perspective, there are simply nine layers <gloss>jiuchong</gloss>. The movement of the Heavens consists of many such layers. The inner layers are relatively soft, but towards the outside, they gradually become harder. If one considers the ninth layer, it has become like a hard shell, where the rotation is even more rapid and tight.

At the very beginning of the creation of living things, the essence of **Yin and Yang** <term>the complementary forces of the universe</term> condensed on its own to form two individuals. This was a birth through "Energy-Transformation" <term>Qihua; spontaneous generation from atmospheric energy</term>. It is like lice, which simply emerge naturally. Once these two individuals existed—one female <gloss>pin</gloss> and one male <gloss>mu</gloss>—all subsequent life was produced gradually through seeds. This is called "Form-Transformation" <term>Xinghua; biological reproduction through physical form</term>. All things in the world follow this pattern.

Heaven and Earth belong to the "World of Form" <term>Xing-er-xia; physical or "below form" things</term>, while *Qian* and *Kun* <note>The primary hexagrams of the *I Ching*, representing the metaphysical principles of the universe</note> belong to the "World of Principle" <term>Xing-er-shang; metaphysical or "above form" things</term>. Heaven and Earth are the physical shells of *Qian* and *Kun*, while *Qian* and *Kun* are the inner nature and disposition of Heaven and Earth. 

Now, as for *Qian* <term>The Creative Principle</term>, when it is still, it is focused, and when it moves, it is direct; it is through this that "Greatness" <gloss>da sheng</gloss> is born. As for *Kun* <term>The Receptive Principle</term>, when it is still, it is closed, and when it moves, it is open; it is through this that "Vastness" <gloss>guang sheng</gloss> is born. The *Original Meaning* <note>Zhu Xi’s own commentary on the *I Ching*</note> states: "*Qian* is a single, solid unit, so in terms of its substance, we call it 'Great.' *Kun* consists of two parts and is hollow, so in terms of its capacity, we call it 'Vast'." When scholars did not understand this, he was asked and replied: "These two sentences explain it very clearly, as they describe it in terms of physical form."

<summary>Master Zhu explains that the "Nine Heavens" are actually nine concentric layers of rotating energy and describes the two stages of life's origin: spontaneous generation followed by biological reproduction. He also distinguishes between the physical Heaven and Earth and their metaphysical principles, Qian and Kun.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the physical and metaphysical structure of the universe, specifically addressing the layers of the atmosphere and the dual modes of biological generation (spontaneous vs. sexual). # HEAVEN AND EARTH

In Heaven, there is an infinite, hard wind.* <note>The original text specifies this wind exists at a height of 10,000 li (approximately 3,000 miles).</note> It is simply that at such a height, the **Air** <term>Qi; the vital breath and material substance of the universe</term> is pure and compressed. The lower part of Heaven is merely the "dregs" of this Air, where it revolves slowly and loosely. I believe that if we were able to climb above the highest mountains, we could not stand, because the Air there is so densely condensed. The *Li Sao* <note>original: "Le Saou"; an ancient Chinese poem titled 'Encountering Sorrow'</note> speaks of "Nine Heavens," but commentators mistakenly explain this as nine separate heavens. I believe that Heaven instead has nine spheres <gloss>jiuchong</gloss>. As Heaven rotates, it consists of several layers; the inner layers are soft, becoming gradually harder toward the outer edge. I suspect that at the ninth sphere, a hard shell has formed, and at that point, the rotation is even more compact.

35. At the beginning of the creation of all things, the subtle essence of **Light and Darkness** <term>Yin and Yang; the fundamental polarities of the universe</term> spontaneously condensed to form two individuals of each species. This is "Energy-Transformation" <term>Qihua</term>, where life is generated much like lice appear spontaneously. These two individuals then split apart into male and female, and from that point on, life was produced gradually through their seed. This process is called "Form-Transformation" <term>Xinghua; biological reproduction through physical form</term>; all the myriad things in existence are generated in this way.

36. Heaven and Earth possess physical bodies, but **Qian and Kun** <term>Khëen and khwăn; the Creative and Receptive metaphysical principles</term> are beyond physical form. Heaven and Earth are the physical manifestation of these principles, while **Qian and Kun** represent the inner nature of Heaven and Earth.

37. "Now the state of Rest for **Qian** <gloss>the Creative/Heaven</gloss> is one of undivided focus; his Motion is direct and straightforward, and thus he is the Great Generator. As for the Earth (**Kun**), her Rest is a closing together; her Motion is an opening up, and she is the vast mother of all." The *Benyi* <note>original: "Pun-e"; referring to 'The Original Meaning of the I Ching' by the philosopher Zhu Xi</note> states that **Qian** is a Monad <gloss>a single, solid unit</gloss> and is substantial; therefore, in terms of his essence, he is called "Great." **Kun** is a Duality <gloss>a divided or hollow unit</gloss> and is empty; and therefore, speaking...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical and metaphysical properties of Heaven and Earth, specifically focusing on the relationship between the first two hexagrams of the I Ching, Qian (Heaven) and Kun (Earth). # THE SUBSTANCE OF HEAVEN AND THE CAPACITY OF EARTH

Heaven surrounds the exterior of the Earth, while Earth resides within Heaven. For this reason, we say that the physical substance <term>Zhi; the material quality</term> of Heaven is vast. Yet, if we speak in terms of **Principle** <term>Li; the underlying pattern of reality</term> and **Air** <term>Qi; the vital breath and material substance of the universe</term>, then it is the Earth that actually contains Heaven. The Air of Heaven resides entirely within the Earth, and the Earth fully receives and absorbs that Heavenly Air. This is why we say the capacity <term>Liang; the volume or measure</term> of the Earth is vast and expansive. It is a single entity, a solid reality; from within, this solidity emerges, flowing and giving rise to life. It is but a single thing. 

Thus, we say that **Qian** <note>original: "Qian"; the first hexagram of the I Ching, representing Heaven and the creative principle. Its symbol consists of three solid lines, representing "oneness" and "solidity."</note> is unitary and solid. Though the Earth appears hard and solid, it is actually hollow or receptive. Because of this, the Air of Heaven can flow within the Earth, and all things can spring forth from within the Earth. Therefore, we say that **Kun** <note>original: "Kun"; the second hexagram, representing Earth and the receptive principle. Its symbol consists of three broken lines, representing "duality" and "emptiness."</note> is dual and hollow. 

Yongzhi <note>original: "Yung-chih"; referring to Chen Yongzhi, a scholar and disciple of the philosopher Zhu Xi.</note> says: "The shape of the Earth is like a lung. Although its physical form is hard, its center is inherently hollow. Therefore, the **Active Energy** <term>Yang Qi</term> can rise and descend <gloss>shengjiang</gloss> within it without any obstruction. It can even penetrate through metal and stone." The Earth thus receives this Air, nurturing and developing the **Ten Thousand Things** <note>A traditional term for all life and matter in the universe.</note>. 

The Master replied: "Indeed. In essence, the form of Heaven is like a **bellows** <term>Gubei; a traditional device used to blow air into a forge</term>. Heaven is the outer leather casing of that bellows. It contains a vast amount of Air within, which expands and contracts, waxing and waning. This is why we say that Qian is unitary and solid. The Earth is also an entity, but its interior is entirely filled with this Air. The Air rises and falls, coming and going; because the interior is hollow, it can accommodate this constant movement. Because Heaven surrounds the Earth, we speak of the greatness of its physical substance. Because Earth contains the Air of Heaven, we speak of the vastness of its capacity. This does not mean the physical form of the Earth is limited, which is why we describe it in terms of 'capacity' <gloss>liang</gloss>. It simply means that the Earth completely..." <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page explains the cosmological relationship between Heaven and Earth using the metaphors of a bellows and a lung, illustrating how the "solid" Heaven provides the energy that the "hollow" Earth receives and circulates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the physical and metaphysical relationship between Heaven and Earth. It builds upon the previous discussion of the "vastness" of Heaven’s substance by contrasting it with the "capacity" of Earth to receive and contain Heavenly Air.</meta>

# HEAVEN AND EARTH

With regard to her capability of containing, she is called “Capacious.” His pupils, not understanding, asked for further explanation. He replied: The meaning of these two statements is perfectly clear. Speaking with regard to their form, then Heaven embraces Earth on the outside, and Earth is in the midst of Heaven; hence it is said that his substance <term>Zhi; the material or physical essence</term> is “Great.” Speaking with regard to Fate <note>Likely referring to the "Mandate" or "Principle" (Li) that governs movement</note> inherent in Air <term>Qi; the vital breath or energy of the universe</term> (that is, Chaos <note>original: "Chaos"; referring to the primordial state of undifferentiated Qi</note>), then Earth embraces Heaven, and Heaven’s Air is all in the midst of the Earth. Earth receives his entire Air, and hence her capability of containing is designated “Capacious.” 

“Great” is just oneness; he is a Monad <note>original: "Monad"; here referring to the single, solid Yang line of the I Ching</note>, and therefore solid; from the inside to the outside he is all substantial, flowing forth, and generating. He is just one thing, and hence it is said that **Qian** <note>original: "Khëen"; the first hexagram representing Heaven and the Creative</note> is a Monad and substantial. Earth, although she is firm, is yet hollow; and therefore Heaven’s Air flows forth into the midst of Earth, and issues forth from the interior of the Earth; hence it is said that **Kun** <note>original: "Khwăn"; the second hexagram representing Earth and the Receptive</note> is a Duad <note>original: "Duad"; here referring to the broken Yin line of the I Ching</note> and hollow. 

Yongzhi <note>original: "Yung-tsze"; referring to Chen Yongzhi, a scholar of the Neo-Confucian school</note> said: "The form of the Earth resembles the lungs; although the substance is hard, yet the centre is hollow, and hence the subtile Air <term>Qi</term> ascends and descends in her midst, and there is no obstruction. Although metal and stones can pass through, yet, Earth receives this Air, and generates and nourishes the myriad of things?" 

He replied: Just so; in fact Heaven’s form is like a bellows; Heaven is just the outer skin case of the bellows; in the centre it encloses much Air, opening out (into the formed universe) and shutting up (in Chaos), increasing and diminishing; and hence it is said that **Qian** is a Monad and solid. Earth is but matter; her midst is full of this Air ascending and descending, coming and going. Because her centre is hollow, therefore she can contain this ascending and descending,

Someone asked: "Yi Chuan <note>The courtesy name of Cheng Yi (1033–1107), a foundational Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> says that the term 'Lord' <note>original: "Di"; often translated as Emperor or God, here referring to a supreme governing principle</note> refers to that which governs. What is it that governs?" Master Zhu replied: "There is naturally a governing power. Since Heaven is an entity of ultimate hardness and ultimate **Yang** <term>Yang; the active, bright, and masculine principle</term>, it naturally rotates in this way without ceasing. Because it acts thus, there must be something that governs it. In a place like this, one must perceive it for oneself; it is not something that words can fully exhaust." He then cited a dozen or so lines from Zhuangzi <note>An ancient Daoist text</note>, such as "Who organizes and maintains this? Who presides over this?" and remarked: "He [Zhuangzi] also perceived this principle."

However, as soon as one perishes, another is born." <gloss>The above consists of thirty-nine entries from the Classified Conversations</gloss> <note>original: "Yu Lei"; referring to the 'Zhuzi Yulei', the recorded dialogues of Master Zhu Xi</note>

Because he <note>Heaven is personified as masculine</note> embraces Earth, hence it is said that his substance is "Great;" because she <note>Earth is personified as feminine</note> contains Heaven's Air, hence it is said that her "Capacity" is great. It is not because Earth's form is complete that "Capaciousness" is predicated of her; but, because Earth receives the entire of Heaven's Air,* therefore we speak of the vastness of her "Capacity."

38. Some one asked, with reference to E-chuen's <note>Cheng Yi (1033–1107), a foundational philosopher of Neo-Confucianism</note> statement that the governing Power is called the "Ruler" (Shang-te) <note>original: "Shangdi"; the Supreme Deity or the primary governing principle</note>; what is the governing power? He replied there certainly is a governing power. Heaven is a most hard and *Yang* <term>Yang; the active, bright, and masculine principle of the cosmos</term> thing, spontaneously revolving without ceasing. There must be a governing power, which causes this Motion. People must inquire into this matter for themselves; it cannot be fully decided by merely discoursing about it. Then, pointing out Chang-tsze's <note>Likely Zhang Zai (1020–1077), though sometimes used for the Daoist Zhuangzi; both discussed the hidden mechanics of the universe</note> several statements as to what regulates and directs all this, he said: He also understands this matter.

39. Lëe-tsze <note>Liezi, a figure in early Daoist philosophy</note> says, Heaven is accumulated Air; the Sun, Moon, and Stars, are lights in the midst of this accumulated Air. This is correct. Some one asking whether Heaven and Earth are perishable or not? He replied, Since they have body and Air (*i. e.* soul), how can they avoid perishing? But, after each destruction, there is a fresh generation <note>A reference to the Neo-Confucian theory of cosmic cycles, where the world is destroyed and reborn in vast epochs</note>.

The Astronomical Treatise of the *Book of Jin* <note>original: "Jin Tianwen Zhi"; a standard history covering the years 265–420 AD, famous for its detailed records of celestial phenomena</note> must be read by everyone. <gloss>In response to Li Jingzi</gloss>

[Someone] asked about clarity and turbidity. [He replied] that these are spoken of in terms of Air <term>Qi; the fundamental energy or "vapor" that constitutes all things</term>. Hardness and softness, or excellence and inferiority, are spoken of in terms of Air as it takes on physical quality. Clarity and turbidity likely pertain to Heaven, while hardness, softness, excellence, and inferiority likely pertain to Earth. Between Heaven and Earth, the ten thousand forms of things each have their own particular functions. Only Heaven is firm and steady above, and Earth is submissive and yielding below. They do nothing for their own sake; they take only the production of life as their work. Thus, the *Book of Changes* <note>original: "Yi"; the I Ching</note> says: "The greatest virtue of Heaven and Earth is called 'giving life'." Master Cheng <note>Cheng Yi (1033–1107), a foundational Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> also said: "Heaven simply takes the production of life as its Way." His discussion on the "Mind of Heaven and Earth" as seen in the Hexagram of Return <note>The "Fu" hexagram, which represents the winter solstice and the first stirring of life-giving energy</note>, and his explanation of it as the "beginning of movement," has already made this principle clear. However, what is meant by "taking the production of life as the Way" is not to suggest that the act of producing life is mechanically identical to the Way itself. <gloss>In response to Zhang Qinfu</gloss>

In the *Dialogue of the Fisherman and the Woodcutter* written by Kangjie <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a philosopher famous for his complex cosmological systems</note>, he discusses how Heaven and Earth mutually depend upon one another. Physical form has its limits, but Air <term>Qi</term> is limitless. This argument is extremely well-organized. # HEAVEN AND EARTH.

This is the substance of what Shao Yong <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), also known as Kang-tsëë, was a philosopher and cosmologist known for his complex mathematical systems explaining the universe</note> says; but, the statements of Astronomers should also be examined into, and then you can understand the matter minutely. For example, the commentary on the "Monthly Ordinances" in the *Book of Rites* <note>original: "Yue-lin-soo of the Le Ke"; a classic text detailing the seasonal rituals and celestial movements of ancient China</note>, along with the "Astronomical Treatise" of the *History of the Jin Dynasty*; you must not omit studying these works.

41. Being asked: The designations “clear and turbid” refer to the Air <term>Qi; the fundamental energy or vital breath that constitutes all things</term>; “Hard and Soft,” beautiful and ugly, refer to the Air as it takes on physical substance; I suppose the “clear and turbid” pertain to Heaven, and the “Hard and Soft,” beautiful and ugly, pertain to Earth? He replied; Chen Liaoweng <note>Chen Guan (1057–1124), a scholar known for his moral rectitude</note> says, "Heaven is Air; Earth is substance." The ancients assert this.

42. In the midst of Heaven and Earth, the various classes of things have a myriad of different forms; and each has its own sphere of action. But, Heaven is steadily fixed above and Earth is humbly placed below, and they do not perform a single act except generating things. Hence the *Book of Changes* <note>original: "Yih King"</note> says, “The great virtue <note>original: "virtus"; used here to denote the inherent power or generative efficacy of nature</note> of Heaven and Earth is called generating;” and Master Cheng <note>original: "Ching-tsze"; refers to Cheng Yi (1033–1107)</note> also says, that the path of Heaven is merely to generate. This refers to the Diagram of Return <note>original: "Fǔh Diagram"; specifically the hexagram "Fu" in the I Ching, which symbolizes the return of life-giving energy after the winter solstice</note> which manifests the Mind of Heaven and Earth: also, if referred to the commencement of Motion, this doctrine is clear. But, when he uses the phrase “Take generating to be the Way,” he does not mean to assert that living things are themselves the Way <term>Tao; the ultimate principle or Reason that governs the universe</term>.

43. Shao Yong, in his *Dialogue between the Fisherman and the Woodman* <note>A philosophical work by Shao Yong presented as a conversation about the nature of the world</note>, states that Heaven and Earth mutually lean upon each other, their form being finite, but their Air <term>Qi</term> being infinite; this statement is excellent. At that time, I suppose, the matter was so stated, and hence Master Yi-chuan's <note>original: "E-chuen"; the pen name of Cheng Yi</note> idea agrees with this.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Neo-Confucian cosmology, transitioning from the general nature of Heaven and Earth to the specific mechanics of Yin and Yang, the Five Phases (Wu Xing), and the progression of the seasons. ## Yin and Yang, the Five Phases, and the Seasons

Heaven and Earth are collectively one great Yin and Yang. A year also has its own cycle of Yin and Yang. A month also has its own cycle of Yin and Yang. Even a single day or a single hour follows this same principle. <gloss>The following discusses Yin and Yang</gloss>

The principles of Yin and Yang and the Five Phases <term>Wu Xing; often translated as Five Elements (Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, Water), these are the five dynamic processes that govern the natural world</term> must be kept constantly before one's eyes. Then, these truths will naturally become firmly established in the mind.

The Five Phases act as Yin and Yang to one another, and yet each contains its own internal Yin and Yang.

Those who receive the most refined essence of the Five Phases become human. If we speak only of the Five Phases and do not mention Yin and Yang, it is because the formation of a human requires the completion of the Five Phases. However, Yin and Yang are inherently present within the Five Phases. Therefore, Master Zhou <note>Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), the foundational figure of Neo-Confucianism, known for his work "Explanation of the Diagram of the Supreme Ultimate"</note> said: "The Five Phases are essentially one Yin and Yang." Aside from the Five Phases, there is no other place to seek Yin and Yang. For example, Jia and Yi <term>the first two of the Ten Heavenly Stems, used in the traditional calendar</term> belong to the phase of Wood. Jia is Yang, while Yi is Yin. Bing and Ding belong to Fire; Bing is Yang, and Ding is Yin. There is no need to speak further of Yin and Yang, for Yin and Yang are already contained within them. Someone once asked: "Is this like speaking of the four seasons without needing to mention 'cold' and 'heat'?" I replied: "Exactly."

There is no principle by which Yang arises suddenly. For example, in the mid-month period before the Winter Solstice <note>the shortest day of the year, marking the peak of Yin and the rebirth of Yang</note> is the period of Minor Snow <note>Xiao Xue; a solar term occurring around November 22nd</note>; at that point, Yang has already grown by one-thirtieth of a part.

**Vocabulary:** Yin and Yang, Five Phases, Seasonal Ordinances, Master Zhou, Jia and Yi, Bing and Ding, Winter Solstice, Minor Snow.

<summary>This page explains that Yin, Yang, and the Five Phases are not separate entities but integrated forces that structure all of time and the human essence. It emphasizes the gradual cyclical transition of cosmic energy rather than abrupt changes.</summary>
<keywords>Yin and Yang, Five Phases (Wu Xing), Neo-Confucianism, Zhou Dunyi, Heavenly Stems, Solar Terms, Winter Solstice, Cosmology</keywords>

# PART IV.

## YIN AND YANG—THE FIVE PHASES—THE SEASONS.
<note>original: "LIGHT AND DARKNESS.—THE FIVE ELEMENTS.—THE SEASONS."</note>

1. Heaven and Earth, viewed as a single whole, constitute one grand cycle of Yin and Yang <note>original: "Light and Darkness"</note>. Every year has its own yearly cycle of Yin and Yang; every month has its own monthly cycle; and every day and every hour follow this same pattern.

2. The teachings regarding Yin and Yang and the Five Phases <term>Wu Xing; the five dynamic processes of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water that govern the natural world</term> must always be visualized through direct observation, and only then will they be firmly remembered.

3. When placed in relation to one another, the Five Phases represent Yin and Yang, and yet each individual phase contains both Yin and Yang within itself.

4. Humanity receives the most refined essence of the Five Phases. Often, only the Five Phases are mentioned rather than Yin and Yang; this is because the Five Phases are the actual components that form a human being, while Yin and Yang reside within the very heart of these phases. For this reason, Master Zhou <note>Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), a foundational Neo-Confucian philosopher; "Chow-tsze" in the original transcription</note> says that the Five Phases are essentially a single manifestation of Yin and Yang. If one were to set aside the Five Phases, there would be no other place to find the operation of Yin and Yang. For example, Jia and Yi <term>the first two of the Ten Heavenly Stems, traditional markers used in the Chinese calendar and for categorization</term> belong to the phase of Wood; Jia is Yang <note>original: "Light"</note> and Yi is Yin <note>original: "Darkness"</note>. Bing and Ding belong to the phase of Fire; Bing is Yang and Ding is Yin. We must not speak of Yin and Yang as though they were separate from the Five Phases, for Yin and Yang are found within them. When someone observed that speaking of them separately would be like discussing the Four Seasons without mentioning heat and cold, the Master replied, "Exactly so."

5. Yang <note>original: "The Light"</note> does not arise with sudden speed; for example, during the first half of the month of the winter sol—

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the cyclic nature of Yin and Yang, moving from their relationship with the Five Phases to their gradual, almost imperceptible transition during the change of seasons and their omnipresence in all spatial orientations.</meta>

In the several days leading up to the Winter Solstice <note>The shortest day of the year in the northern hemisphere, marking the turning point where the "Light" (Yang) energy begins to return.</note>, the vital energy has already grown to twenty-seven or twenty-eight parts. Only when that day arrives does the first line <note>This refers to the first solid "Yang" line of the *Fu* (Return) hexagram in the *Book of Changes*, symbolizing the rebirth of light.</note> begin to form. It is not as if there was absolutely nothing yesterday and then today it all suddenly returned. Generally, at the point where the "stripping away" <note>Referring to the *Bo* (Stripping) hexagram, where the dark "Yin" energy has almost entirely displaced the "Yang" energy.</note> is exhausted, new life is born.

Master Zhuang <note>Zhuangzi, the 4th-century BCE Daoist philosopher known for his focus on natural spontaneity.</note> says: "The processes of creation and transformation move in secret; who could ever perceive them?" This is very well expressed. Similarly, Master Lie <note>Liezi, another foundational Daoist teacher to whom the *Liezi* text is attributed.</note> also states that the cosmic rotation never ceases; "Heaven and Earth move in secret; who could ever perceive them?" In all cases, the single vital breath <term>Qi; the fundamental energy or "breath" that constitutes all matter and spirit in the universe</term> does not advance abruptly, nor does a physical form diminish abruptly. One does not notice its completion, nor does one notice its decline. This is because the Yin and Yang <note>original: "Light and Darkness"</note> are gradually waning and gradually waxing. Within a person's own body, from youth to old age, it is no different.

Between Heaven and Earth, there is only Yin and Yang. Therefore, Master Cheng <note>Master Cheng Yi (1033–1107), a seminal philosopher of the Neo-Confucian movement whose work focused on the "Principle" of the universe.</note> says: "There is only one principle of stimulus and response." These forces called Yin and Yang are nowhere absent. Consider "front and back": the front is Yang, the back is Yin. Consider "left and right": the left is Yang, the right is Yin. Consider "above and below": the upper section is Yang, the lower section is Yin.

[The student] asked: In the Master's *Commentary on the Book of Changes* <term>I Ching; the ancient Chinese manual of divination and philosophy</term>, it is said that Fuxi <note>The legendary cultural hero and sage-king of ancient China, credited with creating the Eight Trigrams.</note> created the *Changes* simply to examine the two ends of the waning and waxing of Yin and Yang. This statement is the most comprehensive.

The Master replied: Although "Yin" and "Yang" are two words, they are actually just the ebb and flow <term>xiaoxi; literally "dissolving and breathing," a technical term describing the cyclic movement and transformation of cosmic energy</term> of a single vital breath. One advance and one retreat; one waning and one waxing. Where it advances, that is Yang; where it retreats, that is Yin. Where it grows, that is Yang; where it wanes, that is Yin. It is simply the waning and waxing of this one vital breath that produces...

<summary>This page explains that the transition between cosmic forces like Yin and Yang is a gradual, hidden process rather than a sudden change, using the Winter Solstice and the human life cycle as examples. It further asserts that Yin and Yang are the fundamental binary components of all spatial relationships and the primary focus of the ancient Book of Changes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the gradual transition between Yin and Yang. ->### LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.<-

[At the winter sol]stice, the mid-season vital energy <term>Qi; the fundamental life force or "breath" that constitutes all things in the universe</term> is associated with "Minor Snow" <note>One of the 24 solar terms in the traditional Chinese calendar, usually beginning around November 22nd.</note>, and the Yang <note>original: "Light"</note> has already generated one part in thirty. A few days before the solstice, twenty-seven or twenty-eight parts are already generated. On the day of the solstice itself, an entire solid line (—) <note>The solid line represents Yang in the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) hexagrams.</note> is completed. It is not as if it had no existence yesterday and then suddenly returns fully formed in a single morning. 

Generally speaking, when the hexagram of "Stripping Away" <note>original: "Pŏh Diagram"; referring to the *Bo* hexagram (剝), where Yin has almost entirely displaced Yang.</note> is completed, then this primordial unity <note>original: "Monad"</note> is generated. Master Zhang <note>Zhang Zai (1020–1077), a prominent Neo-Confucian philosopher.</note> says that "formation and transformation proceed imperceptibly; who can perceive them?" This is well said. As Master Lie <note>Liezi, a Daoist teacher to whom the *Liezi* text is attributed.</note> also explains regarding their endless cycles: "Heaven and Earth alter imperceptibly; who can perceive their changes?" Every vital breath <note>original: "Air" or "soul"</note> does not enter the world abruptly, nor does every physical form fail suddenly; yet we can discern neither the moment of formation nor the moment of decay. In the same way, the Yang and Yin <note>original: "Light and Darkness"</note> decrease gradually and increase gradually. The human body, from childhood to old age, operates in precisely the same way.

6. Within the expanse of Heaven and Earth, there is only one system of Yin and Yang, and thus Master Cheng <note>Referring to one of the Cheng brothers, influential Neo-Confucian thinkers.</note> says that there is only one "stimulus and response." To provide an example of how Yin and Yang are omnipresent: What is in front is Yang <note>original: "Light"</note>, and what is behind is Yin <note>original: "darkness"</note>; the left side is Yang, and the right side is Yin. Furthermore, the upper portion of any object is Yang, and the lower part is Yin. 

The students asked the Teacher: "The *Explanations of the Changes* <note>original: "Yih-shwŏ"; a commentary on the *I Ching*.</note> says that Fu Xi <note>The legendary cultural hero credited with creating the I Ching hexagrams.</note> composed the *Book of Changes* <note>original: "Yih King"</note> with a view to investigating the waxing and waning of the two principles of Yin and Yang; is this statement complete?" He replied: "Although Yang and Yin <note>original: "Light and Darkness"</note> are two different names, in reality, they are but a single vital energy <note>original: "Air"</note> which diminishes and accumulates."

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the cyclical nature of Yin and Yang. While the previous page focused on the gradual return of Yang before the Winter Solstice, this page expands the concept to biological functions like breathing and the philosophical applications of dualities in action and knowledge. It further engages with the theories of the Neo-Confucian masters Zhou Dunyi and Zhang Zai.</meta>

Throughout history, the events that occur between Heaven and Earth are infinite. Therefore, Yin and Yang <note>original: "Yin Yang"; the complementary forces of dark/passive and light/active.</note> can be discussed as a single unified entity, or they can be spoken of as two distinct forces.

Someone asked: Is the expansion and dispersion of *Qi* <term>Qi; the fundamental life force or "breath" that constitutes all things in the universe</term> considered Yang, and its contraction and gathering considered Yin? 
The answer was: It is exactly so. Consider the breathing through the nose. The breath going out is Yang; the breath being drawn back in is Yin. Inhalation is like a snail that has emerged from its shell and then shrinks back inside. It is the gathering back of that which was not fully exhausted during exhalation. If one only exhaled and never inhaled, one would die.

A question was asked regarding the breath: Does death occur when the exhalation gradually diminishes until it is completely spent? 
The answer was: Certainly, it is so. However, that *Qi* is also constantly being generated.

Between Heaven and Earth, Yin and Yang are like a ring without an end; this is the principle of their mutual overcoming. The *Classic of the Hidden Accord* <note>original: "Yin Fu Jing"; an ancient Chinese text focused on the relationship between the cosmos and human action.</note> states that the Way of Heaven and Earth is "steeping" <note>original: "jin" (浸); this term implies a gradual, soaking, or permeating process rather than a sudden change.</note>, thus Yin and Yang overcome one another. The choice of the word "steeping" is most subtle and wonderful. Nothing in the world happens abruptly; it is through this gradual permeating that Yin and Yang prevail.

Generally, wherever language presents two related extremes, Yin and Yang are present. For instance, in "opening up things and completing tasks" <note>original: "kai wu cheng wu"; a phrase from the *I Ching* (Book of Changes) referring to the development of the world and the execution of affairs.</note>, opening things is Yang, while completing tasks is Yin. Similarly, in "extending knowledge and acting with effort" <note>original: "zhi zhi li xing"; a central Neo-Confucian concept emphasizing that understanding must be paired with practice.</note>, extending knowledge is Yang, and acting with effort is Yin. The writings of Master Zhou <note>Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), the philosopher who authored the *Taiji Tushuo* (Explanation of the Diagram of the Supreme Ultimate).</note> frequently express this idea; by following his logic, it can be clearly seen.

Master Hengqu <note>Zhang Zai (1020–1077), a philosopher known for his focus on the "Great Vacuity" and the materiality of *Qi*.</note> said: "The wandering *Qi* is chaotic and bustling; when it unites and forms substance, it gives rise to the myriad differences of people and things. The two extremes of Yin and Yang cycle without ceasing, establishing the Great Meaning of Heaven and Earth." This explanation seems a bit fragmented. It is better to simply say: Yin, Yang, and the Five Elements <term>Wuxing; the five phases or agents of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term> cycle and interweave with one another.

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of breathing and the concept of "steeping" to illustrate the gradual, continuous transition between Yin and Yang. It also applies this dualistic framework to human activities like study and practice, critiquing previous philosophical definitions as overly complex.</summary>

7. When further asked whether the *Qi* that produces and scatters is Yang, and the *Qi* that contracts and gathers is Yin? He replied: That is also correct; consider, for example, the exhaling and inhaling of the breath. Exhalation is Yang, and inhalation is Yin. Inhalation is like a snail that has emerged from its shell and then pulls back inside again; it contracts and returns to its inexhaustible foundation. If the breath only went out without ever drawing back in, then death would occur. When asked regarding this process of breathing: does the breath actually decrease bit by bit until it is entirely spent, leading to death? The *Classic of the Hidden Accord* <note>original: "Yin Fu Jing"; an ancient text often associated with Daoist philosophy and strategy.</note> says that the way of Heaven and Earth is *gradual* <note>original: "jin" (浸); literally meaning to soak or steep, suggesting a process that is slow and steady rather than abrupt.</note>, and because of this, Yang and Yin mutually overcome each other. This word "*gradual*" is excellent. In the workings of the universe, there is no frantic rushing; thus, Yang and Yin can steadily alternate.

# CHINESE TEXT.

Ascending and descending, coming and going—this is how the myriad differences between people and things are brought forth, and how the great principles of Heaven and Earth are established.

When asked about the phrase "The One is thus spiritual," <note>original: "Yi gu shen." A foundational concept in Neo-Confucian metaphysics suggesting that the underlying unity of the universe is the source of all mysterious, unfathomable change.</note> [the Master] replied: Hengqu <note>Hengqu is the honorary name of Zhang Zai (1020–1077), a pivotal philosopher whose work focused on the relationship between the material force (Qi) and the Great Void.</note> explained this exceptionally well; it must be examined with great care. However, the version recorded in the *Reflections on Things at Hand* <term>Reflections on Things at Hand (Jinsilu); a seminal Neo-Confucian anthology compiled by Zhu Xi and Lü Zuqian in 1175</term> differs from the original book. At the time, because Bogong <note>Lü Zuqian (1137–1181), known by his courtesy name Bogong, was a scholar who co-edited the *Reflections* but often favored brevity over comprehensive inclusion.</note> was unwilling to include the text in its entirety, it was not possible to add it back in later. 

Regarding "The One is thus spiritual," Hengqu's own commentary says: "Because it exists in two [states] simultaneously, it is unfathomable." This means it is just this one substance, yet it circulates everywhere among all things. Just like what we call the Yin and Yang, the contracting and expanding, the coming and going, the rising and falling—even when operating among tens, hundreds, thousands, or tens of thousands of things—it is all nothing but this single material force. This is why it is said, "Because it exists in two, it is unfathomable." 

Regarding "The Two are thus transformative," <note>original: "Liang gu hua." This refers to the idea that change only occurs through the interaction of opposites, such as Yin and Yang.</note> the commentary says: "This transformation is the operation of the One." For all matters under heaven, a single thing alone cannot transform. Only when there are two can transformation occur. For instance, only through the interaction of one Yin and one Yang can the myriad things be transformed and produced. Although there are two components, ultimately it is simply the operation of this "One" force. This explanation is extremely refined; one must examine it with great care.

Although Master Cheng <note>Referring to the Cheng brothers, Cheng Hao and Cheng Yi, whose teachings formed the basis of the school of thought Zhu Xi would later formalize.</note> did not explain the two words "Spirit" and "Transformation" <term>Spirit (Shen) and Transformation (Hua); in this context, Shen refers to the sudden, mysterious, and instantaneous aspect of the Way, while Hua refers to its gradual, visible processes.</term> very clearly, it was Hengqu who drew out their meaning: transformation that proceeds gradually is called *Transformation*, while the unfathomable unity is called *Spirit*. 

He also said: "The One is thus spiritual," <gloss>Because it exists in two, it is unfathomable</gloss> and "The Two are thus transformative." <gloss>Regarding the phrase "exists in two," it means it may be in the Yin, or...</gloss>

Hengqu <note>Zhang Zai (1020–1077), a philosopher known for his work on Qi (vital energy).</note> says: The abundant, floating Vital Energy <term>Vital Energy (Qi); the life-force or material substance that makes up all things in the universe.</term> compresses and forms physical substance, generating the myriad distinctions between people and things. Because at that time Bogong <note>Lü Zuqian (1137–1181), the co-editor of the anthology, who favored a more concise selection of texts.</note> would not write it out completely, no addition was afterwards made. Often translated as "God" in 19th-century texts, it refers here to the mysterious, divine, or unfathomable quality of natural laws.</note> exists in both places (the Light and the Darkness), and is therefore beyond human understanding. It thus pervades the midst of the myriad of things, so that nothing can exist without this one thing. Hence it is said that the Spirit is in both places, and is therefore beyond human understanding. # THE PROCESS OF TRANSFORMATION AND THE SUPREME ULTIMATE

...in the Light <note>original: "Yang"</note>. When in the Darkness <note>original: "Yin"</note>, the whole entity is Darkness. When in the Light, the whole entity is Light. "Transformation" <note>original: "Hua"</note> is a sequential progression—day after day, month after month, pushing forward step-by-step—until a full year is completed. This is the nature of "Transformation."

Zhiqing <note>Liao Deming, a prominent disciple of Zhu Xi.</note> says: "The One is thus spiritual" <note>original: "Yi gu shen."</note> is like the way activity and stillness serve as each other’s roots. "The dual is thus transformation" <note>original: "Liang gu hua."</note> is like activity reaching its limit and becoming stillness, or stillness reaching its limit returning to activity. The passage where Hengqu <note>Zhang Zai (1020–1077), a philosopher known for his work on the material force of the universe.</note> discusses how the Darkness gathers and the Light must disperse allows us to see the true nature of these dual forces.

The Five Elements <term>Five Elements (Wuxing); Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water, representing the five phases of natural cycles.</term> are ultimately the Light and Darkness. The Light and Darkness are ultimately the Supreme Ultimate <term>Supreme Ultimate (Taiji); the ultimate creative principle or "blueprint" of the universe.</term>. The Supreme Ultimate is fundamentally the Limitless <note>original: "Wuji."</note>. Here, one should contemplate the "time" or state where there was neither Light and Darkness nor a Supreme Ultimate. If one thinks there is only Light and Darkness, then Light and Darkness are merely "that which is below form" <term>Below Form (Xing-er-xia); a philosophical term for the physical, concrete world of objects as opposed to abstract principles.</term>. Yet, if one speaks exclusively of Principle <term>Principle (Li); the underlying natural law or rational order of all things.</term>, then the Supreme Ultimate can never be separated from the Light and the Darkness. One must deeply immerse oneself in contemplation, taking the meaning of the diagrams apart to examine them minutely, and then bringing them back together to observe the whole.

My <note>Likely the voice of Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the primary synthesizer of Neo-Confucian thought.</note> explanation of this says: It is not that the Supreme Ultimate exists apart from the Light and Darkness; rather, it refers to the original substance found within the Light and Darkness, while speaking of it in a way that does not confuse it with them. This sentence contains three levels of meaning and deserves deeper study. The *Book of Penetrating* <note>original: "Tongshu," a major work by Zhou Dunyi.</note> says: "Stillness without activity, and activity without stillness, belong to things. Activity without [merely] being activity, and stillness without [merely] being stillness, belong to the Spirit." One should understand this concept by following this logic...

No one thing in the world can transmute of itself; there must always be *two* for that purpose, and then transmutation <note>original: "Hua," referring to the process of change or transformation.</note> can take place. As for instance, when one Light <note>Yang</note> and one Darkness <note>Yin</note> exist, then these (being Male and Female) can generate the myriad of things. 12. These two words “God” <note>original: "Shen," which in this context refers to the "Spiritual" or "Divine Efficacy"—the mysterious, non-material aspect of nature—rather than a personified deity.</note> and “Transmutation,” although Cheng Zi <note>Ching-tsze; a reference to one of the Cheng brothers, pioneers of Neo-Confucianism.</note> does not treat of them very clearly, yet Hengqu <note>Hwăng-keu; the philosopher Zhang Zai (1020–1077).</note> has explained them. He also says, “Unity, therefore God; Two, therefore He can transmute,” means that He is in both places; sometimes in the Darkness, and sometimes in the Light. When in the Darkness, His whole body is Darkness; and when He is in the Light, His whole body is Light. Transmutation is each thing succeeding in order. Day follows day; month follows month; each festival follows in rotation and completes each year; this is Transmutation. Zhiqing <note>Chih-king; Liao Deming, a disciple of the philosopher Zhu Xi.</note> says, that “Unity therefore God” resembles alternate Motion and Rest, the central point of which is their Root: “Two, therefore He can transmute,” resembles moving to the utmost and resting, resting to the utmost and again moving.

13. Hengqu says: The Darkness collects together, and the Light scatters. Thus he understands the natural disposition of the Light and the Darkness.

# THE SUPREME ULTIMATE, MOVEMENT, AND STILLNESS

...should be examined together.

One asked: “Movement produces the Yang; stillness produces the Yin.” The commentary states: “The Supreme Ultimate <term>Taiji: the ultimate source and governing principle of the universe</term> is the innate wonder of nature; movement and stillness are the mechanism upon which it rides.” Now, the Supreme Ultimate is simply Principle <term>Li: the formal, incorporeal pattern or 'reason' behind all things</term>. Principle cannot be spoken of in terms of movement or stillness. However, it is only through movement that Yang is produced, and through stillness that Yin is produced. Because Principle is housed within Vital Energy <term>Qi: the material force, breath, or 'stuff' of which the physical world is made</term>, it cannot be without movement and stillness. Regarding the phrase “the mechanism upon which it rides,” the word “ride” is used as one rides in a carriage. These states of movement and stillness are carried upon the Vital Energy; almost imperceptibly, movement becomes stillness, and stillness becomes movement again.

The Master replied: “That is correct.”

The questioner asked again: “Movement and stillness have no edge; Yin and Yang have no beginning. That movement is born from the stillness that preceded it, and that stillness was born from the movement before it. For now, we simply speak of the process starting from this point.”

The Master replied: “Indeed.”

It was asked: “When it is said that the Supreme Ultimate moves and produces Yang, does this mean the Yang moves first? The current explanation says: ‘Substance <term>Ti: the fundamental reality or 'body' of a thing</term> must be established before Function <term>Yong: the practical application or 'operation' of that reality</term> can be carried out.’ How is this to be understood?”

The Master replied: “Substance naturally exists first. The subsequent statement that ‘stillness produces Yin’ simply describes the process of mutual generation without end.”

Generally speaking, Yin and Yang are two distinct ends. Yet, within Yin, there is naturally both Yin and Yang; and within Yang, there is also both Yin and Yang. The Way of Heaven <note>original: "Qian"</note> forms the male, and the Way of Earth <note>original: "Kun"</note> forms the female. Although the male belongs to the Yang category, one cannot say he is without Yin. Although the female belongs to the Yin category, one cannot say she is without Yang.
