# LIGHT AND DARKNESS, AND THE SUPREME ULTIMATE

...should be regarded as the period when there was no Light and Darkness <term>Yin and Yang: the dual forces of passive/dark and active/light</term>, and no Great Extreme <term>Taiji: the Supreme Ultimate or the source of all existence</term>. If we consider this Infinite <term>Wuji: the Limitless or the state of non-being before creation</term> to be merely the Light and Darkness, we are in error, for these are assuredly corporeal <note>meaning they have physical form and substance</note>; and if we consider it to be Fate <term>Li: the fundamental Principle or Reason of the universe</term>, then the Great Extreme and the Light and Darkness had not yet become separated—meaning it is different from either of these. 

You must diligently reflect upon this; take the diagrams, open them, and minutely investigate them; also place them together and examine them. I explain these statements thus: it (the Infinite) cannot be separated from the Light and Darkness; the Light and Darkness refer to its original body; and, we must not speak of it as being confounded <note>mixed up or confused</note> with the Light and Darkness. These statements have these three meanings; you must examine them thoroughly. 

The *Tung-shoo* <note>original: *Tongshu*, or "Penetrating the Book of Changes" by the philosopher Zhou Dunyi</note> says: "That which when at Rest cannot Move, and when in Motion cannot Rest is Matter; that which Moves, yet moves not; Rests, yet rests not, is God <term>Shen: the Divine Efficacy or the wondrous, spiritual power of nature</term>." These statements should be compared.

### 15. The Mechanism of the Supreme Ultimate

Being asked as follows: "Moved and generated the Light <term>Yang</term>, Rested and generated the Darkness <term>Yin</term>" is explained to mean that the Great Extreme is the original self-existent Adorner <note>The "Adorner" refers to the subtle, wondrous function of the Supreme Ultimate in shaping the world</note>; Motion and Rest are the spring upon which He rides. The Great Extreme is just Fate <term>Li: Principle</term>. 

We cannot predicate <note>assert or declare</note> Motion and Rest of Fate alone; but, that which Moved and generated the Light, and Rested and generated the Darkness, is Fate inherent in the Air <term>Qi: the Vital Energy or material force</term>, and which therefore cannot but Move and Rest. "The spring upon which He rides," that is, "rides" as in a chariot. His Motion and Rest are the result of His riding upon the Air, while He is not Himself affected by the perpetual Motion and Rest. Is this so? 

The Master replied: "Just so."

# THE DUAL FORCES IN THE BODY AND WORLD

In the human body, the vital energy <term>Qi: the life force or breath</term> belongs to the Active <term>Yang: the light, active, or masculine principle</term>, yet within this energy, there are both Passive and Active aspects. The blood belongs to the Passive <term>Yin: the dark, receptive, or feminine principle</term>, yet within the blood, there are also both Passive and Active aspects. 

When asked about the movement and stillness of these dual forces, generally speaking, Spring and Summer represent movement and belong to the Active; Autumn and Winter represent stillness and belong to the Passive. Regarding the span of a single day: daytime is Active and moves, while nighttime is Passive and remains still. Even regarding a single hour or a brief moment, there is no time without movement and stillness, and no time without the presence of the dual forces. 

It is said: There is no place where the Passive and Active are not present. Whether looking horizontally or vertically, they can always be seen. Looking horizontally, the left is Active and the right is Passive. Looking vertically, the top is Active and the bottom is Passive. A palm turned upward is Active; a palm turned downward is Passive. Facing the light is Active; turning one's back to the light is Passive. 

The text *Correcting Ignorance* <note>original: *Zhengmeng*, a seminal work by the philosopher Zhang Zai</note> says: "The energies of the Passive and Active circulate and arrive in succession; they clash in gathering and dispersing; they seek one another in rising and falling; they intermingle and rub against one another <note>the original text uses the term *yinyun*, describing a state of primordial, misty chaos where elements blend</note>, both combining with and restraining each other. They cannot be made into a single entity." This is precisely what is meant.

Houzhi <note>a student of the master</note> asked: "The Active transforms and the Passive combines—what does 'combining' mean?" 

The reply was: "The Active moves, and the Passive follows it."

There are instances where the Passive and Active are spoken of in opposition, such as East being Active and West being Passive, or South being Active and North being Passive. There are also instances where they are spoken of in an interwoven sense, such as day and night, or cold and heat—one being horizontal and the other vertical. Master Yichuan <note>original: Cheng Yi, a famous 11th-century Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> stated: "The *Book of Changes* <term>Yi: literally 'Change,' referring to the classic divination text the I Ching</term> is about transformation itself." This refers only to those things that are in opposition...

It emphasizes that these forces are inseparable, constantly interacting through movement and stillness.</summary>

# LIGHT AND DARKNESS, MOVEMENT AND STILLNESS

The Active and Passive forces <note>referred to here as Light and Darkness, or Yang and Yin</note> have no beginning; each Movement is generated from the previous Stillness, and each Stillness from the previous Movement; can we describe the matter in this way? He replied: Yes.

16. The Supreme Ultimate <term>Taiji: the "Great Extreme" or fundamental source of all things</term> moved and generated the Active force <note>Light/Yang</note>; does this mean that the Active force moved first? The commentators say that there must first be a physical body in order for the active principle to act through it; how can this be? He replied: the physical body (that is, the force of inertia <note>original Latin: *vis inertiæ*</note>) must certainly exist first. The following sentence, “Stillness generated the Passive force <note>Darkness/Yin</note>,” simply implies that they mutually produce each other without ceasing.

17. Speaking generally, the Active and the Passive are simply two Principles, and the Passive divides into both Active and Passive, just as the Active also contains both Passive and Active. The Creative principle <note>original: *Khëen* (Qian), representing Heaven</note> perfects the Male, and the Receptive principle <note>original: *Khwăn* (Kun), representing Earth</note> perfects the Female. Although the Male belongs to the Active force, we cannot say that he does not also contain the Passive; and although the Female belongs to the Passive force, we cannot say that she does not also contain the Active. The vital energy <note>original: *Air* (Qi)</note> of which a human's body is composed belongs to the Active, and yet it has both Active and Passive aspects; his blood <term>vital spirit <note>original Latin: *anima*</note></term> belongs to the Passive, and yet it also has both Active and Passive aspects.

Regarding each hour and minute; not a single hour is without Movement and Stillness, or the Active and Passive forces. Is this so? # THE INTERACTION OF SUBSTANCE AND FUNCTION

...is merely the flow of the Passive and Active forces <note>Yin and Yang</note>. It does not speak of the complex principle of how these forces interweave and interact. When discussing the *Book of Changes* <term>Yi Jing: an ancient divination text used to understand the patterns of the universe</term>, one must include both these meanings. <gloss>Substance exists after Heaven and Earth. Function arises before Heaven and Earth. That which stands in opposition is Substance. That which flows is Function. Substance is still, while Function is active. ○ The above are twenty items from the Classified Conversations <note>original: *Yulei*, referring to the collected sayings of Master Zhu Xi</note></gloss>

The Passive and Active are simply one vital energy <term>Qi: the breath or energy that makes up all things</term>. When the Passive energy flows, it becomes the Active. When the Active energy condenses, it becomes the Passive. It is not as if there are two distinct things standing against one another. This principle is very clear; Master Zhou <note>Zhou Dunyi, 1017–1073, a pioneer of Neo-Confucianism</note> already stated this in his *Diagram of the Supreme Ultimate*. <gloss>Answer to Yang Yuanfan</gloss>

To say that "the alternation of the Passive and the Active is called the Way <note>the Dao</note>" already involves the realm of physical form and objects. To say that the "Five Natures" <note>the virtues of benevolence, righteousness, propriety, wisdom, and faith</note> are "below form" <term>xing er xia: the physical or concrete world</term> is, I fear, not quite correct. The Passive and Active are indeed "below form" <note>in that they are manifestations of energy</note>, yet the reason *why* they alternate is Principle <term>Li: the underlying law or pattern of the universe</term>. This Principle is "above form" <term>xing er shang: the metaphysical or abstract realm</term>. The "Five Activities" <note>referring to the Five Elements or Phases in practice</note> are indeed <gloss>Answer to Yang Zishun</gloss> "below form," but the Nature of the "Five Constants" <note>the internal moral principles</note> is Principle, and thus is "above form."

A question was asked regarding Master Cai’s statement: "Heaven’s Root" <note>a term for the point where Yang/Light begins to return</note> represents the state of a good person, while the "Moon’s Cave" <note>the point where Yin/Darkness begins to return</note> represents the state of a petty person. He says the "Thirty-Six Palaces" are the lines of the Active and Passive within the Eight Trigrams. I suspect that the terms "human" and "thing" <note>or perhaps the nature of character</note> cannot be so easily judged as good or evil based on these. He also says "the thirty-six palaces are all spring," meaning even the "Moon’s Cave" is part of the spring <note>the life-generating force</note>. The Master replied: The sages and worthies have said in many places that the Active is good and the Passive is evil. Because...

# LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.

Whether horizontal or perpendicular, both forces can be seen. In the horizontal, the left is Light <note>Yang</note> and the right is Darkness <note>Yin</note>; in the perpendicular, the upper part is Light and the lower part Darkness. The palm of the hand is Light, the back of the hand is Darkness. Opposite the light is Light, and the opposite side to the bright one is Darkness. The <term>Zheng Meng <note>original: "Ching Mung," a seminal work by Zhang Zai (1020–1077) titled "Correcting Ignorance"</note></term> says that the Light and Dark Air <term>Qi <note>the vital energy or breath of the universe</note></term> revolve in a circle, coming alternately, collecting and scattering, mutually agitating, ascending and descending, and mutually seeking each other; the male and female influences mutually entwining, mutually joining, and mutually ruling; they cannot be separated from each other. That is the explanation.

19. <term>Hao Zi <note>original: "How-tsze," likely referring to a disciple or contemporary of the philosopher</note></term> asked: The Light changes, and the Darkness unites with it; what is this uniting? He replied: The Light moves on and the Darkness follows it.

20. The Light and Darkness may be regarded as opposites; as for instance, the East is Light and the West is Darkness; the South is Light, and the North is Darkness. They also may be regarded as intermingled; as for instance, day and night, cold and heat, horizontal and perpendicular. <term>Cheng Yi <note>original: "E-chuen," the courtesy name of the influential Neo-Confucian philosopher Cheng Yi (1033–1107)</note></term> says that “<term>Yi <note>original: "yih"</note></term>” signifies “Change;” this refers to the flowing forth and revolving Light and Darkness as opposites, and not to the intermingling Light and Darkness united in one. In speaking of Change we should unite both these ideas.

21. The Light and Darkness are merely one Air <term>Qi</term>. The Dark Air flowing forth becomes Light, and the Light Air coagulating becomes Darkness: it is not that these are two mutually opposed things. This doctrine is very plain; <term>Zhou Dunyi <note>original: "Chow-tsze," 1017–1073, the first of the "Five Masters" of the Song dynasty</note></term>, in his diagram of the <term>Supreme Ultimate <note>original: "Great Extreme," or Taiji</note></term>, explains it.

From the perspective of **Correct Principle** <term>Li <note>the fundamental ordering principle of the universe in Neo-Confucian thought</note></term>, the two forces certainly cannot exist without one another. However, from the perspective of their opposition, each has its own domain. I suspect that what <term>Shao Yong <note>referred to by his posthumous name "Kangjie" (1011–1077), a philosopher who mapped the universe through hexagrams and numbers</note></term> expressed in his poetry refers to the source of all life: that "balanced energy" <note>zheng qi</note> produces human beings, while "unbalanced energy" <note>pian qi</note> produces other creatures and objects. This is the primary distinction between the Light and the Dark. The argument made by <term>Cai Yuanding <note>referred to by his courtesy name "Jitong" (1135–1198), a mathematician and astronomer who was a close disciple of Zhu Xi</note></term> is an extension of this theory, yet his meaning is also sound. <gloss>Reply to Gan Jifu</gloss>

He also said that both Light and Darkness can be described in a positive light; however, it is also acceptable to regard Darkness as evil and Light as good. I am grateful for your instruction that Light is good and Darkness is evil. Indeed, there are many instances where the Sages and Worthies speak in this manner.

Because from the perspective of Correct Principle, the two cannot exist without each other, yet in their opposition, they have distinct roles. I suspect that when Shao Yong first spoke of the Heaven’s Root and the Moon’s Grotto, he was speaking of the combination of the "balanced" and "unbalanced." Later, when he said that "all is Spring" <note>implying a state of universal life and growth</note>, he was focusing solely on the "balanced" and righteous aspect. I do not know if this is correct.

He replied: Look at the *Posthumous Works* <note>the collected teachings of the Cheng brothers, influential early Neo-Confucians</note> where it says, "Both good and evil are part of Heavenly Principle," and "Even evil cannot be said to be outside of one's Nature <note>Xing</note>," and "One cannot say that muddy water is not water." Look also at the *Commentary on the Book of Changes* regarding the section: "The principle that the Light <note>Yang</note> can never be fully exhausted." From these examples, the meaning can be inferred. Furthermore, if we examine the facts of reality—consider creatures such as owls, vipers, and scorpions...

# THE VITAL ENERGY OF HEAVEN AND EARTH

Can it be said that noxious weeds and poisonous herbs are not produced by the vital energy <term>Qi <note>The breath or life-force that makes up all physical things</note></term> of the **Yin** <note>the dark, cool, passive principle</note> and **Yang** <note>the bright, warm, active principle</note> of Heaven and Earth? <gloss>Answered by Jifu <note>Jifu is the courtesy name of the philosopher Cai Yuanding, 1135–1198</note></gloss>

That which fills the space between Heaven and Earth and constitutes the process of **Creation** <term>Zaohua <note>The ongoing transformation and "cooking" of the universe</note></term> is nothing more than the beginning and end, the waxing and waning, of the two vital energies of Yin and Yang. Yang is born in the North, grows in the East, and reaches its peak in the South. Yin begins in the South, reaches its midpoint in the West, and ends in the North. Therefore, Yang usually resides on the left, and its merit lies in giving birth, growing, and nourishing. Its qualities are strength, brightness, public-spiritedness, and righteousness; to it belong all the ways of the **Noble Person** <term>Junzi <note>An idealized person of high moral character</note></term>.

Yin usually resides on the right, and its business is to harm, wound, and destroy. Its qualities are weakness, darkness, selfishness, and personal gain; to it belong all the ways of the **Petty Person** <term>Xiaoren <note>A person of small mind or low moral standing who seeks only personal advantage</note></term>. When the **Sages** created the *Classic of Changes* <note>original: "Yi"</note>, drawing the **Trigrams** <note>the eight symbols representing fundamental natural forces</note> and appending the commentaries <note>referring to the *Xici*, or "Appended Remarks" of the I Ching</note>, their demonstration of the moments of progress and retreat, of waxing and waning, was profound indeed. <gloss>From Fu Bogong's preface <note>Fu Bogong refers to the scholar Fu Yan, a contemporary of Zhu Xi</note></gloss>

The vital energies of Yin and Yang overcome one another, yet neither can exist without the other. However, when it comes to the manifestations of good and evil, the matter is different. For if we speak in terms of vital energy, then movement and stillness have no edge, and Yin and Yang have no beginning. They fundamentally stand side-by-side without a sequence of "before and after," or a distinction between good and evil. But if we speak of the manifestations of good and evil, then the **Human Nature** <term>Xing <note>The innate moral compass provided by Principle</note></term> is fundamentally only good and without evil. The purpose of learning, therefore, is to remove evil...

<summary>This page explains that while both good and "evil" things (like poison) are made of the same universal energy, the Sages associated Yang with the noble person and Yin with the petty person. It argues that while energy itself is neutral, human nature is fundamentally good and must be purified through learning.</summary>

...and is completely good. One can no longer claim that [good and evil] cannot exist without one another. Currently, people take **Yin** and **Yang** as symbols of Good and Evil and claim they are inseparable. Therefore, they feel compelled to say: "The <term>Petty Person <note>Xiaoren; a person of low moral character who pursues selfish gain</note></term> does what is not good, yet a heart for goodness occasionally surfaces," using this as proof that Yin cannot exist without Yang. 

But then, why do they not also say: "The <term>Noble Person <note>Junzi; an exemplary person of high moral character</note></term> does good, yet a heart for evil occasionally surfaces," as proof that Yang cannot exist without Yin? Surely they know that there is no such principle. 

Furthermore, it is said that if one completely overcomes self-interest, one becomes pure <term>Moral Principle <note>Yili; the inherent righteousness and order of the universe</note></term>, which is also not separate from the "correctness" of Yin and Yang. Thus, Good can certainly exist without Evil. Where then is this claim that they "cannot be without each other"? 

Generally, when we reach the subtle depths of Moral Principle—where things join, scatter, and interweave—the transformations are infinite, yet they do not contradict one another. If we discuss it in terms of Yin and Yang versus Good and Evil: the "correct" states of both Yin and Yang are good; it is their "maladies" <note>Original: 疹 (zhen), referring here to corruption, deviation, or imbalance</note> that are evil. <margin>This refers to what Master Zhou <note>Zhou Dunyi, 1017–1073, a founding figure of Neo-Confucian philosophy</note> meant by "Strength can be good or evil; Gentleness can likewise be good or evil."</margin> If we speak of them as symbolic categories, then Yang is good and Yin is evil. If we speak of them in terms of movement and stillness, then Yang is the "guest" <note>the active, manifesting element</note> and Yin is the "host" <note>the quiet, internal element</note>. 

There are many such classifications. One must broaden one's mind to observe them and not be constrained by a single explanation. <gloss>In response to Wang Zihe <note>Wang Sui, a scholar and disciple of the philosopher Zhu Xi</note></gloss>

Yin and Yang fill the space between Heaven and Earth. Their <term>waxing and waning <note>Xiaoxi; the natural cycles of growth and decay</note></term>, their <term>closing and opening <note>Hepi; the cosmic pulse of contraction and expansion</note></term>, bring the myriad things to their end and their beginning. Everywhere the eye falls, whether in the visible or the invisible, there is nothing that is not...

He critiques the idea that a Noble Person must harbor inner evil just because a Petty Person occasionally shows goodness.</summary>

26. The energies of Light and Darkness—<term>Yang and Yin <note>Original: "Light and Dark Air" (Qi)</note></term>—mutually overcome one another, and neither can be seen as non-existent in the natural world. However, if we view them as <italic>symbols</italic> of Good and Evil, the situation changes. Regarding the natural energy of the universe, Motion and Rest have no beginning, and Light and Darkness have no starting point. At their origin, they are a pair; there is no distinction of which comes first or which is "good" or "evil." 

But if we speak of them as symbols of Good and Evil, then Man’s Nature is fundamentally Good and contains no inherent Evil. Through education, a person still desires to cast away Evil and perfect their Goodness; we cannot speak of such a person as anything other than exclusively Good. 

Now, if we claim that Light and Darkness are symbols of Good and Evil, and also say that neither can exist without the other, then we would have to admit the following: while the mean man does evil daily, his "good heart" still manifests at times, proving that Darkness cannot exist without Light. But if that is true, why do we not also say that the Model Man is daily Good, and yet his "evil heart" must manifest at times, to prove that Light cannot exist without Darkness? From this, we can see that the comparison fails.

<meta>This page continues a critical analysis of the I Ching (Book of Changes). ...is exactly so. However, Mr. Su <note>Referring to Su Shi (1051–1101), a famous Song Dynasty statesman and scholar</note> believes that once the hexagram images are established, the forces of Light and Darkness become hidden. He argues that whatever is visible to us are merely physical objects, and not the forces of Light and Darkness themselves. In this, he has lost the true Principle. One who truly understands the fundamental root of Light and Darkness certainly does not simply point to living things and call them "Light and Darkness," but neither do they search for these forces somewhere outside of the physical forms and perceptions of the world. <gloss>Critique of Mr. Su’s Interpretation of the Changes</gloss>

The phrase "one Yin and one Yang" refers to the continuous cycle of coming and going without rest. If we speak of the entire substance of the <term>Way <note>Original: Dao; the ultimate path or fundamental principle of the universe</note></term>, nothing is more manifest than this cycle. However, if one believes that Yin and Yang are merely metaphors used to describe what the Way *resembles*, then the Way and the forces of Yin and Yang become two separate entities—using the latter only to illustrate the former. The origins of Light and Darkness are simply the mechanisms of Motion and Rest. When motion reaches its peak, rest begins; when rest reaches its peak, motion begins. Thus, there is Light within Darkness and Darkness within Light. Nothing exists in isolation or in a state of independence. This interplay of "one Yin and one Yang" is precisely what constitutes the Way. To say today that "one Yin and one Yang" implies a state where these forces have not yet interacted and things have not yet been born—a vast, empty state containing no objects, yet which cannot be called non-existent—is merely a "resemblance" of the Way. But if that were the case, what thing would the Way actually be? These scholars do not understand why the Way is the Way; instead, they wish to use the "teachings of emptiness and extinction" <note>A common Neo-Confucian polemic term for Buddhist concepts of Sunyata (emptiness) and Nirvana</note> to speculate about it. That is why their theories are as they are. <gloss>Critique of Mr. Su’s Interpretation of the Changes</gloss>

Now, to say that the warm, nourishing energy flourishes in the Southeast, while the harsh, freezing energy flourishes in the Northwest, is the teaching of the <term>Ritualists <note>Original: Lijia; scholars specializing in the "Book of Rites"</note></term>. To say that the Light...

->LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.<-

... (derived from the absurdity of the latter proposition) that no such doctrine exists. Moreover, if we claim that by completely overcoming our own depravity and becoming thoroughly Good, we never separate from the "upright" <note>The "upright" (zhèng) portion refers to the balanced, healthy, or correct alignment of universal forces</note> portion of Light and Darkness, then Good would exist without Evil. If that were so, what becomes of our previous argument that one cannot exist without the other? Generally speaking, the boundaries of pure and minute Goodness unite, scatter, and mingle together; their transformations are endless, and they do not oppose one another. 

Furthermore, regarding Light and Darkness <note>Refers to Yin and Yang</note> and Good and Evil: the upright portion of Light and Darkness is entirely Good, while their noxious <note>Harmful or unbalanced</note> portion is entirely Evil. Regarding the various types of these <term>simulacra <note>Original: xiàng (象); refers to the hexagrams or the symbolic images that represent the patterns of the universe</note></term>, the Light is considered Good and the Darkness is considered Evil. In terms of Motion and Rest, the Light is the "guest" and the Darkness is the "host." <note>In traditional Chinese thought, the 'guest' (kè) is the active, moving element that visits, while the 'host' (zhǔ) is the still, receiving foundation.</note> These classifications are numerous. All of this must be examined with great care and cannot be defined by a single, simple statement.

27. Light and Darkness fill the space between Heaven and Earth. They decrease and increase, close and open, and they both end and begin the "myriad of things" <note>A traditional term for all existing things in the universe</note>. Of everything that catches the eye—whether it is corporeal <note>Having a physical body</note> or incorporeal <note>Spiritual or without physical form</note>—not a single thing exists without them. 

Shoo-she <note>Referring to Su Shi (1051–1101), also known as Su Dongpo, a famous statesman and intellectual of the Song Dynasty</note> argues that these symbolic images are substantial realities while the forces of Light and Darkness themselves remain hidden. He believes that everything visible is merely material and is not actually Light and Darkness. This doctrine is incorrect. To truly understand the root of Light and Darkness, we must not simply point to existing physical objects and label them "Light and Darkness"; however, we also must not go looking for these forces in some other place *outside* of the things we can see.

While the previous page discussed the inherent relationship between the forces of Light and Darkness and their connection to Good and Evil, this page pivots to a technical critique of how various schools of thought—Astronomers, Ritualists, and the authors of the "Explanation of the Trigrams"—map these forces onto the physical compass and the calendar.</meta>

# 102

<margin>But</margin>
The claim that <term>Light <note>Original: Yang (陽); the active, bright, and masculine principle of the universe.</note></term> is born at the North <note>Original: Zi (子); the first of the twelve Earthly Branches, associated with the North and the Winter Solstice.</note> and corresponds to the hexagram **Return** <note>Original: Fu (復); representing the return of Light after the height of winter.</note>, while <term>Darkness <note>Original: Yin (陰); the passive, dark, and feminine principle.</note></term> is born at the South <note>Original: Wu (午); the seventh Earthly Branch, associated with the South and the Summer Solstice.</note> and corresponds to the hexagram **Meeting** <note>Original: Gou (姤); representing the first appearance of Darkness after the height of summer.</note>, is the theory held by the Astronomers <note>Referring to the "Calendar-makers" (Li Jia), who aligned the hexagrams with the solar year.</note>. The claim that the trigram **Wind** <note>Original: Xun (巽)</note> is positioned in the Southeast and the trigram **Heaven** <note>Original: Qian (乾)</note> is positioned in the Northwest is the theory found in the *Explanation of the Trigrams* <note>Original: Shuo Gua (說卦); one of the "Ten Wings" or classic commentaries of the I Ching.</note>. These three schools each maintain their own separate theories. While the words of the Ritualists and the Astronomers can still be harmonized, the *Explanation of the Trigrams* stands alone; its arrangement of trigram positions is a theory unto itself and does not coordinate with the other two.

Now, your recent teaching seeks to merge these into a single system, yet within that attempt, there are internal contradictions. This is why I cannot help but have my doubts. To say that the Southeast is a "soft Darkness" position because a single line of Darkness has already been born there, and that the Northwest is a "firm Light" position because a single line of Light has already been born there, would mean that the Light which flourishes in the Spring and Summer could not be considered Light, and the Darkness which flourishes in the Autumn and Winter could not be considered Darkness. Instead, you would be treating the tiny, initial moment of their birth as the primary factor. 

Furthermore, to say that a single line of Darkness is born in the Southeast and a single line of Light is born in the Northwest implies that Darkness is not born at the true South—the "Meeting" position—but instead wanders off to the Southeast. It implies that Light is not born at the true North—the "Return" position—but instead travels to the Northwest. If we say that the **Wind** trigram is placed in the Southeast because of the birth of a single line of Darkness, then would the **Heaven** trigram be placed in the Northwest because of the birth of a single line of Light? Moreover, the original text of the *Explanation of the Trigrams* merely associates the **Wind** trigram with "cleanliness and order," while for **Heaven** it <insert>constantly</insert> takes the meaning of "struggle" <note>The "struggle" (zhàn) refers to the conflict between Light and Darkness at the transition of the year.</note> alone. It never once mentions the initial birth of a single line of Darkness or Light...

# 103

->LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.<-

Continuing without pause is the complete bodily substance of <term>Reason <note>Original: Li (理); the fundamental principle or law of the universe in Neo-Confucianism.</note></term>. Nothing can be clearer than this; however, to imagine that we can take <term>Light <note>Original: Yang (陽)</note></term> and <term>Darkness <note>Original: Yin (陰)</note></term> and establish a comparison between them and Reason is to wrongly assume that Reason, Light, and Darkness are all separate entities that can be added together. The beginning of Light and Darkness is nothing more than the source of <term>Motion and Rest <note>The cyclical nature of the universe: active (Yang) and passive (Yin) states.</note></term>; moving to the furthest point and then resting, resting to the furthest point and then moving. Consequently, Light exists within Darkness, and Darkness exists within Light; neither can exist independently of the other. It is in this manner that the alternating forces of Light and Darkness are defined as Reason. Some now claim, regarding this alternation, that before Light and Darkness interacted and before any objects were created—when not a single thing yet existed—Reason cannot be said to have been non-existent. But if that were true, what exactly is Reason? All such talk reveals an ignorance of how Reason functions and resembles the "mystical school" of thought. It is merely guesswork, which is why they speak in such a way.

29. Regarding the warm and genial <term>Air <note>Original: Qi (氣); the vital energy or material substance that makes up all things.</note></term> gathering in the southeast, and the harsh, cold Air gathering in the northwest: these are the claims of the Ritualists. The idea that Light is generated at the <term>North <note>Original: Zi (子); the first Earthly Branch, representing the winter solstice.</note></term> during the <term>Hui <note>Original: Hui (會); a cosmic time period.</note></term> of the **Return** Diagram <note>Original: Fu (復); Hexagram 24, where the first line of Yang returns.</note>, and that Darkness is generated at the <term>South <note>Original: Wu (午); the seventh Earthly Branch, representing the summer solstice.</note></term> during the **Meeting** Diagram <note>Original: Gou (姤); Hexagram 44, where the first line of Yin appears.</note>, are the claims of the Astronomers. That the position of the **Wind** Diagram <note>Original: Xun (巽); associated with the southeast in the Later Heaven sequence.</note> is in the southeast, and the position of the **Heaven** Diagram <note>Original: Qian (乾); associated with the northwest in the Later Heaven sequence.</note> is in the northwest, are the claims of the Diviners. These three schools of thought each describe the world differently;

# 104

...theories of the past. All these rugged and repetitive arguments ultimately lead nowhere. It is better to directly identify <term>Firm-Light <note>Original: Yang-gang (陽剛); the active, strong principle.</note></term> as <term>Benevolence <note>Original: Ren (仁); the core Confucian virtue of humanity and life-giving warmth.</note></term> and <term>Gentle-Darkness <note>Original: Yin-rou (陰柔); the passive, yielding principle.</note></term> as <term>Righteousness <note>Original: Yi (義); the virtue of justice, boundary-setting, and structural integrity.</note></term>. 

This approach is clear and simple. If we view it this way, then the process of growth and origination is Benevolence, while the stern, withering air of autumn is Righteousness. The theories of the various schools of thought have failed to grasp this. Although the "withering air" <note>Original: su-sha (肅殺); the cold, killing frost that clears away the old in autumn.</note> appears to be "firm" or "harsh," it is in truth the energy of the universe contracting and withdrawing into hiding; thus, there is no harm in classifying it as Gentle-Darkness. <gloss>Reply to Yuan Jizhong <note>Yuan Jizhong, also known as Yuan Shu (1131–1205), was a scholar and contemporary of Zhu Xi known for his work on the Book of Changes.</note></gloss>

Regarding the <term>Twelve Hexagrams <note>Original: Shi-er gua (十二卦); these represent the twelve stages of the lunar/solar year.</note></term>, the Light begins at the <term>Rat <note>Original: Zi (子); the North, representing the Winter Solstice when Light is first reborn.</note></term> position and reaches its conclusion at the <term>Snake <note>Original: Si (巳); the end of Spring/beginning of Summer.</note></term> position. Darkness begins at the <term>Horse <note>Original: Wu (午); the South, representing the Summer Solstice when Darkness is first reborn.</note></term> position and reaches its conclusion at the <term>Pig <note>Original: Hai (亥); the end of Autumn/beginning of Winter.</note></term> position. 

There is another theory regarding the energy of the <term>Four Seasons</term>: here, Light begins at the <term>Tiger <note>Original: Yin (寅); the start of Spring.</note></term> and ends at the <term>Goat <note>Original: Wei (未); the end of Summer.</note></term>, while Darkness begins at the <term>Monkey <note>Original: Shen (申); the start of Autumn.</note></term> and ends at the <term>Ox <note>Original: Chou (丑); the end of Winter.</note></term>. Although these two theories seem slightly different, the conflict is only over two positions on the calendar. 

The reason for this is that at the Rat position, the first spark of Light is indeed born, but it has not yet emerged from the earth. By the time it reaches the Tiger position—associated with the <term>Hexagram of Peace <note>Original: Tai Gua (泰卦); Hexagram 11, symbolizing a balance where Heaven and Earth interact.</note></term>—the three stages of Light have emerged above the ground, and the warm, thick energy begins from that point. At the Snake position—the <term>Hexagram of Heaven <note>Original: Qian Gua (乾卦); Hexagram 1, representing pure active energy.</note></term>—the six stages of Light have reached their peak, yet the warm energy has not yet finished its course. Therefore, at the Horse position, though the first stage of Darkness is born, it does not yet harm the Light. It is only when reaching the Goat position—the <term>Hexagram of Retreat <note>Original: Dun Gua (遯卦); Hexagram 33, representing the withdrawal of Light as Darkness grows.</note></term>—that the warm energy finally dissipates. 

Similarly, while Darkness is born at the Horse position, the "stern and freezing air" does not truly begin until the Monkey position. At the Pig position, although the six stages of Darkness have reached their peak, the freezing air does not fully exhaust itself until the Ox position. The logic follows this same pattern. The energy within the earth is difficult to see <note>The author explains the "lag" between astronomical shifts (solstices) and physical weather (the heat of summer or cold of winter).</note>.

...and the statements of the <term>Ritualists <note>Scholars focused on the 'Li' or Rites, often correlating social order with cosmic cycles.</note></term> and <term>Astronomers <note>Official court observers who calculated the calendar and planetary movements.</note></term> may possibly be reconciled. With regard to the <term>Diviners <note>Those who used the I Ching (Book of Changes) for prophecy and determining auspicious timing.</note></term>, the position of the <term>Diagrams <note>Original: Gua (卦); refers to the hexagrams or trigrams used in Chinese cosmology.</note></term> depends upon their own statements alone, and they differ from both the other schools. 

In the present communication these three are treated as one, and yet statements are made which are inconsistent with such an idea, and which make it positively doubtful. Now, to assert that at the south-east point a <term>Darkness <note>Original: Yin (陰); the passive, cool, and receptive principle.</note></term> is already generated, and that this point is the throne of the Darkness and softness; that, at the north-west a <term>Light <note>Original: Yang (陽); the active, warm, and creative principle.</note></term> is already generated, and that this point is the throne of the Light and Darkness; then, the accumulated Light in Spring and Summer cannot be regarded as Light, and the accumulated Darkness in Autumn and Winter, cannot be regarded as Darkness. This is, on the contrary, to regard what is only their minute commencement, as their full power. 

If we assert that a complete Darkness is generated at the south-east and a complete Light at the north-west; then, the Darkness is not generated at the exact south at the <term>Woo <note>Original: Wu (午); the direction of the South, associated with the peak of Summer and high noon.</note></term> throne, but erroneously goes to the east; and the Light is not generated at the exact north, at <term>Tsze <note>Original: Zi (子); the direction of the North, associated with the Winter Solstice and midnight.</note></term>, the throne of the <term>Fŭh Diagram <note>Original: Fu (復) or Hexagram 24, "Return"; it represents the first spark of Yang energy returning at the Winter Solstice.</note></term>, but lingers at the west. To say that the <term>Seuen Diagram <note>Original: Xun (巽) or Trigram/Hexagram "Wind"; in the Later Heaven sequence, it is positioned in the South-East.</note></term> is generated by a Darkness, and has its throne upon the south-east, then, is not this to consider that <term>Khëen <note>Original: Qian (乾) or Trigram/Hexagram "Heaven"; it represents pure Light/Yang energy.</note></term> is generated by a Light and that its throne is upon the north-west? 

Moreover, with regard to the original Diagrams, these allude only to their purity and completness in the <term>Seuen</term>, and to their contending in the <term>Khëen</term>, and nothing more; and do not assert any thing about the commencement of the generation of each Light and Darkness. All this, therefore, is irregular, and in fact, perfectly unintelligible. It is not so clear or laconic as just to consider the Light...

# 106

...the energy of the earth is easily recognized. Therefore, the **Zhou people** <note>The Zhou Dynasty (c. 1046–256 BCE).</note> established the month of the <term>Zi <note>Original: 子; the first of the twelve Earthly Branches, corresponding to the month of the winter solstice.</note></term> as the start of the year. Although this follows the <term>Heavenly System <note>Original: Tian Tong (天統); the cosmic order of celestial movements.</note></term>, when **Confucius** discussed the governance of a state, he favored the **Xia calendar** <note>The calendar of the Xia Dynasty, which begins in early spring when life begins to stir, rather than at the mid-winter solstice.</note> as the standard. This was because it clearly illustrates the visible beginning and end of <term>Yin and Yang <note>The fundamental dualities of dark/light, passive/active.</note></term>. By examining the diagrams and tracing their logic, this explanation becomes clear. <gloss>Reply to Yuan Jizhong</gloss>

Your previous letter suggested that the warmth and richness of the Southeast corresponds to <term>Humaneness <note>Original: Ren (仁); the primary Confucian virtue of benevolence or "human-heartedness."</note></term>, while the harshness and freezing cold of the Northwest corresponds to <term>Righteousness <note>Original: Yi (義); the virtue of justice, duty, and discernment.</note></term>. This phrasing originates from the <term>Meaning of the Village Drinking Ceremony <note>Original: Xiangyin Jiuyi (鄉飲酒義); a chapter in the Book of Rites detailing the moral symbolism of social rituals.</note></term>. However, if we examine the source of those words, although it distinguishes between Humaneness and Righteousness, it does not apply the distinction between <term>Yin and Yang</term> or <term>Soft and Hard</term>. 

Yet, later in that text, there is the theory that **Yang energy** emerges from the East. From this, it can be inferred that Humaneness belongs to Yang, and Righteousness must belong to Yin. Your letter failed to observe this, and instead, you insisted on making Humaneness "Soft" and Righteousness "Hard." 

This is already an error, and it leads to the further problem of "Softness" being unable to belong to Yang, and "Hardness" being unable to belong to Yin. Consequently, you have forced "warmth and richness" to be categorized as Soft, and "harshness and freezing" to be categorized as Hard. Furthermore, you have moved the Yin of the North to the South to govern the "Softness of Humaneness," and moved the Yang of the South to the North to govern the "Hardness of Righteousness." Regarding the compass directions and the seasonal climates, you have completely reversed them. The arguments you use to justify this are mostly inconsistent and contradictory, and they simply do not fit together. By asserting that the Northeast is Yang and the Southwest is Yin, you have grasped only half the truth while losing the rest...

### LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.

...and the "Hard" <note>Referring to the active, solid principle often associated with Yang.</note> to be <term>Humaneness <note>Original: Benevolence (仁, Ren); the primary Confucian virtue. While usually seen as "soft" or life-giving, the author is exploring a specific categorical alignment here.</note></term>, and <term>Darkness <note>Original: Yin.</note></term> and the "Soft" to be <term>Righteousness <note>Original: Rectitude (義, Yi); the virtue of justice or duty.</note></term>; for in this way, the act of generating will be Humaneness, and the act of destroying <note>In this context, "destroying" refers to the pruning or "killing" energy of autumn that allows for harvest and eventual rebirth.</note> will be Righteousness; and then the statements of the three schools are all reconciled. Although destruction resembles the "Hard," yet, in reality, the collected and stored up <term>Energy <note>Original: Air (氣, Qi); the vital force or vapors of the universe.</note></term> of Heaven and Earth may, without objection, be considered Darkness and Soft.

30. With regard to the **12 Diagrams** <note>These are the twelve "Sovereign Hexagrams" of the I Ching, which represent the waxing and waning of Yin and Yang throughout the lunar year.</note>, the **Light** <note>Yang.</note> commences at <term>Zi <note>Original: Tsze (子); the first Earthly Branch, corresponding to the 11th lunar month and the Winter Solstice.</note></term> and ends at <term>Si <note>Original: Sze (巳); the sixth Earthly Branch, corresponding to the 4th lunar month.</note></term>; the **Darkness** <note>Yin.</note> commences at <term>Wu <note>Original: Woo (午); the seventh Earthly Branch, corresponding to the 5th lunar month and the Summer Solstice.</note></term>, and ends at <term>Hai <note>Original: Hae (亥); the twelfth Earthly Branch, corresponding to the 10th lunar month.</note></term>. Although these divisions of the circle are slightly different, yet the difference only extends to two "thrones" <note>Positions on the compass/calendar wheel.</note> (one commencing at Zi and the other at Yin). In the **Zi** throne, although one part Light is generated and does not yet come forth from the formed Earth, yet, arrived at the **Yin** throne—the <term>Tai Diagram <note>Original: T'ae (泰); Hexagram 11, "Peace," where the three Yang lines have risen above the three Yin lines, symbolizing the arrival of Spring.</note></term>—then three Lights are generated and come forth above the Earth, and the warm genial Energy commences from this point. At the **Si** throne—the <term>Qian Diagram <note>Original: Khëen (乾); Hexagram 1, "The Creative," consisting of six solid Yang lines.</note></term>—although it is the extreme limit of six Lights, yet the warm, genial Energy has not ended; and therefore, at the **Wu** throne, although one Darkness is generated, and does not yet injure the Light, yet, arrived at the **Wei** throne—the <term>Dun Diagram <note>Original: Tun (遁); Hexagram 33, "Retreat," where the first two Yin lines begin to push the Yang lines upward and out.</note></term>—the Energy which succeeds the warm and genial Energy begins to draw to a close. At the **Wu** throne the Darkness is already generated, and the "coagulated" <note>Referring to the cold, condensing nature of Yin energy.</note> Energy commences at **Shen**. At the **Hai** throne, although the extreme limit of six Darknesses, yet the coagulated Energy is exhausted at the **Chou** throne. Righteousness also resembles this. Now the Energy in the midst of the Earth is diffi—

# 108

...its half. I have already clearly seen the errors within those diagrams.

I have previously argued that **Light** <note>Original: Yang (陽).</note> governs advancement, while **Darkness** <note>Original: Yin (陰).</note> governs retreat; Light governs growth, while Darkness governs decline. That which advances and grows possesses a strong <term>Energy <note>Original: Qi (氣); the vital breath or force of the universe.</note></term>, whereas that which retreats and declines possesses a weak Energy. This is the reason why Darkness and Light are characterized as the "Soft" and the "Hard."

The "Hardness" of **Light** is warm and thick. It resides in the Southeast, governs Spring and Summer, and takes "generation and growth" as its primary work. The "Softness" of **Darkness** is stern and congealing. It resides in the Northwest, governs Autumn and Winter, and takes "gathering and storing" as its primary work. Generation and growth constitute the act of life; gathering and storing constitute the act of "killing" <note>In the natural cycle, "killing" (殺, sha) refers to the pruning or withdrawal of life force in autumn and winter, which allows the cycle to reset.</note>. This is the reason why Hardness and Softness correspond to <term>Humaneness <note>Ren (仁).</note></term> and <term>Righteousness <note>Yi (義).</note></term>.

Viewed from this perspective, are the proper positions of Light and Darkness, Hardness and Softness, and Humaneness and Righteousness not perfectly clear?

As for the statement by **Yang Ziyun** <note>Yang Xiong (53 BCE – 18 CE), a famous Han dynasty philosopher whose work *Taixuanjing* attempted to provide a system similar to the I Ching.</note> that "Humaneness is soft and Righteousness is hard": he was speaking only from the secondary manifestations of their practical application. This is what is called "Darkness within Light" and "Light within Darkness." While his view may certainly stand as its own valid interpretation, it is not discussed here simply to avoid mixing different categorical levels.

<margin>Reply to Yuan Jizhong <note>Yuan Shu (1131–1205), a historian and scholar known for the "Chronicles of the Zizhi Tongjian," who frequently corresponded with the Neo-Confucian master Zhu Xi.</note>. The above items consist of eleven entries from the Collected Works.</margin>

->**LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.**<-

...is difficult to discern, but the **Air** <note>Original: Qi (氣); the vital energy or breath of the universe.</note> above the Earth is easy to recognize; therefore, the people of the **Chow Dynasty** <note>The Zhou Dynasty (c. 1046–256 BCE).</note> established **Tsze** <note>Original: Zi (子); the first of the twelve Earthly Branches, associated with the winter solstice and the start of the Light (Yang) cycle.</note> to be the first throne. Although they thus obtained the **Complete Heaven** <note>A term referring to the full cycle of the celestial movements.</note>, yet, according to the instructions of **Confucius** concerning government, they took the **Hea Dynasty** <note>The Xia Dynasty (c. 2070–1600 BCE).</note> time to be the first throne <note>Original: Yin (寅); the third Earthly Branch. While the Zhou calendar began the year at the winter solstice (Zi), the Xia calendar—which Confucius preferred—began in early spring (Yin).</note>; thus making plain the commencement and termination of the **Light and Darkness** <note>Original: Yang (陽) and Yin (陰).</note>. On examining the diagram, this explanation will be clear.

31. The present communication considers the warm and genial **Air** of the southeast to be **Benevolence** <note>Original: Ren (仁); often translated as Humaneness.</note>, and the coagulated **Air** of the northwest to be **Rectitude** <note>Original: Yi (義); often translated as Righteousness or Justice.</note>. This is the statement found in the **Hëang-yin-tsëw-e** <note>Original: Xiangyin Jiu Yi (鄉飲酒義), or "The Meaning of the District Cup-Quaffing Rite," a chapter in the Classic of Rites.</note>. But, if we take this as our authority, although it distinguishes Benevolence and Rectitude, yet it does not discriminate between the Light and Darkness, or the **Soft and the Hard**. 

However, afterwards, there is another statement to the effect that the **Light Air** issues forth on the east; thus taking Benevolence to belong to the Light, and Rectitude to belong to the Darkness, as may be deduced from this. The present communication shows a want of examination into this, and considers Benevolence to be **Soft**, and Rectitude to be **Hard**; this is a mistake. 

A further mistake is the claim that the Soft cannot belong to the Light, nor the Hard to the Darkness; and thus, the warm and genial Air is forced to be Soft, and the coagulated Air is forced to be Hard. Moreover, it removes the northern Darkness to the south, causing it to rule over the Softness of Benevolence; and removes the southern Light to the north, causing it to rule over the Hardness of Rectitude. This is to reverse the position of the thrones, and is the origin of such [erroneous] statements.

***

* Heaven emerges from Chaos in the **Tsze** <note>Original: Zi (子).</note> period, during the twilight of the world, when 1/30th portion of the Light is generated.

# CHINESE TEXT.

In a previous written reply, the Master stated: "The **Nature** <note>Original: Xing (性); the inherent essence of a being or thing.</note> of **Light and Darkness** <note>Original: Yang and Yin.</note> and the **Five Elements** <note>Original: Wuxing (五行); Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth.</note> are each endowed by a single **Air** <note>Original: Qi (氣); the vital energy or material force of the universe.</note>, yet the **Nature** is one."
I asked: "Are these two instances of the word '**Nature**' the same?"
He replied: "They are the same." He further explained: "That which is the same is the **Principle** <note>Original: Li (理); the underlying cosmic order or pattern.</note>; that which differs is the **Air**. This is why it is said that in the production of the **Five Elements**, each possesses its own unique nature."
The student **Jie** <note>Refers to the student recording the conversation.</note> asked again: "Does this mean that Wood is inherently Wood and Fire is inherently Fire, yet their **Principle** remains one?"
The Master responded: "Consider this light. Some of it falls upon the lid of the inkstone, and some falls upon the ink itself. The light is essentially one and the same." <gloss>The following discusses the Five Elements</gloss>

The most refined essence of the **Air** is called the **Spirit** <note>Original: Shen (神); here referring to the mysterious, transformative power of the divine or natural order.</note>. The physical elements of Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth are not the **Spirit** themselves; rather, that which *causes* them to be Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth is the **Spirit**. In human beings, this is the **Principle**; it is the source of **Humaneness, Righteousness, Propriety, Wisdom, and Trustworthiness**. <note>The Five Constant Virtues of Confucianism.</note>

Regarding Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth: although it is said that each of the **Five Elements** has its own nature, one must also understand that every single object also possesses the **Principle** of all five. **Kangjie** <note>The courtesy name of Shao Yong (1011–1077), a Song Dynasty philosopher famous for his cosmological and mathematical studies.</note> deduced this with great precision.

The **Heavenly One** <note>A reference to the *Hetu* or "Yellow River Map," a numerological diagram where numbers represent the creation of elements.</note> naturally produces Water, and the **Earthly Two** naturally produces Fire. The production of Water simply means that from the very beginning, the quality of **wetness** <note>Original: Shi (濕).</note> was present. Wood...

### LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.

is utter confusion and perverseness: they cannot be reconciled together. Moreover, to make the north-east the **Light** <note>Original: Yang (陽); the active, bright, and masculine principle.</note>, and the south-west the **Darkness** <note>Original: Yin (陰); the passive, dark, and feminine principle.</note>, is partly correct and partly incorrect. I have already traced all these errors on the diagram. 

Now, to explain the matter; the **Light** rules over entering, and the **Darkness** over receding; the **Light** rules over increase and the **Darkness** over decrease. Entering and increasing, the **Air** <note>Original: Qi (氣); the vital energy or material force of the universe.</note> is strong; receding and decreasing, the **Air** is weak: it is thus that the **Light and Darkness** are **Hard and Soft** <note>Original: Gang (剛) and Rou (柔); often used in the I Ching to describe the nature of hexagram lines.</note>. 

The **Light** is **Hard**, warm and genial, dwells at the south-east, and rules over the Spring and Summer; and to cause growth is its province. The **Darkness** is **Soft** and congealed, dwells on the north-west, rules over the Autumn and Winter, and to gather in and store up is its province. Growth is Life; gathering in and storing up is Death; and it is thus that the **Hard** and the **Soft** are **Benevolence and Rectitude** <note>Original: Ren (仁) and Yi (義); the primary virtues in Confucian ethics, here mapped onto cosmic forces.</note>. 

Thus examining the matter, are not the thrones of the **Light** and the **Darkness**, the **Hard** and the **Soft**, **Benevolence and Rectitude**, apparent? And, as to the statement of **Yang Tsze-yun** <note>Referring to Yang Xiong (53 BCE – 18 CE), a Han dynasty philosopher and author of the *Tai Xuan Jing* (Canon of Supreme Mystery).</note>, that **Benevolence** is also **Soft**, and **Rectitude** also **Hard**, this refers to their activity not flowing forth. Now, as to what has been said about **Darkness** being in the **Light**, and **Light** in the **Darkness**; his meaning does not differ from this: but we must not confound this with the other statements in our explanations.

[Wood] is born with a soft foundation. Metal is born with a hard foundation. Vice-Director Huang asked: "Do the physical substances of Metal, Wood, Water, and Fire all belong to Earth?"

[Huang] asked: "Since fire is born by attaching itself to wood, does it not also belong to Earth?" 

[The Master] replied: "Fire itself is a thing of the empty void." 

[Huang] asked: "Is the mere presence of warm, hot **vital energy** <note>Original: *Qi* (氣).</note> then Fire?" 

[The Master] replied: "Correct."

32. That which is unchangeable is **Principle** <note>Original: "Fate" (理, *Li*). In Neo-Confucian thought, this is the underlying law or blueprint of the universe.</note>, and that which is diverse is the **Vital Energy** <note>Original: "Air" or "Monad" (氣, *Qi*). This is the "stuff" or breath of the universe that takes many forms.</note>. He also remarked that the texts state, regarding the generation of the **Five Elements** <note>Original: *Wu Xing* (五行): Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water.</note>, that each has its own Nature. It is now further inquired whether this means that Wood is spontaneously Wood, and Fire is spontaneously Fire, and that their inherent **Principle** is a single **Unity** <note>Original: "Fate is Unity."</note>. The teacher replied, it is just like the light reflecting upon the cover of this inkstone, and also upon the ink; that light is but one.

33. The pure and bright portion of the **Vital Energy** is the **Divine** <note>Original: "God" (神, *Shen*). In this context, it refers to the spiritual essence or the marvelous activity of nature rather than a personal deity.</note>; Metal, Wood, Water, and Earth are not the Divine. However, that which causes them to become Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth is the Divine. In Man, this is **Principle** <note>Original: "Fate."</note>, which causes him to possess **Benevolence**, **Rectitude**, **Propriety**, **Wisdom**, and **Trustworthiness** <note>These are the Five Constant Virtues of Confucianism.</note>.

34. Although Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth are called the **Five Elements**, and each has its own nature, yet each has the **Principle** of the Five Elements—acting as one **Vital Energy**—inherent within it. We should not be ignorant of this. **Master Kangjie** <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a philosopher famous for his mathematical and cosmological systems.</note> has already explained it in great detail.

35. **Heaven** is the **One** <note>Original: "Monad" (一, *Yi*).</note> and spontaneously generated Water; the **Earth** is the **Two** <note>Original: "Duad" (二, *Er*).</note>, and spontaneously generated Fire. Generating Water merely means that when these forces united, the whole mass was moist. Wood is merely the generation of a **Soft** substance; Metal is the generation of a **Hard** substance. The explanation of the Five Elements given in the *Correcting Ignorance* <note>Original: *Zheng Meng* (正蒙) by Zhang Zai.</note> is correct: it says that Wood is the subtle and clear portion of Earth; and also that Fire and Water do not originate from Earth. These statements from *Correcting Ignorance* are very accurate, and not one word is unnecessary.

This was also the concept of the “one” used by the Greek philosopher **Pythagoras**.

# THE SUBSTANCE OF YIN AND YANG

Yin takes Yang as its substance; Yang takes Yin as its substance. Water is bright on the inside but dark on the outside. Fire is dark on the inside but bright on the outside. **Hengqu** <note>The courtesy name of Zhang Zai (1020–1077), a foundational thinker of Neo-Confucianism.</note> said: "The essences of Yin and Yang are mutually stored within each other's dwellings." This is precisely what he meant.

<column-break/>

Pure brightness reflects inward, while turbid brightness reflects outward. Pure brightness belongs to Metal and Water. Turbid brightness belongs to Fire and the Sun. 
Through the transformation of Yang and the union of Yin, Water and Fire are first produced. Water and Fire are **Vital Energy** <note>Original: *Qi* (氣). At this stage, they are seen as ethereal forces rather than solid matter.</note>; they are fluid and flickering, and their substance tends toward emptiness. Their physical forms are not yet fixed.

<column-break/>

Next, Wood and Metal are produced, and these have definite and fixed forms. While Water and Fire initially arise spontaneously, Wood and Metal depend upon the **Earth** <note>Original: *Tu* (土). In the sequence of the Five Elements, Earth is often seen as the central anchoring phase or the "mother" that provides physical substrate.</note>. All types of Metal come forth from within the Earth as it revolves and produces them.

<column-break/>

Generally, when Heaven and Earth produce things, the light and pure come first, followed by the heavy and turbid. **Heaven’s "One" produces Water; Earth’s "Two" produces Fire.** <note>A reference to the *Hetu* (River Chart) numerology where 1, 3, 5 are heavenly numbers and 2, 4 are earthly numbers.</note> These two substances are the lightest and purest among the Five Elements. Metal and Wood are heavier than Water and Fire, and Earth is heavier still than Metal and Wood. 

However, if discussing the **Pitch Pipes** <note>Original: *Lülü* (律呂), the standard system of musical temperament.</note>, the heavy and turbid are considered first. **Gong** <note>The first note of the pentatonic scale.</note> is the heaviest and most turbid; **Shang** comes next, followed by **Jue**, then **Zhi**, and finally **Yu** is the last.

<column-break/>

Regarding the transition of Yin and Yang into the Five Elements: there are those who speak of them separately—for instance, Wood and Fire are Yang, while Metal and Water are Yin. There are also those who speak of them in combination...

# THE TEN STEMS AND THE FIVE ELEMENTS

For example, the **Jia** of Wood, the **Bing** of Fire, the **Wu** of Earth, the **Geng** of Metal, and the **Ren** of Water are all **Yang** <note>The "Light" or active principle</note>. On the other hand, **Yi, Ding, Ji, Xin,** and **Gui** are all **Yin** <note>The "Dark" or receptive principle</note>. By reasoning from this, the principles of the **Five Constants** <term>Wuchang: the five cardinal virtues of benevolence, righteousness, propriety, wisdom, and trustworthiness</term> and the nature of Strength and Submissiveness can be perceived. <gloss>Reply to Huang Shangbo</gloss> <note>original: "Hwang Shang-pih," a student of Zhu Xi</note>

Being asked: Regarding the sequence of one—Water, two—Fire, three—Wood, four—Metal, and five—Earth; I venture to suggest that at the very beginning of **Energy** <note>Qi</note>, there was only warmth. Warmth leads to steaming dampness; steaming dampness leads to growth and extension; extension leads to solidification; and solidification leads to physical form and substance. Although these five elements exist simultaneously, if we deduce their chronological order, the logic might follow this path. He replied: I previously saw **Wu Dounan** <note>A scholar known for his work on the *Book of History*</note> state that the **Five Matters** and the **Various Verifications** <term>Shuzheng: various omens or signs of governance found in the "Great Plan" chapter of the Book of Documents</term> should all follow this sequence. His words also have logic. <gloss>Reply to Huang Shangbo</gloss>

Being asked: The Two Energies <note>Yin and Yang</note> and the Five Elements create and transform the myriad things. With every closing and opening, ten thousand changes are born. Is the so-called "Energy of the Five Elements" the same as the movement of thunder, wind, water, and fire? Or is it the uneven and scattered variations of the Two Energies? Former scholars said that "every single thing possesses all elements," but if a person's **Energy-endowment** <term>Qibing: the specific quality of Qi an individual receives at birth, determining their health and character</term> is biased toward one element, can it still be said they "possess all"? Some say that while all things possess them, within the Five Elements, some receive more and some receive less. Thinking on this, I am quite bewildered and do not understand. He replied: The Energy of the Five Elements is like—

### LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC. <note>Yin and Yang</note>

41. ...they should assume were undetermined. Secondly, they generated Wood and Metal; and then their forms were determined. Water and Fire were spontaneously generated at first, then Wood and Metal commenced from the Earth. The five kinds of metals were successively produced from the Earth.

42. Speaking generally, Heaven and Earth generated all things; their light and pure **Air** <term>Qi: the vital energy or "breath" that constitutes all matter</term> was produced first, followed by the heavy and gross. Heaven is the **Monad** <note>original: "Shang-te" (Shangdi), the Lord on High; the "Monad" refers to the singular, active principle of One</note> and generated Water; the Earth is the **Duad** <note>The receptive principle of Two</note> and generated Fire. These two things are the most *subtle* and pure portions of the Five Elements. Metal and Wood are heavier than Water and Fire, and Earth is heavier than even Metal or Wood. To illustrate this using the notes in music: the heavier and coarser notes come first. **Gong** <note>original: "Kung"</note> is the most heavy and coarse note, **Shang** is next, **Jue** <note>original: "Këö"</note> is next to that, then comes **Zhi** <note>original: "Ch'ing"</note>, and **Yu** is last.

43. If we divide and categorize **Light** <note>Yang</note> and **Darkness** <note>Yin</note> according to the Five Elements, then Wood and Fire represent the Light, while Metal and Water represent the Darkness. If we speak of them as a single, united **Air** <note>Qi</note>, then the **Jia** of Wood, the **Bing** of Fire, the **Wu** <note>original: "Mow"</note> of Earth, the **Geng** <note>original: "Kăng"</note> of Metal, and the **Ren** <note>original: "Jin"</note> of Water are all Light. Conversely, **Yi** <note>original: "Yih"</note>, **Ding**, **Ji** <note>original: "Sze"</note>, **Xin**, and **Gui** <note>original: "Kwei"</note> are all Darkness. By examining this, you can understand the doctrine of the strength or yielding nature of the **Five Constants** <term>Wuchang: the five cardinal virtues—benevolence, righteousness, propriety, wisdom, and trustworthiness—mapped onto the elements</term>.

44. Being asked: The first element is called Water, the second Fire, the third Wood, the fourth Metal, and the fifth Earth. I suppose this arrangement means that the **Air** <note>Qi</note> at the beginning was warm, and being warm it then ascended as hot vapor. This ascending vapor then spread out like a bud; by spreading out it coagulated, and once coagulated it took on a substantial form. Although these five elements all existed at the same moment, yet,

Categories such as warmth, coolness, cold, heat, dryness, moisture, hardness, and softness—all these things that fill the space between Heaven and Earth are of this nature. If you pick up a single object, there is none that does not possess these five qualities; the only difference lies in the proportions of each. <gloss>Categories such as the five musical notes, the five colors, and the five flavors are also like this. — Reply to Lyu Ziyue</gloss> <note>Lyu Ziyue was a scholar and contemporary of the philosopher Zhu Xi, often engaging in metaphysical correspondence.</note>

A question was asked: "Regarding the order of their generation based on physical substance, we say: Water, Fire, Wood, Metal, and Earth. In this system, Water and Wood are considered **Light** <note>Yang</note>, while Fire and Metal are considered **Darkness** <note>Yin</note>. Does this refer to the sequence indicated on the diagram? Why are Water and Wood called Light, and Fire and Metal called Darkness?"

The answer was: "Heavenly One generates Water; Earthly Two generates Fire; Heavenly Three generates Wood; Earthly Four generates Metal. One and Three are odd numbers, belonging to **Light** <note>Yang</note>; Two and Four are even numbers, belonging to **Darkness** <note>Yin</note>." <gloss>Reply to Lin Ziyu</gloss> <note>This refers to the "River Chart" (Hetu), an ancient cosmological diagram where odd numbers are associated with Heaven/Light and even numbers with Earth/Darkness.</note>

A question was asked: "Regarding the order of their movement based on the **Air** <term>Qi: the vital energy or "breath" that constitutes all matter</term>, we say: Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water. In this case, Wood and Fire are **Light** <note>Yang</note>, while Metal and Water are **Darkness** <note>Yin</note>. Does this refer to the way they operate in practice? Why are Wood and Fire called Light, and Metal and Water called Darkness?"

The answer was: "This is spoken in terms of the four seasons. Spring and Summer constitute the **Light** <note>Yang</note>, while Autumn and Winter constitute the **Darkness** <note>Yin</note>." <gloss>Reply to Lin Ziyu. The above are five entries from the Collected Works.</gloss>

The Master asked the scholars: "How did the ancients arrange the twelve double-hours of the day?" The students pondered it but could not find the answer. 44. Regarding the examination into their order of priority and posteriority, I assume it is as has been stated?

He replied: I previously saw a statement by Wu Zunnan <note>original: "Woo Tsow-nan," a scholar of the period</note> that the **Five Activities** <note>The human faculties</note> and the **Five Regulators** <note>The environmental forces</note> should all be arranged in this specific order; his statement is correct.

45. A question was asked regarding the **Two Energies** <term>Yin and Yang: the fundamental polarities of the universe</term> and the **Five Elements** <term>Wuxing: Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term>, which create and transform the myriad of things. Through the alternating cycles of the universe's "opening and closing," these myriad changes are produced. That which is called the **Energy** <term>Qi: the vital breath or material force of the universe</term> of the Five Elements consists of the revolving forces of Thunder, Wind, Water, and Fire, as well as the mingling and scattering of the two primary Energies <note>Yin and Yang</note>. 

Earlier scholars claimed that these elements are inherent in every single thing. However, if the Energy that a human receives can be deflected or unbalanced, can it truly be said that this Energy pervades all things equally? Some suggest that although all things possess these <gloss>referring to both the "Good" and "Evil" (balanced and unbalanced) Energy</gloss>, they are received in different proportions within the Five Elements. Are these statements perhaps confused or unintelligible?

He replied: The various types of the Energy of the Five Elements—for example, Warmth, Coolness, Heat, Cold, Dryness, Moisture, Hardness, and Softness—completely fill the space between Heaven and Earth. If you take any single object, it contains all these Five Elements; however, some things obtain them in a greater proportion, while others receive them in a lesser proportion.

Does this refer to their specific order in the Diagram? <note>Referring to the Hetu or "River Chart," an ancient cosmological map</note> And if so, how can Water and Wood be called Light, while Fire and Metal are called Darkness?"

# THE ETYMOLOGY OF TIME AND THE VITAL ENERGY

The character for **intent** <note>original: 志 *zhi*</note> is derived from the components for "to go" and "heart." It signifies that which the heart is directed toward. In ancient times, the character for **time** <note>original: 時 *shi*</note> was similarly derived from "to go" and "sun." It also signifies the point the sun has reached. For instance, when the sun reaches the position of "Noon" <note>the seventh of the twelve earthly branches</note>, it is called the **Hour of the Horse** <note>original: 午時 *wushi*, 11 AM – 1 PM</note>. When it reaches the "Late Afternoon" position, it is called the **Hour of the Sheep** <note>original: 未時 *weishi*, 1 PM – 3 PM</note>. All twelve hours of the day follow this logic.

Researching ancient philology, we find that the word for **Sun** <note>original: 日 *ri*</note> means "solidity" or "fullness," while the word for **Moon** <note>original: 月 *yue*</note> means "deficiency" or "waning." The moon has its times of being incomplete, but the sun is perpetually solid and full. This is its nature. Just as there are slight variances in the movement of the heavens, the movements of the moon and stars are slower and cannot keep pace with the sun; only the sun remains "iron-clad" and fixed in its regularity. It is also said that by observing the **Big Dipper** <note>original: 北斗 *beidou*</note>, one can perceive the rotation of the heavens.

<gloss>The following discusses the correspondence of time</gloss>

**Fate** <note>literally "numbers" or "calculations"</note> is simply a way of calculating the seasonal nodes of the **Vital Energy** <term>Qi: the fundamental life-force and material substance of the universe</term>. Broadly speaking, there is only one universal Energy. As the forces of **Light and Darkness** <term>Yin and Yang</term> spread, they manifest as the **Five Elements** <term>Wuxing: Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term>.

Within the Five Elements, each possesses its own Yin and Yang aspects. The characters *Jia* and *Yi* <note>the first two Celestial Stems</note> belong to Wood; *Bing* and *Ding* <note>the third and fourth stems</note> belong to Fire. Spring is categorized as Wood, and Summer is categorized as Fire. Whether it be the year, month, day, or hour, there is nothing that is not governed by the Energy of the Five Elements. The stems *Jia, Yi, Bing,* and *Ding* are further classified into Yin and Yang; they are simply the manifestations of the "Two" (Yin and Yang) and the "Five" (Elements) Energies. When a human being is born, they happen to encounter this specific Energy. Some receive a "clear" portion of it, while others receive a "turbid" portion. Whether one is noble or lowly, long-lived or short-lived, it is all determined by this. Thus, there are irregularities and differences among people, just as we see in the natural world.

**Key Terms:**
**Yin and Yang**; **Five Elements**; **The Celestial Stems** <note>original: 甲乙丙丁</note>; **The Big Dipper**; **Vital Energy** <note>Qi</note>; **Seasonal Nodes**.

<summary>The author explains the etymological connection between the sun's movement and the concept of time, while arguing that human destiny (nobility, health, and lifespan) is determined by the quality of Qi—the universal energy—present at the moment of birth.</summary>
