# LIGHT AND DARKNESS, ETC.

...rated **Fire**; Heaven, the **Triad** <note>the number three</note>, generated **Wood**; the Earth, **Tetrad** <note>the number four</note>, generated **Metal**. The **Monad** <note>the number one</note> and **Triad** are **Light** <note>Yang energy</note>, the **Duad** <note>the number two</note> and **Tetrad** are **Darkness** <note>Yin energy</note>.

47. Being asked: Speaking of the order of their motion, with regard to their **Air** <note>original: 氣 *qi*, the vital energy or material force</note>; then, they are **Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water**; and, **Wood and Fire** are **Light**, **Metal and Water** are **Darkness**; is not this speaking of them with regard to their revolutions? Yet, how can **Wood and Fire** be called **Light**, and **Metal and Water Darkness**? 

He replied: This refers to the **Four Seasons**; the **Spring and Summer** are **Light**, **Autumn and Winter** are **Darkness**.

48. He asked his pupils how the ancients arranged the **twelve hours**? They could not explain this. 

The teacher said: The character **Che** <note>original: 志 *zhi*, meaning "will" or "intent"</note> is formed of **Che** <note>original: 之 *zhi*, meaning "to go"</note> and **Sin** <note>original: 心 *xin*, meaning "heart"</note>, and it means what the mind does (i.e., reflects). The ancient character **She** <note>original: 時 *shi*, meaning "time"</note>, was formed from **Che** <note>original: 之 *zhi*, "to go" or "to reach"</note> and **Jih** <note>original: 日 *ri*, "sun"</note>, and is what days limit. 

For, when the day arrives at **Woo** <note>the seventh earthly branch, associated with the horse</note>, it is called the **Woo time** <note>Noon, 11 AM – 1 PM</note>; and when it arrives at **Wei** <note>the eighth earthly branch, associated with the sheep</note>, it is called the **Wei time** <note>1 PM – 3 PM</note>; all the twelve hours are to be explained thus. 

The ancients explain the character **Jih** (Sun) as "**solid**," and **Yuĕ** (Moon) <note>original: 月 *yue*</note> as "**broken off**;" the Moon at times being deficient, but the Sun being always full. The case is so. Thus Heaven's revolutions have also irregularity; the Moon and Stars also, are slow and cannot overtake it; but the Sun is, as it were, fixed like iron. 

He further said: Look at **Ursa Major** <note>the Big Dipper</note>, and then you can perceive the revolutions of Heaven.

49. **Number** is merely the calculation of the times and seasons of the **Air**. Speaking generally this is merely one **Air**; the **Light and Darkness** scatter and become the **Five Elements**, and each of these **Five Elements** has the **Light and Darkness** inherent in it. **Këa-yih** <note>original: 甲乙 *jia-yi*, the first two Heavenly Stems</note> is **Wood**, **Ping-ting** <note>original: 丙丁 *bing-ding*, the third and fourth Heavenly Stems</note> is **Fire**: **Spring** belongs to **Wood**, and **Summer**...

<page-num>122</page-num>

# THE GENERATION OF THE FIVE ELEMENTS

**Yang** transforms and **Yin** combines <note>original: 陽變陰合 *yang bian yin he*</note>, thereby generating **Water, Fire, Wood, Metal, and Earth**. **Light** <note>Yang</note> and **Darkness** <note>Yin</note> are the **Vital Energy** <note>original: 氣 *qi*, the breath or material force of the universe</note>; they generate the physical substance of these **Five Elements** <note>original: 五行 *wuxing*, also translated as the Five Phases</note>. When Heaven and Earth create living things, the Five Elements are the very first to appear. 

The Earth itself is composed of **Soil** <note>original: 土 *tu*, the element of Earth</note>; this soil contains within it many varieties of things such as metal and wood. Between Heaven and Earth, what matter exists that is not made of the Five Elements? The Five Elements along with the two principles of Yin and Yang—these seven things churning and mixing together—are the fundamental materials for creating all life.

The Five Elements are distributed in a natural sequence, and the **Four Seasons** proceed accordingly. **Metal, Wood, Water, and Fire** are divided among **Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter**. The element of **Earth**, however, is "lodged" <note>original: 寄旺 *jiwang*, meaning it flourishes or resides temporarily</note> within all four seasons. 

For example, Spring belongs to the element of **Wood**, but twelve days after the **Pure Brightness Festival** <note>original: 清明 *Qingming*, a solar term in the traditional calendar occurring in early April</note>, that is the time when the energy of the Earth flourishes. In each of the four seasons, the Earth energy flourishes for eighteen days, totaling seventy-two days across the year. It is specifically during the eighteen days at the end of the Summer season that the Earth’s energy is at its absolute strongest; it is for this reason that it has the power to generate the **Metal** of the Autumn season.

If one examines the traditional diagrams of these principles, relationships such as **Wood generating Fire** or **Metal generating Water** are shown with small lines connecting them to one another. However, **Fire generating Earth** and **Earth generating Metal** are unique, as the lines pass directly through the center of the Earth <note>referring to Earth’s position at the center of the five-point cosmological compass</note>. The others simply pass by the sides, as can be clearly seen. <gloss>The three passages above are from the *Classified Conversations* <note>original: 語類 *Yulei*, referring to the *Zhuzi Yulei* or the "Classified Conversations of Master Zhu [Xi]," a major Neo-Confucian philosopher</note></gloss>

<summary>This page explains the Neo-Confucian cosmology of how the interaction of Yin and Yang produces the Five Elements, detailing how the Earth element acts as a transitional force between the four seasons.</summary>

<page-num>123</page-num>

# LIGHT AND DARKNESS, AND THE ELEMENTS

...to Fire. Regarding Years, Months, Days, and Hours, not one of these is devoid of the **Vital Energy** <note>original: 氣 *qi*, the fundamental breath or force of the universe</note> of the **Five Elements** <note>original: 五行 *wuxing*, the phases of Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</note>. The **Wood Stems** <note>original: 甲乙 *jia-yi*, the first two of the Ten Heavenly Stems</note> and the **Fire Stems** <note>original: 丙丁 *ping-ting*, the third and fourth Heavenly Stems</note> belong both to the **Light** <note>Yang</note> and the **Darkness** <note>Yin</note>; that is to say, they belong to the Vital Energy of the Two Principles and the Five Elements.* When people are born, they receive this Vital Energy—some a pure portion, and some a coarse one. Whether a person is honorable or mean, old or young, all are formed this way. This is why irregularities and differences in human nature and destiny arise.

50. The Light <note>Yang</note> transforms and the Darkness <note>Yin</note> pairs with it, and together they generate **Water, Fire, Wood, Metal, and Earth**. The combined energy of Light and Darkness generates the physical substance of these Five Elements. When Heaven and Earth create all things, the Five Elements are the very first to be obtained. The Earth element is simply soil, which contains within it every variety of metal and wood. In the expanse between Heaven and Earth, what single thing exists that does not contain the Five Elements? The Five Elements, along with the Light and the Darkness, are seven forces revolving together, and they are the fundamental materials from which all things are created. 

The Five Elements spread out in a natural order, and the **Four Seasons** rotate. Metal, Wood, Water, and Fire belong respectively to **Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter**, while Earth is inherent in and supports each of the four seasons. For example, Spring belongs to the element of Wood, but twelve days after the **Pure Brightness Festival** <note>original: 清明 *Tsing-ming* or *Qingming*, a traditional solar term in early April</note> is the time when the Earth element resides in and strengthens the season. Each season has eighteen days where it is supported and adorned by the Earth element—seventy-two days in total across the year. However, during the eighteen days of the Summer season, the Earth’s Vital Energy is at its most brilliant, and because of this, it is able to generate the Metal element of Autumn.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical comparison between Chinese Neo-Confucianism and Classical Western thought, specifically focusing on the concept of a supreme deity or "Divine Reason" inherent in matter. The author draws parallels between Zhu Xi's "Fate" or "Principle" and the Stoic or Platonic conceptions of God.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

# NOTES.

The Confucianists are Monotheists, while, at the same time, they are Pantheists, as their one God (Spirit) <note>original: 神 *shén*</note> pervades the entire Universe and all its parts, and is the soul of each.

All the Pagan philosophers made God (God <note>original Greek: Θεος, *Theos*</note>, God <note>original Latin: *Deus*</note>) inherent in Eternal Matter—the Great Origin of all things; so that "God penetrates, pervades, and animates matter," and is "the Divine Reason¹ inherent in the whole universe and all its parts." Seneca <note>A Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says, "You mention Nature,² Fate, Fortune; names of this kind are all names of God <note>original Latin: *Dei*</note> variously employing his power." "The Air of Anaximenes <note>An ancient Greek Pre-Socratic philosopher</note> is a subtle ether animated with a Divine principle, whence it becomes the origin of all beings."³ "When Anaximenes speaks of Air, as when Thales <note>The first recognized Greek philosopher</note> speaks of Water, we must not understand these elements as they appear in this or that determined form on Earth, but as Water and Air pregnant with vital energy, and capable of infinite transmutations."⁴

In this "Fate" <note>likely referring to the Chinese concept of *Mìng* (Decree) or *Lǐ* (Principle)</note> of <term>Choo-tsze</term> <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential Neo-Confucian scholar who synthesized fundamental Chinese philosophical concepts</note> then, we have the God (God <note>original Greek: Θεος, *Theos*</note>, God <note>original Latin: *Deus*</note>) pre-eminently <note>original Greek: κατ' ἐξοχὴν, *kat' exochen*</note> (the Supreme God <note>original: 至神, *zhì shén*</note>) of the Western philosophers, and the God pre-eminently (the Supreme God) of the *Yih King* <note>The I Ching or Book of Changes</note>.

(2, 3.) As Choo-tsze here states that the eternal Primordial Matter was generated by Fate or God, so also in the West, Matter, although considered to be eternal, was yet held by many to have been generated by the Deity. It is stated, for instance, in the *Timaeus* (Ch. ix.) that the Universe was generated by its "Creator and Father;" and Plato is supposed to have followed Hermes and the Egyptians in thus holding that "the matter of all things emanated from the Deity or divine nature itself."⁵

The title "Nature" <note>original: 性 *xìng*</note> here given to Fate or God, sometimes includes the Primordial Air <note>original: 氣 *qì*, the vital energy or material force</note> in which He is always inherent; "Nature is Fate;" "when we speak of Nature, we include the Air."⁶ Yet, Choo-tsze warns his disciples not to confound these two together, for this designation is only given to the Primordial Air in consequence of the Better Nature <note>original Latin: *Melior Natura*</note>, or God, who is inherent in it; he says, ...... "Yet if we always consider the Air to be Nature, and Nature to be the Air, we do not clearly understand the subject."⁷

The "Nature" of the Confucianists therefore is precisely the "Nature" of the Stoics; and both schools designate the Divine Principle "Reason" <note>original: 理 *lǐ*</note>. "The Stoics divide nature into two parts; one, that which works; the other, that which offers itself to be wrought upon. In the former is the power of acting, in the latter is simple matter, nor is one able to do any thing without the other. Thus under one term of nature, they comprehend two things very diverse, God and the world, the artificer and the work, and they say that one cannot do without the other, as if nature were God mixed up with the world. For sometimes they so confound things, that God becomes the very soul of the world, and the world the body of God."⁸ "What is nature," says Seneca, "but God the Divine Reason inherent in the whole universe and in all its parts? Or, you may call him if you please, the Author of all things."⁹

(4. 5.) Choo-tsze having explained to his pupils that the Great Origin of all things is Fate or God (Spirit <note>original: 神 *shén*</note>) inherent in the Primordial Matter, or the Infinite, Eternal, mass of Air <note>Qi</note> from which all things are generated; one of those present propounds a question—

2. See par. 2, 3, and 24. Also, Cudworth's Intellectual System Vol I. p. 249 note.

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Neo-Confucian metaphysics and Western classical philosophy, specifically focusing on the relationship between Principle (Fate/God) and Matter, and the four stages of cosmic movement as defined in the Book of Changes.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>

# NOTES.

to the Philosopher, as to which of these the priority in point of time is to be assigned. To this question there are two replies given by <term>Choo-tsze</term> <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential Neo-Confucian scholar who synthesized fundamental Chinese philosophical concepts</note>, viz: 1st. That although we must not consider the one to be prior to the other in point of time, both being equally eternal, yet Fate or God <note>The author uses "Fate" and "God" to translate the Chinese concept of *Lǐ* (Principle) or *Mìng* (Decree), the immaterial governing law</note> being Incorporeal has in this respect priority over the Matter in which He is inherent, and which is a Corporeal and Material mass; and 2nd. When we treat of the origin of these two, Fate or God has the decided priority, being self-existent, while the Primordial Matter is generated by or emanates from Him. "Fate generated the Air." <note>original Chinese: 命生氣 *mìng shēng qì*—referring to Principle generating the vital energy or *qì*</note>¹

Hence while these two are eternally united together, and can never exist separately from each other, they are nevertheless totally distinct Principles; and thus, although God is not superior to Matter in *time*, He most decidedly is so by *nature*. This was precisely the doctrine held by Probus, Simplicius, and all the latter Platonists.²

(6.) This Great Origin of all things, or God inherent in Matter, is expressed in numbers, in the <term>Yih King</term> <note>The *I Ching* or Book of Changes</note>, by **1** or Monad. The inherent Fate or God is an Indivisible Unity, and the Infinite Primordial Air in which this Principle is inherent, is the material portion of Unity, that is to say Monad <note>original: 太一 *Tàiyī*, often translated as the "Great One"</note> which divides into the several parts of the Universe. Hence the arranged Kosmos regarded as one whole, is designated Fate or God, and God is inherent in all its parts, even down to the meanest insect. Hence also, Man's soul or Principle of life is Fate, or God <note>original: 神 *shén*</note> Himself.³

In the Orphic verses "the whole world is represented as one great animal, God being the soul thereof."⁴

The Stoics used the designation "Fate" as one of the names of God Himself.⁵ Also, according to these latter Philosophers, "Since the active principle of nature is comprehended within the world, and with matter makes one whole, it necessarily follows that God penetrates, pervades, and animates matter, and the things which are formed from it; or, in other words, that he is the soul of the Universe." They further held that the soul of Man is a portion of the Divinity inherent in the Universe, which they regarded as "one Great Whole."⁶

(7.) The eternity of Motion here propounded is a well known tenet in Philosophy. Aristotle held the Motion of the first sphere or <term>Primum Mobile</term> <note>The "First Mover," the outermost sphere in the geocentric model of the universe</note>, to be "without beginning, middle or end."⁷

In order to understand the statements made in this paragraph, we must refer to the first sentence in the Yih King to which Classic Choo-tsze constantly refers as his authority for the doctrines which he inculcates. The Universe moves round in a never ending circle, and the Yih King⁸ divides this <term>Khëen</term> <note>original: 乾 *Qián*, the "Creative" or "Heaven" hexagram</note> or animated Kosmos into four parts, thus:

->**The Origin**<-
元
(Yuan)

->**Integrity** 貞 (Zhen) | [Circle] | 亨 **Prosperity** (Heng)<-

->**Benefit**<-
利
(Li)

A large circle is flanked by four Chinese characters at the cardinal points: 元 (Origin) at the top, 利 (Benefit/Harvest) at the bottom, 貞 (Integrity/Firmness) at the left, and 亨 (Prosperity/Permeance) at the right.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Neo-Confucian metaphysics, specifically how the "Great Origin" or "God" (Lǐ) interacts with "Primordial Air" (Qì) to create a cyclical universe. It follows the previous page’s introduction of the Monad and the inherent unity of the cosmos.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>

# NOTES.

This Circle represents the perpetual course in which the <term>Kosmos</term> <note>The universe conceived as an ordered, harmonious system</note> revolves. At <term>Yuen</term> <note>Yuan: "Origin," the beginning stage of the cosmic cycle associated with Spring</note> all things come forth, as for instance in the Spring season of the year; and at <term>Ching</term> <note>Zhen: "Steadfastness" or "Correction," the final stage of the cycle associated with Winter</note> they decay, as in Winter, only, however, to break forth into renewed life on again reaching the point <term>Yuen</term>. Hence <term>Choo-tsze</term> <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the preeminent Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> holds that "there is no termination to the present state of things," the eternal Universe and all its parts perpetually moving in an endless circle. From <term>Yuen</term> to the termination of <term>Hăng</term> <note>Heng: "Pervasion" or "Growth," the stage associated with Summer</note> at <term>Le</term> <note>Li: "Utility" or "Harvest," the stage associated with Autumn</note>, is the place of Motion in which things spring forth, grow, and flourish; and from <term>Le</term> to the termination of <term>Ching</term> at <term>Yuen</term>, is the place of Rest or *force of inactivity* <note>original Latin: vis inertiæ</note>, in which things decay and die. Hence all Motion is preceded by Rest and Rest is always followed by Motion in a perpetual circle, so that Motion and Rest are both eternal. The "opening and shutting" of the <term>Kosmos</term> refers to the Greater Revolutions of the Universe, which are compared in the <term>Yih King</term> <note>The I Ching, or Book of Changes, an ancient Chinese divination manual and philosophical text</note> to the opening and shutting of a door. This doctrine of the endless succession of similar worlds will come under notice in the division of this chapter which treats of Heaven and Earth. The designation "Great Extreme" <note>Taiji: the "Supreme Ultimate" or the source of all existence</note> is treated of in the third division, and the Light and Darkness into which the Primordial Air <note>Qi: the vital energy or "breath" that makes up all physical things</note> divides, in the fourth and last division.

(8.) Man, as well as the other parts of the <term>Kosmos</term>, is formed from the Primordial Air, and the inherent Fate or God <note>The author uses "Fate" or "God" to translate the Chinese term *Shén* (神), meaning spirit or divine intelligence</note> then becomes his soul as well as the soul or animating Principle of every other portion of the One Great Whole. One of the Philosopher's pupils now asks, where was this Fate or God before Man was generated as a body in which He could exist and act? <term>Choo-tsze</term> replies that this animating Principle of the Universe existed before any portion of it was, as it were, decerpt <note>An archaic term meaning "plucked away" or "separated from"</note> in order to form the soul in Man; just as the sea exists before any water is taken out of it. As each thing comes into existence, it receives a portion of this Divinity as its animating Principle; and it is the same Principle which is inherent in each. Yet, adds <term>Choo-tsze</term>, God (the Spirit) or the Divinity within me, is the Host while I am but his guest, being only united with him for a time; for, He is Eternal and Self-existent, while my body in which He dwells is mortal, and therefore subject to decay.

(9.) The <term>Kosmos</term> in which Fate or God is inherent, like the body in Man, is subject to decay. It is alternately in a Chaotic, and in an arranged state. <term>Choo-tsze</term> is now asked how Fate or God (the Spirit), being invisible, manifests His presence in the Primordial Air; to which he replies, that the chief portions of the Universe are preserved by Him from utter destruction while in Chaos, so that in due time a new <term>Kosmos</term> springs forth from the materials of the old one. Hence the <term>Phœnix</term> is used to represent the succession of worlds. That this tenet of an animated Universe is not confined to the Philosophers in China, is plain from the common phrase "Living Heaven and Living Earth" <note>original Chinese: 活天活地 (huó tiān huó dì)</note>, used every where throughout the Empire, and by all classes.

Zeno <note>The founder of Stoicism</note> also held in common with all the Philosophers of antiquity that the Universe is "a living and sentient substance" <note>original Greek: οὐσία ἔμψυχος καὶ αἰσθητική</note>.¹ "All the theogonies make an eternal chaos the origin of all things."² This eternal Chaos is merely a Man, and thus every Pagan System commences from the First Man in his Mundane or deified character, whose soul is the Deity Himself, "Anterior to all of them (Gods, Goddesses and Demi-gods) the primordial matter *or person*, was chaos."³ And Chinese history states that: "It is also handed down from antiquity, that the first to appear

<summary>This page explains the Neo-Confucian concept of the universe as a perpetual, cyclical machine driven by the Four Virtues (Origin, Growth, Harvest, and Steadfastness). It also describes the human soul as a temporary "guest" of the eternal divine principle (Shen), drawing parallels with Stoic and Platonic views of a sentient, animated universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Neo-Confucian cosmology, specifically exploring the relationship between the material "Primordial Air" (Qì) and the spiritual "God" or "Fate" (Shén). It builds on the previous page's discussion of the cyclical nature of the universe by identifying these forces with figures from Western classical philosophy, such as the Demiurge and the gods of Anaximenes.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>

-># NOTES.<-

(10.) *<term>Shang-te</term>* <note>Shangdi: "The Lord on High," a supreme deity in ancient Chinese religion, here interpreted as the physical heavens</note> or <term>Heaven</term>, the animated <term>Primordial Air</term> <note>Qì: The vital energy or material substance from which all things are formed</note> is the second <term>God</term> or the <term>Demiurgus</term> <note>In Platonic philosophy, a "craftsman" or secondary deity who shapes the physical world from pre-existing matter</note>, who grows up, as it were, from <term>Chaos</term> into the fully arranged <term>Kosmos</term> <note>The universe conceived as a harmonious, ordered system</note>. The Hermaic books <note>Texts attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, blending Greek and Egyptian philosophy</note> state that "The world is a second God." "God is the father of the world, and the world is the son of God." "This whole world is a great God and the image of a greater."¹

(11, 12, 13.) <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> <note>Shén: Spirit, divinity, or the intelligent principle within nature</note> is superior to the Infinite <term>Primordial Air</term> by nature, but is not prior *in time*, both being equally eternal. But, the very existence of the whole <term>Kosmos</term> and each part of it, entirely depends upon the inherent <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> (Spirit). The <term>Primordial Air</term>, which is <term>Heaven</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>*, receives his animation and power to act as the <term>Demiurge</term>, in generating, nourishing and pervading all things, from the supreme <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> (Spirit) who is his animating soul, and without which he could have no existence. <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> is the only "Incorporeal substance" in the Universe and both He and the <term>Primordial Air</term> which he surrounds and also pervades, are equally Infinite and Eternal. <term>God</term> (Spirit) is Infinite as to space, and <term>Heaven</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>* is Infinite as to his parts or portions into which he divides.

(14, 15.) "The <term>Great Framer</term>" is a title of the <term>Demiurgus Heaven</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>*, from whose ethereal substance all things are made, and in which each is again absorbed at death, as in the case of the Western <term>Kronos</term> <note>In Greek myth, the titan of time who devoured his children; often equated with the Roman Saturn</note> or "devouring <term>Jupiter</term>."

(16, 17.) When <term>Heaven</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>* breathes forth his Air, then all things exist and flourish; but when he draws in his breath they all die being deprived of their animating principle. This *<term>Shang-te</term>*, however, so frequently mentioned in the Classics, is not a personal being dwelling in the heavens, but is <term>Heaven</term> itself animated by an inherent soul called <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> who is the real source of all happiness and misery. *<term>Shang-te</term>* or <term>Heaven</term> is merely the revolving <term>Primordial Air</term>, or the <term>Kosmos</term>, which is sometimes arranged, and sometimes in a state of <term>Chaos</term>, when all things return to his bosom. <term>Chaos</term> or "decline," and the arranged <term>Kosmos</term> or "fulness," follow each other to infinity <note>original Latin: ad infinitum</note>.

(18.) <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> (Spirit) being eternally united with the <term>Primordial Air</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>*, acts through the latter, who is therefore merely the <term>Demiurgic Framer</term> and Ruler of the <term>Kosmos</term>, and could not exist without this soul which dwells within him.

(19.) The Seasons in this system are not mere states of the atmosphere, but are animated beings, portions of the one <term>Primordial Air</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>*, and hence they are sacrificed to. They are each animated by the one <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> (Spirit) which pervades all things. Hence these Gods are precisely such as the Gods of <term>Anaximenes</term> <note>A Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who argued that "Air" was the primary substance of the universe</note> were, for, "Anaximenes made infinite Air to be the first original and cause of all things; and yet was he not therefore silent concerning the gods, much less did he deny them; nevertheless he did not believe the air to have been made by the gods, but the gods to have been all generated out of the air."² These "Four Airs" or portions of <term>Heaven</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>*, <term>Confucius</term> thus describes: "The lofty azure vault is <term>Heaven</term> (that is <note>original Latin: i. e.</note>, *<term>Shang-te</term>*); the Spring is Azure Heaven <note>original Chinese: 蒼天 (Cāngtiān)</note> (the Lord on High); the summer is Vast Heaven <note>original: 昊天 (Hàotiān)</note> (the Lord on High); the Autumn is Merciful Heaven <note>original: 旻天 (Míntiān)</note> (the Lord on High); and the Winter is High Heaven <note>original: 上天 (Shàngtiān)</note> (the Lord on High).³ Hence *<term>Shang-te</term>* or <term>Heaven</term>, like <term>Jupiter</term> or <term>Heaven</term>, is both the father and ruler of the Gods, as well as of Men.

(20.) In this paragraph <term>Choo-tsze</term> <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the preeminent Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> refers to the numbers of the <term>Yih King</term> <note>The I Ching, or Book of Changes</note>.⁴ The <term>Primordial Air</term> which is <term>Heaven</term> or *<term>Shang-te</term>*, is "The Great <term>Monad</term>" <note>original: 太一 (Tàiyī); the "Great One" or supreme metaphysical unity</note> or

***

It uses comparisons to Greek philosophy and ancient Chinese dictionaries to argue that the heavens and the seasons are animated, semi-divine parts of a single eternal cycle.</summary>

It builds on the previous page's discussion of the cyclical nature of the universe by identifying these forces with figures from Western classical philosophy, such as the Demiurge and the gods of Anaximenes.</meta>

-># NOTES.<-

number 1, who, like his counterpart the <term>Monad</term> <note>From the Greek word for "unit," referring in philosophy to the "One" or the ultimate, indivisible source of all things.</note> Jupiter, generates the world from his own substance, by his own gyrations; *e. gr.* <note>exempli gratia: for example</note> "Hence Rites and Ceremonies derive their origin from the Great Monad (i.e. *<term>Shang-te</term>* <note>Shangdi: The "Lord on High" or the Supreme Ruler.</note>), who dividing became Heaven and Earth; gyrating, became Light and Darkness; transmuting, became the Four Seasons; and separating orderly became the <term>Demon-gods</term> <note>original: 鬼神 (guǐshén). These refer to the spirits of the dead and the spiritual forces of nature.</note>,”^1 &c.

The Confucian Monad then, is the <term>Primordial Air</term> <note>Qì: The fundamental substance of the universe, often translated as vital energy or breath.</note> which is animated by the inherent <term>Fate or God</term> <note>Shén: The spiritual or divine intelligence within nature.</note> (神), who, although He pervades all things is yet an Indivisible Unity;^2 and hence the numbers of the *<term>Yih King</term>* <note>Yijing: The Book of Changes, an ancient divination text centered on cosmic patterns.</note> are animated Beings. <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>An ancient Greek philosopher who famously taught that the universe is structured according to mathematical numbers.</note> also held that “Numbers were Beings:” he believed that “numbers were things in reality, not merely in symbol:” and, <term>Aristotle</term> <note>The famous Greek philosopher and student of Plato who laid the foundations for Western logic and science.</note> states that “the Pythagoreans did not separate Numbers from Things. They held Number to be the Principle and Material of things, no less than their essence and power.” “The primary Being according to <term>Anaximander</term> <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed that the "Apeiron" (the Boundless) was the source of all things.</note> is unquestionably a Unity. It is One yet All. It comprises within itself the multiplicity of elements from which all mundane things are composed; and these elements only need to be separated from it to appear as separate phenomena of nature. Creation is the decomposition of the Infinite. How does this decomposition originate? By the eternal motion which is the condition of the Infinite,”^3 &c.

As all the inferior Gods are parts of *Shang-te* or the Primordial Air, they are all equally *Shang-te*; *e. gr.* <note>original Chinese: 上帝即天也聚天之神而言之則謂之上帝</note> “*Shang-te* is Heaven; all the Gods of Heaven collectively are *Shang-te*.”^4 That is, Heaven or *Shang-te* is “one God yet all Gods,” and like his counterpart Heaven or Jupiter, might thus address the other deities:—

->“Heaven-dwellers, Gods, who are my limbs; whom our power,<-
->divided into different offices, creates.”<-^5 <note>original Latin: "Cœlicolæ, mea membra, Dei; quos nostra potestas / Officiis divisa facit."</note>

Hence *Shang-te* is a mere compound of creatures worshipped as a Creator.

(21.) Man, as well as the <term>Kosmos</term> <note>The universe conceived as a perfectly ordered system.</note> or *Shang-te*, is made from the Primordial Air ; and the Air within a Man, and which constitutes his twofold soul, is precisely the same as that without him. Thus the Confucianists seem to have gone through the same process of reasoning as the Philosopher <term>Anaximenes</term> <note>An ancient Greek philosopher who believed that "Air" was the primary substance of the universe.</note>, whose ideas are thus represented by Lewes :^6 “His life he believed to be Air. Was there not also without him no less than within him, an ever-moving, ever-present, invisible Air? The Air which was within him, and which he called Life, was it not a part of the Air which was without? And if so, was not this Air the Beginning of things? He looked around him and thought his conjecture was confirmed. The Air seemed universal. The Earth was as a broad leaf resting upon it. All things were produced from it; all things were resolved into it. When he breathed, he drew in a part of the universal life. All things were nourished by Air, as he was nourished by it.” <term>Diogenes of Apollonia</term> <note>A later Greek philosopher who further developed the idea that the cosmic Air was intelligent and divine.</note> held that the Air is “a soul, and therefore it is living and intelligent.”^7 “Form is the habitation of Life, <term>Air</term> <note>Qì: The material breath or spirit.</note> (氣) is the Origin of Life.”^8

(22.) The Infinite Primordial Air is an Intelligent Being or Animal, which, in virtue of it's union with the Eternal Fate or God (神), is endowed with the powers of understanding and sensation. These powers are conferred upon the spiritual portion

<summary>This page correlates the Neo-Confucian concept of the "Great Monad" (Shangdi) and "Primordial Air" (Qì) with ancient Greek philosophical traditions, including the teachings of Pythagoras, Anaximander, and Anaximenes. The author argues that in this system, God and the universe are viewed as a single, animated, and divisible substance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Neo-Confucian cosmology, drawing parallels between the Chinese concepts of the "Primordial Air" (Qi) and "God/Fate" (Shen) and the "Mind" (Nous) or "Demiurge" of Western Classical philosophy. It explores the idea of the universe as a "Great Man" (Macrocosm) and the human being as a "Little Heaven" (Microcosm).</meta>

<page-num>132</page-num>

-># NOTES.<-

of the Air, that is to say, the most subtle Ether. Thus the Primordial Air, or Heaven, or <term>Shang-te</term> <note>Shangdi: The "Supreme Ruler" or God in Chinese tradition.</note>, is a compound Being or living creature <note>The author uses the word "Animal" in the classical sense of an animated or soul-possessing being.</note>; the visible Heaven (including the Earth) being his body <note>original Chinese: 體 (tǐ)</note> and the subtle Ether being his rational soul, which is made to be a rational soul by the inherent Fate or Reason or God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note>; and hence this soul is called the Spirit of Heaven <note>original Chinese: 天 之 神 (Tiān zhī Shén)</note>, the God (or rational soul) of Heaven or *Shang-te*. All the Confucianist Gods, in fact, are souls. The usual designation of this subtle Ether or rational soul is “Mind,” the Mind <note>original Greek: Νοῦς (Nous) and Latin: Mens</note> of the Western philosophers. It is plain then—1. That Chaos consists of Mind inherent in grosser Matter; and 2. That this Mind or <term>Demiurgus</term> <note>The "craftsman" or creator-god in Platonic philosophy who shapes the material world.</note> is only the second God. “If there were no Air, then Fate would not have any thing to rest upon.”¹ This “Air,” <term>Choo-tsze</term> <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200): The most influential Neo-Confucian philosopher of the Song Dynasty.</note> also designates as “Mind” <note>original Chinese: 心 (Xīn)</note>; “If there were no Mind, then Fate would not have any thing to rest upon.”² This is the “Mind, the King of all things” <note>original Greek: Νοῦς πάντων βασιλεύς (Nous pantōn basileus)</note> of Plato; or his “one archetypal Mind, the Demiurge;” and this mind he held to be eternal yet generated by the First God whom he calls “the Good” <note>original Greek: τ' αγαθόν (to agathon)</note>.³ “Mind,” (i. e. Jupiter), “says <term>Macrobius</term> <note>A 5th-century Roman philosopher known for his commentary on Cicero.</note> was begotten from that God who is truly supreme.”⁴ <term>Archytas</term> <note>A Greek philosopher and contemporary of Plato who belonged to the Pythagorean school.</note> in his book of principles says that “There must be something better than Mind; and this thing better than Mind, is that which we (properly) call God.”⁵

(23, 24.) Man and <term>Shang-te</term> or the <term>Kosmos</term> <note>The universe conceived as a harmonious and orderly system.</note> are precisely the same, both being made from exactly the same Principles. Hence Heaven or <term>Shang-te</term> is merely “a Great Man,” and Man is “a little Heaven” or <term>Shang-te</term>, or <term>Microcosm</term> <note>From the Greek for "little world," the idea that a human being mirrors the structure of the universe.</note>. Hence also the soul in both is the same, namely, Incorporeal Reason or God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note>. And as in the World, so also in Man, this First God unites with his “Mind” and makes it to be a rational soul; and hence the latter is, in the Classics styled God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note>, or the Divinity within us.

“Man, according to the <term>Stoics</term> <note>Followers of a school of Greek philosophy that taught that the universe is governed by divine reason (Logos) and that humans should live in harmony with nature.</note>, is an image of the world; one whole composed of body and Mind. The mind of man is a spark of that Divine fire, which is the soul of the world. That Eternal Reason by which all nature is animated, and which by its productive power, communicates essential qualities to every thing that exists, impressed the forms, qualities, and powers of man, upon certain portions of matter. The soul of man being a portion of the Deity, is then of the *same nature*; a subtle fiery substance, endued with intelligence and reason; but the *energy* of this principle is confined and constrained in the birth of man, by its union with grosser matter.”⁶

(25.) The “Fate” of each thing existing first, before the thing itself has any substantial form, refers to what <term>Choo-tsze</term> states in paragraph 10, where he defines Fate or God to be the <term>Great Vacuum</term> <note>The Tàixū or Great Void, representing the state of the universe before physical forms arise.</note> which surrounds the <term>Kosmos</term>. In this Fate or Vacuum are contained all the Forms <note>original Chinese: 象 (Xiàng)</note> or Ideas <note>original Chinese: 意 想 (Yìxiǎng), according to the Kangxi dictionary</note> of things before they obtain form by union with a portion of the Primordial Air.

(26.) Fate or God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note>, who is the “Incorporeal Reason” of the <term>Yih King</term> <note>Yijing: The Book of Changes.</note>, must have an ethereal vehicle to rest in. Abstract Reason can do nothing of itself, no more than abstract Matter can. Join both together and Matter becomes animated and capable of action, while the inherent Divine Reason makes it to be a rational animal. Deprive Man’s soul of this Divine Reason or God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note> and it ceases to be an intelligent Mind; separate the Divine Reason from the soul and the former has no medium through which to act, for God can do nothing without Matter. This eternity of Matter was, in

full intelligence. Yet this Divine Principle, the soul of each alike, is one and the same. He resembles pure Light which may be dulled, and pure water which may be muddied.

(34.) Here we have another appellation, given in the <term>Yih King</term> <note>I Ching: The "Book of Changes," an ancient Chinese text used for divination and philosophical study.</note> to the Confucianist Fate or God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note> and which proves Him to be the **God par excellence** <note>original Greek: Θεος κατ' εξοχην</note> of Plato and other philosophers in the West, namely, "**The Good**" <note>original Greek: τ'αγαθον (t'agathon)</note>.¹

(35.) The twofold soul in Man and in the rest of creation, is <term>Air</term> <note>Qi: The vital breath or energy that constitutes the material universe.</note>; the "**Nature**" <note>original Chinese: 性 (Xìng): The innate essence of a being.</note> of each is the Divine Principle which pervades all alike. Hence when this Air, which is the soul, disperses at death, the being ceases to exist, the soul returning to the entire mass of primordial Air (or "**Heaven**") from which it originally came.

(36.) The Eternal Fate or God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note> is the <term>First Mover</term> <note>An Aristotelian concept: the primary cause of all motion in the universe that is not itself moved by anything else.</note>, and the Primordial Air, or Great Monad, or <term>Shang-te</term> <note>Shangdi: The "Supreme Ruler" or highest deity in ancient Chinese tradition.</note>, depends upon Him for the power of Motion and also the *force of inertia* <note>original Latin: vis inertiæ</note>. In this we have further proof that this "**Fate**" of the Confucianists is the "**Fate**" of the Stoics; that is to say, their Supreme God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén); original Greek: Θεος (Theos); original Latin: Deus</note>. "**The essence of the First Mover is different from that of corporeal substances; indivisible, because unity is perfect; immutable, because nothing can change itself, and eternal because motion itself is eternal. This power is an incorporeal Intelligence, happy in the contemplation of himself; the first cause of all motion, and in fine, the Being of beings, or God.**"²

### THE GREAT EXTREME.

The previous section treats of animated Chaos, or the Eternal, Infinite, Primordial Matter or Air as one dark mass, in which the Eternal, Self-existent Fate or God is inherent. In this section the Primordial Air is treated of as divided into two Airs, in order to the formation of the complete Kosmos <note>The universe seen as a well-ordered whole.</note>. These two portions are respectively designated <term>Yang</term> <note>original Chinese: 陽</note> or **Light**, the subtle **Ether** or **Mind**, the <term>male generative principle</term> <note>original Sanskrit: Linga</note> of the Hindus; and <term>Yin</term> <note>original Chinese: 陰</note> or **Darkness**, the grosser Air, and the <term>female generative principle</term> <note>original Sanskrit: Yoni</note> of the Hindus. These are the "**Two Forms**" <note>original: "Two E"; refers to the Liangyi, the two primary forces of the I Ching.</note> of the <term>Yih King</term> <note>I Ching: The Book of Changes.</note>, the former being designated <term>Khëen</term> <note>Qian: The hexagram representing the Creative or Heaven.</note> and the latter <term>Khwăn</term> <note>Kun: The hexagram representing the Receptive or Earth.</note>, in that *Classic*. The animating soul, which in Chaos is designated Fate, is now designated "**The great Extreme**" <note>Taiji: The "Supreme Ultimate" or the absolute principle of the universe.</note>. The following figures will assist the student to obtain a clear idea of the statements made in this section:

(Paragraphs 1, 2, 3, 4.) In the first paragraph <term>Zhu Xi</term> <note>original: Choo-tsze; a 12th-century philosopher whose interpretations defined Neo-Confucianism.</note> tells us that Fate or God is the *Great Extreme*, and in par. 3 that the Primordial Air is the Great Extreme; this designation therefore like that of "**Nature**," includes both the *Immaterial Principle* or God, and the *Eternal Matter* from which He makes the *Universe*. Hence as in the case of "**Nature**" we are distinctly told that the designation "**Great Extreme**" is only given to the *Matter* or *Kosmos* in consequence of the inherent Divine Principle or God; <term>for example</term> <note>original Latin: e. gr.</note> "(The Great Extreme) is *Khëen* and *Khwăn*, (Fig. 2. above) united in one. Because of its one God <note>original Chinese: 神, Shén; see Fig. 1.</note> it is called the Great Extreme; because of its two

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Neo-Confucian cosmology, focusing on the distinction between the primary spiritual principle (God or Fate) and the secondary material manifestation (the Universe or Shang-te). It draws heavy parallels between the philosophy of Zhu Xi (Choo-tsze) and Western thinkers like Cicero, Plato, and the Stoics.</meta>

# NOTE.

---

As the <term>Primordial Air</term> <note>Qi: The vital energy or material substance of the universe.</note> is generated by <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> <note>original: 神, shén</note> it follows that the <term>Kosmos</term> (or *Shang-te* <note>original: 上帝, shàng dì; the "Lord on High" or the personified universe.</note>) formed from that Air, is only the <term>second God</term> <note>original: 神, shén</note>; so also Cicero, speaking of the Universe, says, “Thus that eternal God, procreated this perfectly happy god <note>original Latin: *deum*</note>, the world.”¹ So also Plato.² The Kosmos Jupiter or Heaven, like the <term>Kosmos Shang-te</term> or Heaven, was Androgynous <note>Having both male and female characteristics.</note>.³ And, as the Confuciánists hold that their one God <note>original: 神, shén</note> or First God is part of a spherical Universe; so also did the Stoics, concerning their one God <note>original Greek: Θεος, Theos; or Latin: Deus</note> or First God.⁴

(5, 6, 7.) The designation “<term>Great Extreme</term>” <note>Taiji: The "Supreme Ultimate" or the absolute principle.</note> is given both to the Fate or God inherent in the twofold Primordial Matter, and also to the Matter itself divided into “<term>Incomprehensible</term>” <note>Likely referring to Wuji: The "Limitless" or "Infinite."</note> because, as a perfect <term>Unity</term> he is in both the Light and the Darkness, (including the entire series made from each), whole and entire, without division of substance.⁵ This First God is also designated “<term>Reason</term>” <note>original: 道, dào; The Way.</note> and “<term>Incorporeal Reason</term>” in the same Classic.

All things are generated by the animated <term>Primordial Air</term> or <term>Heaven</term> or <term>Shang-te</term>, by his constant gyrations; but he owes all his powers of Motion, etc., as well as his very existence to his inherent <term>Soul</term>, the <term>Immaterial Great Extreme</term> or <term>Fate</term> or <term>First God</term> <note>original: 神, shén</note>. In fact he would “turn upside down,” but for the presence of this preserving Power.

(8, 9.) Choo-tsze <note>Zhu Xi: The leading philosopher of the Neo-Confucian school (1130–1200 AD).</note>, in these paragraphs, explains the Confuciánist idea as to the eternity of Light and Darkness, Motion and Rest. The animated Heaven or material <term>Great Extreme</term> or <term>Shang-te</term>, perpetually revolves producing the Light and the Darkness, which are animated beings in virtue of the inherent <term>Deity</term>.

(10.) From <term>Chaos</term>, or <term>God</term> inherent in <term>Matter</term>, to the full arrangement of that Matter into the complete <term>Kosmos</term>, this animated Universe is to be regarded as One Whole Animal or Being, namely, the <term>Great Monad Shang-te</term> <note>original: 太一, tài yī; the "Great One."</note>, who, as a great Hermaphrodite, generates all things from his own substance (Air), and within himself.

(11.) In this paragraph we have another striking proof that the <term>First God</term> of the <term>Yih King</term> <note>I Ching: The Book of Changes.</note> is the same as the First God of the Western Philosophers. The <term>Great Extreme</term> or <term>Fate</term> or <term>God</term> is a perfect indivisible <term>Unity</term> which pervades all things without division of substance, just as the Moon is seen whole and entire in each river and canal without being divided into several Moons.⁶ As the designation “<term>The Great Extreme</term>” includes both Fate or God, and the Matter or Air in which he is inherent, one of Choo-tsze's disciples asks him, to which portion of the Great Origin of all things he refers when he speaks of the <term>Great Extreme</term> being an indivisible <term>Unity</term>—to Fate (or God), or to the <term>Primordial Matter</term> (or Air)? To which the Philosopher replies that his statement refers to Fate, or the <term>Immaterial Principle</term>, which, as we have seen, he himself calls <term>God</term> <note>original: 神, shén</note>.⁷ This is the <term>Supreme God</term> <note>original: 至神, zhì shén</note> or <term>First God of the Classics</term> and the title given to him, namely, “<term>The Adorner of all things</term>” is used by the Confuciánists to distinguish Him from the second god or <term>Shang-te</term> who is the twofold Air and is styled “<term>Demon-god</term>” <note>original: 鬼神, guǐ shén; spiritual beings or ancestral spirits often associated with the operations of nature.</note>. Hence Choo-tsze tells us when treating of the First God, that, “This God <note>original: 神, shén</note>

# NOTE.

---

the First God of Western Philosophers (God <note>original Greek: Θεὸς; Latin: Deus</note>); Melissus, for instance, affirmed that his “One Being” was Incorporeal, and he designated Him “Infinite <note>original Greek: ἄπειρον, apeiron</note> to signify his eternity and incorruptibility.”¹

This First God or Incorporeal <term>Great Extreme</term> <note>Taiji: The "Supreme Ultimate," viewed here as the ultimate immaterial reality.</note> is “Omnipresent, Incorporeal, and Infinite.” Motion and Rest are the properties of the <term>Primordial Air</term> <note>Qi: The material substance or life-force of the universe.</note> or <term>Shang-te</term> <note>Shangdi: The "Lord on High." The author interprets this as the "Demiurge" or a secondary creator-god who gives form to the world.</note>, the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>A Platonic term for a deity who fashions the physical world.</note>, and are produced by the power of the First God, but must not be confounded with Him. In paragraph 15 these two parts of the Great Extreme are carefully distinguished. The Active portion of the Primordial Air or *Shang-te*, possesses the power of Motion, bestowed upon it by the First God, who makes that subtle Ether His <term>commanding faculty</term> <note>original Greek: Hegemonikon; the guiding or governing part of the soul in Stoic philosophy.</note>; while *Shang-te's* inert or more material portion, his ethereal body in fact, possesses the <term>force of inertia</term> <note>original Latin: vis inertiæ.</note>. This moving and resting *Shang-te* or Air, however, must not be mistaken for the First God or Immaterial portion of the Great Extreme who confers these powers upon him. *Shang-te* is the Second God or animated <term>Kosmos</term> <note>The universe seen as a well-ordered whole.</note>, (body and soul) of the Western Philosophers—the “son” of the First God because generated by Him.

The 17th paragraph makes this distinction very clear. The designation “Great Extreme” includes God <note>original Chinese: 神, shén</note> and the arranged Kosmos in which He is inherent; the title “Nature” includes God <note>original Chinese: 神, shén</note> and the Primordial Air from which the Kosmos is formed; but, in neither case must the Matter be confounded with the <term>Better Nature</term> <note>original Latin: Melior Natura; a phrase from Ovid referring to a divine power that brings order to chaos.</note> inherent in it.

->**God (神)** inherent in **Air (氣)** is Chaos and “Nature” and “Heaven.”<-

->**God (神)** inherent in **the divided Air** is the arranged Kosmos and “The Great Extreme” and “Heaven.”<-

->**Darkness (陰)** or **Khwăn** and **Light (陽)** or **Khëen**<-
<note>Khwăn (Kun) and Khëen (Qian) are the fundamental hexagrams of the I Ching representing Earth/Receptive and Heaven/Creative.</note>

From the completed being “Heaven” or the Kosmos, Man receives his “Virtuous Nature,” which is the Deity Himself <note>original Chinese: 神, shén</note> who is the “Incorporeal Reason” <note>Likely referring to the Dao (The Way).</note> inherent in the Mind of the Kosmos or *Shang-te*, and also in the Mind of Man, the lesser *Shang-te* or <term>Microcosm</term> <note>A "little world"; the idea that a human being mirrors the structure of the entire universe.</note>; “making Mind to be Mind.”² The title “The Good” <note>original Greek: τ’αγαθον, t'agathon; used in the Yih King/I Ching</note> given to the First God or the “Incorporeal Reason,” has been already mentioned as the designation of the first divine <term>Hypostasis</term> <note>The underlying fundamental reality or personhood of a deity.</note> of Plato's theology.

(19.) The description given in this paragraph of “The Great Extreme” or God, corresponds to that given of Him by the Egyptian Philosophers; for example: “Ammon in his books calleth God <term>most hidden</term> <note>original Greek: πάγκρυφον, pankryphon</note>, and Hermes plainly declareth that it is hard to conceive God, but impossible to express him.”³ “God <note>original Chinese: 神, shén</note> is not confined to place and is Omnipresent. He unites Himself to the Mind of Man, which thus has its origin in Unity. Reason and Unity are but forced names of God <note>original Chinese: 神, shén</note>; if we—

# NOTES.

---

God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note> for the power of Motion and also of the **force of inertia** <note>original Latin: vis inertiæ</note> which he possesses. In the formed Kosmos <note>The universe conceived as a complex, harmonious system.</note> this Fate or First God is inherent in the divided Primordial Air <note>Qi: The vital material force or breath of the universe.</note> which is Mind or the **Lord on High** <note>original: Shang-te (Shangdi)</note>; but, with regard to origin He generated that Air or Mind or the **Lord on High**.

(33.) By the “Great Extreme” <note>Taiji: The "Supreme Ultimate" or the highest principle of the universe.</note> in this paragraph is meant the self-existent Fate or God; although, properly, He does not receive that designation until the formation of the Kosmos displays His power; or, when the Matter in which He is inherent divides into two kinds <note>Referring to Yin and Yang.</note> in order to generate the world.

(34.) The animated Kosmos is properly speaking “The Great Extreme,” which grows up from Chaos, and forms one whole, just like a tree with its numberless branches. When the end of each Kalpa <note>A vast period of time in Eastern cosmology, signaling a cycle of world creation and destruction.</note> arrives, then generation ceases for a while, and all things return to the bosom of Chaos or The Great Extreme, *i. e.* <note>that is</note> Fate or God inherent in the Primordial Air.

(35.) The revolutions of the Seasons, as well as the greater revolutions of the Kosmos, are all brought about by the power of the inherent Unity or God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note>, who adorns all nature by His presence.¹

(36—42.) The Light and Darkness <note>Yin and Yang</note> are the twofold division of the Primordial Air or “Mind,” when the latter comes forth from Chaos to generate the world, his body. The inherent self-existent Fate or God must not be confounded with this twofold animated Air. The latter is properly speaking “The Great Extreme” <note>Taiji</note> while the proper title of the former is “The Infinite” <note>Wuji: Often translated as the "Limitless" or the formless void from which the Supreme Ultimate arises.</note>; and the inseparability of the two is expressed by the conjunction “and,” thus, “***Infinite and Great Extreme.***” <note>Wuji er Taiji</note> These must never be separated or confounded together. Before Chow-tsze <note>Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), a foundational figure of the Neo-Confucian movement.</note> adopted this phrase to describe the Great Origin of all things, there was danger, according to Choo-tsze <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the primary synthesizer of Neo-Confucian philosophy.</note>, of the Incorporeal Fate or God being confounded with the Primordial Air in which He is inherent, and which He animates by His presence, both being included under the one title “Great Extreme,” just as they are under the one title “Nature.” In paragraph 42, “The Great Extreme” refers to Fate or God, who, as the Divine Reason <note>Li: The underlying principle or law of all things.</note>, makes Mind to be Mind, yet Reason and Mind must not be confounded together. They are totally distinct although not separate things.

(43.) In the **Book of Changes** <note>original: Yih King (I Ching)</note> the formation of the Kosmos is expressed in numbers. One or “The Great Monad” <note>The ultimate single unit or primal unity.</note> which is Mind, or the **Lord on High** <note>original: Shang-te</note>, or Heaven, is the Light or subtile Ether; Two or Duality is Darkness or the grosser Air or Matter (Earth); and the union of these two, forms the Triad, which is the Kosmos and also The First Man. These are the “Three Powers of Nature” <note>Sancai: Heaven, Earth, and Humanity.</note>. The Monad consists of Unity inherent in One; the latter being generated by the former; but it is unnecessary always to state this, and hence The Great Monad is styled “The Great Extreme,” although in reality he receives this title merely in virtue of the inherent Unity, or Reason, or Fate or God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note>.

Pythagoras <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and mathematician who taught that number was the essence of all things.</note> held that Monad “is the fountain of all number. The Duad is imperfect and passive, and the cause of increase and division. The Triad, composed of the Monad and Duad, partakes of the nature of both,”² etc.

(44.) “The Great Extreme” in this paragraph is the Primordial Air or Great Monad or the **Lord on High** <note>original: Shang-te</note>, who by his gyrations generates Light and Darkness under the

<summary>This page analyzes the Neo-Confucian concepts of the Supreme Ultimate (Taiji) and the Limitless (Wuji), interpreting them through the lens of Western classical philosophy as "Fate" or "God" versus the "Primordial Air" (Qi). It explains the cosmological formation of the universe through the transition from Unity (the Monad) to Duality (Yin and Yang) and finally the Triad (the Kosmos).</summary>
<keywords>Neo-Confucianism, Taiji, Wuji, Shangdi, Qi, Li, Zhou Dunyi, Zhu Xi, Pythagoras, Monad, I Ching, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed commentary on Neo-Confucian metaphysics, specifically discussing the relationship between the unchanging divine principle (Shen) and the transformative material world (Qi) as described in the Classic of Changes.</meta>

# NOTES.

---

guidance of the inherent Unity or God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note>. In paragraph 45, the latter or “Incorporeal Great Extreme” <note>Taiji: The "Supreme Ultimate," understood here as a non-physical principle.</note> is meant.

(46.) The meaning of the title *Classic of Changes* <note>original: 易 經 (Yih King/Yi Jing)</note> is “The Classic of Change;” that is to say, the *Classic of Changes* is the Classic which contains the history of the various Changes and Transmutations which take place during the formation of the various parts of the Cosmos <note>The universe conceived as a harmonious, orderly system.</note>. As however Fate or Unity or the First God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note> never changes, it is the Primordial Air <note>Qi: The vital breath or material force of the universe.</note> or The Great Monad or the Lord on High <note>original: Shang-te (Shangdi)</note>, in which He is inherent, which undergoes these various changes, and hence this Air is designated “change.” The sentence in the *Classic of Changes*, therefore, to which Zhu Xi <note>original: Choo-tsze; the preeminent Neo-Confucian philosopher of the 12th century.</note> alludes, means that “change has the Great Extreme, which generated the Two Powers <note>original: Two E (Liangyi); referring to Yin and Yang.</note>,” etc. That is to say, in the centre of The Great Monad or the Primordial Air, which divides into the Two Powers, Light and Darkness, is the First God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note>, who is inherent in it and who confers upon it the power to act as the Divine Craftsman <note>original: Demiurge; a philosophical term for the entity that fashions the physical world.</note> in the generation of the Cosmos. The Two Powers or Heaven and Earth <note>original: Khëen-khwăn (Qian and Kun), the primary hexagrams of the Classic of Changes.</note>, as this twofold Air is designated in the *Classic of Changes*, form The Great Extreme, properly so called, who generates the Cosmos from his own substance, Air, and who rules and governs it when generated. “(The Great Extreme) is Heaven and Earth <note>Qian-Kun</note> united in one Air. Because of his *one* God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note> he is called The Great Extreme.” “Heaven and Earth <note>Qian-Kun</note> is The Ruler <note>original: Shang-te</note> who governs the Myriad of things <note>All things in the created world.</note>, and pervades the midst of the Six Children <note>The other six trigrams of the I Ching representing natural forces.</note>; but God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note> refers to Him who adorns the Myriad of things.”¹

Zhu Xi and the other Confucianists, therefore, most distinctly insist that the one Indivisible Unity or God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note> is far above the Lord on High <note>original: Shang-te</note> or the animated Cosmos, upon whom “He rides as a man rides upon a horse;” and this animated Cosmos, like Plato’s, is only the second God, or the Demon-god body and soul. It is plain also that Mind, or Light, or the Lord on High <note>original: Shang-te</note>, (like the elder Cupid or Light) is the first generated god, born from chaos.

(47—50.) God inherent in the Primordial Air, is the Great Origin of all things. God <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note> is the Incorporeal Reason <note>Li: The principle or pattern that governs the universe.</note>, and the Air is the corporeal Receptacle. When the subtile Air or Mind is in chaos, that is His Rest or non-manifestation; and when all things spring forth into being, that is His Motion or manifestation. The title “Great Extreme” given to Mind or the Lord on High <note>original: Shang-te</note> corresponds exactly to the title “*Small Beam*” <note>original Latin: Tigillum; the central supporting beam of a structure.</note> given to Jupiter, which signifies “the centre beam of a house.”

<header>NOTES.</header>

### HEAVEN AND EARTH.

This section opens with the formation of the <term>Universe</term> <note>original: Kosmos; the universe conceived as an orderly and harmonious system.</note> from the <term>Great Origin</term> of all things, namely, <term>God</term> (神) <note>original: 神 (Shen); here referring to the divine spirit or governing principle.</note> inherent in the <term>Primordial Air</term> <note>original: Qi; the vital energy or material force of the cosmos.</note>. This animated <term>Primordial Air</term>, which is The <term>Great Monad</term> <note>original: Taiyi; the supreme oneness.</note> or <term>Heaven</term> or the <term>Lord on High</term> <note>original: Shang-te (Shangdi).</note>, now begins to revolve, the power of <term>Motion</term> being bestowed upon him by the inherent <term>First God</term> (神). At first the entire circle is dark, watery, and chaotic, but as the whole mass revolves, <term>Light</term> or the ethereal <term>Fire</term> is set free, and ascends up to the highest and outermost circle of the heavens, shining through the Air and constituting the animated visible <term>Heaven</term>; the lower portion forming his ethereal vehicle. The sediment which floats about in this muddy watery chaos, settles down, in consequence of <term>Heaven</term>'s gyrations, and congealing, forms the <term>Earth</term> which remains immoveable in the centre, resting as the Greek philosopher <term>Anaximenes</term> held, “like a broad leaf” on the Air of <term>Heaven</term>. The <term>Sun</term>, <term>Moon</term> and <term>Stars</term> are made from the <term>Primordial Air</term> and are therefore animated, like all the other parts of the <term>Universe</term>, by the one <term>God</term> (神) inherent in it, and in all its parts; who pervades all things without division of substance.

(Par. 1–4.) The first agreement with western <term>Philosophy</term> to be noticed here is, that during the <term>Chaotic period</term>, the whole face of <term>Nature</term> is enveloped in <term>Darkness</term>.^1 Such was the doctrine of the <term>Egyptians</term>, for instance, who are said to have deified the *shrew-mouse* <note>original: mus araneus</note>, which is blind, because they considered that “darkness was older than light.”^2 This darkness is the <term>Female Principle</term> of the <term>Confucianists</term>, and is designated *Yin* <note>The passive, dark, or feminine force in nature.</note>; which is the *Source/Womb* <note>original: Yoni; a Sanskrit term for the female generative organ or source.</note> of the <term>Hindus</term>, and the goddess *Juno* of the <term>Greeks</term> and <term>Romans</term>. According to the <term>Confucianist Cosmogony</term>, therefore, <term>Darkness</term> or <term>Night</term> is the <term>Mother of the Gods</term>. This was also the doctrine of the mythical poet <term>Orpheus</term> and others.^3

The firstborn from <term>Chaos</term> and <term>Night</term>, is the <term>Light</term>, (see par. 6). This <term>Light</term> is a pure ethereal <term>Fire</term> and is made to be an <term>Intellectual Mind</term>, as we have seen, by the inherent <term>Divine Reason</term> or <term>God</term>. “<term>Intellect</term>, <term>Sensation</term> and <term>Motion</term> belong to the <term>Light</term>; bodily form to the <term>Darkness</term>.”^4 This <term>Light</term>, or ethereal <term>Intellectual Fire</term>, or <term>Mind</term>, is situated at the highest and outermost circle of <term>Heaven</term>, “the highest part of the upper portion” of which “is most pure and bright,” (par. 3.) “<term>Mind</term> is the brilliant portion of the Air.”^5 That is to say, he is the highest of the nine spiral circles of which the animated <term>Heaven</term> consists. This animated <term>Heaven</term> by his gyrations preserves the <term>Earth</term> in existence, and carries around with him Day and Night and the <term>Sun</term>, <term>Moon</term> and <term>Stars</term>. This <term>Light</term> or <term>Mind</term> is designated *The Creative* <note>original: Khëen (Qian); the first hexagram of the Yi Jing, representing Heaven and active energy.</note> in the *Classic of Changes* <note>original: Yih King (Yi Jing)</note>; “*The Creative* is the commencement of all things, hence he is designated <term>Heaven</term>, and <term>Light</term>, and <term>Father</term>, and <term>Prince</term>.”^6 Lastly, this <term>Mind</term> or <term>Heaven</term> being now born from <term>Chaos</term> consorts with <term>Earth</term>, his <term>Mother</term>, and these two generate all things; “*The Creative* is <term>Heaven</term>, and hence he is styled <term>Father</term>; *The Receptive* <note>original: Khwân (Kun); the hexagram representing Earth and passive energy.</note> is <term>Earth</term>, and hence she is styled <term>Mother</term>.” “<term>Heaven</term> and <term>Earth</term> are the <term>Father</term> and <term>Mother</term> of all things.” “Mar-

->**The Twelve Earthly Branches and the Cosmic Cycle**<-

<note>This diagram illustrates the Chinese zodiacal system used to measure both time and the stages of universal creation and destruction.</note>

1. **Zi** (子) <note>Transliterated as Tsze; corresponding to the Rat.</note>
2. **Chou** (丑) <note>Transliterated as Chaou; corresponding to the Ox.</note>
3. **Yin** (寅) <note>Corresponding to the Tiger. Text: "1st Month: Spring commences."</note>
4. **Mao** (卯) <note>Transliterated as Maou; corresponding to the Rabbit.</note>
5. **Chen** (辰) <note>Transliterated as Shin; corresponding to the Dragon.</note>
6. **Si** (巳) <note>Transliterated as Sze; corresponding to the Snake.</note>
7. **Wu** (午) <note>Transliterated as Woo; corresponding to the Horse.</note>
8. **Wei** (未) <note>Corresponding to the Goat.</note>
9. **Shen** (申) <note>Corresponding to the Monkey.</note>
10. **You** (酉) <note>Transliterated as Yu; corresponding to the Rooster.</note>
11. **Xu** (戌) <note>Transliterated as Seuh; corresponding to the Dog.</note>
12. **Hai** (亥) <note>Transliterated as Hae; corresponding to the Pig.</note>

Each complete revolution of this circle is called a <term>Great Cycle</term> (**元**) <note>original: Yuen; often equated with the Sanskrit term Kalpa, a vast period of time in Eastern cosmology.</note> and consists of 129,600 years. Each subdivision is called a <term>Conjunction</term> (**會**) <note>original: Hwuy</note>, and is supposed to consist of 10,800 years. In the first <term>Conjunction</term> (*Zi*) Heaven or the <term>Lord on High</term> <note>original: Shang-te (Shangdi); the supreme governing deity.</note> comes forth, the door of the <term>World Egg</term> <note>original: Ovum Mundi; a common mythological motif representing the universe in its earliest, unhatched state.</note> being opened, in which he was preserved from the preceding <term>Great Flood</term> <note>original: Deluge</note>. In the second (*Chou*) his wife Earth comes out; and in the third (*Yin*) the First Man appears, who is their son, or the union of both his parents (the <term>Unit and the Pair</term> <note>original: Monad and Duad; Pythagorean terms for the number one and two, representing the source and its first division.</note> or Heaven and Earth) in human form. This Man comes forth from the <term>World Egg</term> in Spring, and Confucius made this period the first month of the year, in imitation of the <term>Xia Dynasty</term>¹ <note>original: Hea Dynasty; the semi-mythical first dynasty of China (c. 2070–1600 BC).</note>. This First Man is evidently the <term>Ordered Universe</term> <note>original: Kosmos</note> or the <term>Lord on High</term> in human form, while the <term>Ordered Universe</term> or <term>Lord on High</term> or <term>Universal Mind</term> is merely this First Man in his deified or worldly character; and hence we are distinctly told in the Confucian Classics that “MAN *is the Mind of Heaven and Earth*.”²

The <term>Great Flood</term> which reduces all things to primitive Chaos, prevails during the last or 12th <term>Conjunction</term>, which is the ninth period from the appearance of the First Man, just as the period of the <term>Biblical Flood</term> <note>original: Noetic Deluge; referring to Noah’s flood.</note> was; and, on the return to the first <term>Conjunction</term> (*Zi*) the <term>Ordered Universe</term> is again generated from Chaos as before. The Cycle of 60, formed by the combination of the above circle with another of 10 divisions, is said to have been invent—

<summary>This page explains the Chinese concept of a "Great Cycle" (Yuen), lasting 129,600 years, during which the universe is created, populated by humanity, destroyed by a flood, and reborn. It equates the Chinese "First Man" with the cosmic mind and draws parallels between these cycles and Western/Biblical traditions.</summary>

<header>NOTE.</header>

...ed by "Naou <note>Likely referring to Da Nao (大撓), a legendary figure credited with inventing the sexagenary cycle, though the author suggests a phonetic link to the biblical Noah.</note> the Great" after the Deluge.^1 The period of the destruction of the Universe is represented under the **Po Diagram** <note>The *Po* (Stripping) hexagram in the I Ching, representing decay or the falling away of old forms.</note> of the **Yih King** <note>The *I Ching* or Book of Changes.</note>, in which each round of Nature terminates, and all things come to an end in consequence of the universal depravity of mankind.^2 The "**Mean Man**" <note>original: *xiaoren*; a person of low moral character or small-mindedness.</note> gains the ascendency in this Diagram, and the "**Prince**" or Model Man <note>original: *junzi*; the noble or superior man.</note> or **Shang-te** <note>The Lord on High or Supreme Deity.</note> retires into quiescence. At the following or **Fuh Diagram** <note>The *Fu* (Return) hexagram, representing the return of light and the beginning of a new cycle.</note> however, this Prince, or Heaven, or Shang-te, the "**Mind of Heaven and Earth**," who is preserved in the *World Egg* <note>original: *Ovum Mundi* (Latin); a mythological concept of the universe beginning from an egg.</note> from destruction, by the inherent Fate or First God (**神**), arouses himself from his "non-manifestation," as **Choo-tsze** <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200 AD), the preeminent Neo-Confucian scholar.</note> terms it, and precedes to generate a new body or **Kosmos** <note>original: Greek word for "order" or "the world."</note> from the Primordial Air, or Matter of the old world.

In the **Yih King** each "spreading out" of the world is attributed to **Khëen** <note>Qian; the hexagram representing Heaven and the Creative principle.</note> or animated Heaven, and is compared to the opening of a door, through which all creation issues forth; and each return to Chaos or the *World Egg*, or "The Great Receptacle" as it is called, is attributed to **Khwăn** <note>Kun; the hexagram representing Earth and the Receptive principle.</note> or animated Earth, and is compared to the shutting of a door, so that all things may be safely stored up until the period arrives for them to issue forth again after the Deluge has subsided; "Hence shutting the door refers to **Khwăn**, and opening the door to **Khëen**. Each shutting and opening is Change (i.e. a revolution of the world); and the everlasting succession (of these revolutions) is pervading without obstruction."^3 These are called the "Great Revolutions," and they are compared to a year; for example: "A day has a day's revolution; a month has a month's revolution; and a year has a year's revolution. Heaven and Earth's commencements and terminations are the Great Revolutions." "**Shaou Pih-wăn** <note>Shao Bowen (1057–1134 AD), a scholar of the Song dynasty known for his work on cosmology.</note> says that each complete Great Revolution resembles a year."^4

> "After an interval," says **Seneca**, "in which the deity (i.e. Jupiter, the Mind or soul of Chaos) will be intent upon his own conceptions, the world will be entirely renewed, every animal will be reproduced, and a race of men free from guilt, and born under happier stars, will repeople the earth. Degeneracy and corruption will, however, again creep into the world; for, it is only when the human race is young, that innocence remains upon the earth. The grand course of things from the birth to the destruction of the world, which according to the Stoics, is to be repeated with endless succession, is accomplished within a certain period. This period or fated round of nature, is probably what the ancients meant by the Great Year."^5

> "The Cosmogony of an anonymous Etrurian preserved by Suidas......limits the duration of the universe to a period of 12,000 years, 600 of which passed in the production of the visible world, before the formation of man. Another doctrine ascribed to the Etrurians, which agrees with the tenets of the Stoics, is the entire renovation of nature after a long period or *great year*, when a similar succession of events would again commence."^6

**Plato** also held that "the world will remain for ever, but that by the action of its animating principle, it accomplishes certain periods within which every thing returns to its ancient place and state. This periodical revolution of nature is called the Platonic or Great Year."^7

The author highlights striking parallels between these Chinese hexagram-based cycles and the "Great Year" or periodic renovations taught by Western philosophers like Seneca and Plato.</summary>
