<meta>This page continues the discussion of cosmic cycles and the preservation of the "Mind of Heaven" within the World Egg. It transitions from the physical evidence of past cataclysms to a comparative study of how Greek, Roman, and Chinese traditions describe the origins of humanity and the divinity of the universe itself.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>NOTE.</header>

Xenophanes <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 570–478 BC) known for his early observations of fossils.</note> thought that the Earth “had at one time been covered with water, in proof of which he noticed the numerous shells found inland and on mountain tops, together with the prints of various fish which he had observed in the quarries of Syracuse, in the island of Paros and elsewhere. From these facts he inferred that the earth had once been covered with water, and even that it would again be so covered at some future time to the destruction of animal and human life.”^1 Herodotus, from the same premises, draws a like inference with regard to lower Egypt.^2

With regard to the formation of the First Man, Plato held that he was made from the four Elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth.^3

“It was, we know, the belief of nearly every nation, that the Earth, impregnated by the power of the ether and the stars, brought forth from its womb in different places the progenitors of the human family. Hence Brutus, commanded by the oracle to salute his mother, kissed the earth, an action of his that very few have rightly comprehended. Similar was the notion of the Gauls, when they affirmed that they were the descendants of Dis <note>A Roman god of the underworld, often identified with the earth's fertile depths.</note>, that is, the Earth.”^4

(7, 8, 9, 10.) Heaven and Earth having come forth from the <term>World Egg</term> <note>original: *Ovum Mundi*; a mythological concept where the universe or a primordial creator deity is hatched from an egg.</note>, in which they were preserved during the Deluge, now become the Great Father and Mother of all things. With these, they form one complete whole which is worshipped entire in all its parts and members, in detail, in the state religion of China.^5 <term>Heaven</term> or <term>Lord on High</term> <note>original: *Shang-te* (Shangdi); the supreme deity in ancient Chinese theology.</note> surrounds the Earth, and Earth itself is made from his substance, so that the whole <term>Universe</term> <note>original: *Kosmos* (Greek); meaning the world or ordered universe.</note> is styled “<term>Heaven</term>.” Thus <term>Shang-te</term> is the <term>Heaven</term> above the Earth, and also the portion below the Earth, that is to say, <term>the Underworld</term> <note>original: *Hades*; the Greek term for the realm of the dead.</note>. He is, in a word, a Great Monad <note>A single, indivisible ultimate unit or being.</note> and yet all things; Earth, Sun, Moon, and Stars, Mountains, Rivers, Trees, Plants, Birds, Beasts, Reptiles etc., being all made from his substance, Air; and all being his various parts and members. Hence all are worshipped equally with himself. “With regard to the whole (<term>Heaven</term> or <term>Universe</term>), then Earth is this one <term>Heaven</term>, and the six children are also this one <term>Heaven</term>,” or <term>Shang-te</term>.*^6 The family saved from each Deluge, according to the <term>Book of Changes</term> <note>original: *Yih King* (I Ching).</note>, are <term>Qian</term> <note>original: *Kheen*; the hexagram representing Heaven and the creative principle.</note>, <term>Mind</term> or <term>Shang-te</term> or <term>Heaven</term>, <term>Kun</term> <note>original: *Khwān*; the hexagram representing Earth and the receptive principle.</note> or <term>Earth</term>, his wife, three sons and their three wives (and sisters). This family of Eight are the <term>Gods of the Greater Nations</term> <note>original: *Dii Majorum Gentium*; in Roman mythology, the twelve primary Olympian deities.</note> of the Pagan world.^7

So also in the West, the animated <term>Universe</term> or <term>Jupiter</term> was called <term>Heaven</term>; for example: “The world, and that which by another name is called <term>Heaven</term> (<gloss>cœlum</gloss>), by whose revolutions all things are governed, ought to be believed a <term>Divine Power</term> <note>original: *Numen*; a Latin term for spiritual force or divinity.</note>, eternal, immense, such as never was made, and shall never be destroyed.”^8 “Let the universe then be called <term>Heaven</term>, or by any other name which it usually receives,”^9 etc. Hence this <term>Heaven</term> or <term>Jupiter</term> is called by <term>Valerius Soranus</term> “<term>The One God who is all things</term>” <note>original: *Deus unus at omnes*.</note>. And <term>Jupiter</term> himself addresses the other gods as “<term>Heaven-dwellers, my own members, Gods</term>” <note>original: *Cœlicolœ, mea membra, Dei*.</note>.^10 etc. The portion below the Earth, or <term>the Underworld</term>, was also <term>Heaven</term> or <term>Jupiter</term>; and hence <term>Orpheus</term> calls “<term>Jupiter</term> and <term>the Underworld</term> (personified as <term>Pluto</term>), one and the same God.”^11 <term>Aristotle</term> states that <term>Anaximenes</term> also regarded his <term>Primordial Air</term> as “God,” that is to say, as <term>Heaven</term> or <term>Jupiter</term>.^12

(11, 12.) The <term>Primordial Air</term> or <term>Great Monad</term> (<term>太一</term>) <note>The *Taiyi*, or Great One/Great Monad in Chinese philosophy.</note> generates the visible

<summary>This page explores the parallels between Chinese and Greco-Roman concepts of a pantheistic universe, where the Supreme Deity (Shang-te or Jupiter) is synonymous with the physical world and its elements. It highlights the belief in a cyclical destruction and rebirth of the world, emerging from a "World Egg."</summary>
<keywords>Xenophanes, fossils, Plato, four elements, Shang-te, Taiyi, I Ching, Jupiter, pantheism, World Egg, deluge, comparative religion</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the World Egg (Ovum Mundi) theory, specifically examining how the Supreme Deity (Shang-te) is conceptualized as the "Mind" of the universe, inhabiting a physical "Body" composed of the elements. It draws extensive parallels between Chinese Neo-Confucianism, Egyptian mythology, and Platonic philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>NOTES.</header>

world from his own substance. The refined portion or Ether is his Mind, and the coarser Air he forms into the visible world as his body, which he fills and brings to life, just as the Mind fills and brings to life the body in a human being. Outside the whole body of Heaven or the <term>Kosmos</term> <note>The universe seen as a well-ordered and beautiful whole.</note>, is a hard ethereal shell which preserves this divine animal from destruction: so that, even in its organized state, the Universe is regarded as an egg, in which every living thing is preserved, and which floats on the waters of Chaos with its eight inhabitants while the Great Flood prevails.

(15.) The natural color of Heaven or <term>Shang-te</term> <note>original: 上帝; the "Supreme Emperor" or "Highest Deity" in Chinese tradition.</note> is a deep azure approaching black. $^1$ Both the Hindus and Egyptians represent this Great Father "Heaven" as being of a black or dark azure color. The latter called this <term>Demiurge</term> <note>A deity responsible for fashioning the physical world.</note> Cneph, and worshipped him, personified, "in a statue of human form, and a blackish sky-colored complexion... and thrusting forth an egg out of his mouth, by which was meant the world." $^2$

(19, 20, 21.) Mind, or the refined ether (<term>Shang-te</term>), is endowed with intelligence by Fate, and holds a middle place, as we have seen, between this First God and the coarser Primordial Air which he (<term>Shang-te</term>) fills and brings to life, and from which he forms his body—Heaven and Earth, or the visible world. Compared with the First God he is regarded as being "physical," while, in comparison with his body the world, he is considered to be a "spiritual" substance. The distinction between this Mind or the second god, and Fate or the First God, is insisted upon most distinctly in paragraph 20. Mind or *Shang-te* would cease to be a Mind if Fate or the First God (the Incorporeal Reason) were not inherent in it; so that these two, although wholly different and distinct in their nature, must never be regarded as separated from each other. *Shang-te* or Mind, the Intellectual Ethereal Fire, is the Ruler of all the lesser Gods, as well as of the world his body; and if Heaven or the Universe were not animated by this ethereal Mind, then, "Oxen would produce horses, and peach trees would send forth plum-blossoms," as there would be no Ruler to generate the various parts of the universe according to their kind. In Chaos, this Creative Mind or *Shang-te* remains still and passive, as if engaged in meditation, until the time arrives for the arrangement of a new Universe from the ruins of the old world; that is to say, from the Primordial Matter in which he exists, and of which he is the most refined and ethereal portion. This Mind is designated <term>Khëen</term> <note>original: 乾 (Qián); the first hexagram of the Yih King, representing the Creative, Heaven, and strength.</note> in the <term>Yih King</term> <note>The I Ching or "Book of Changes," an ancient Chinese divination text and philosophical foundation.</note> because he is "Hard" ether, and he is called "*Shang-te*" because he rules his body the world, just as the Mind rules the body in Man. The complete Universe or Heaven, therefore, is merely a Man, while Man is a <term>Microcosm</term> <note>A "little world"; the idea that a human being mirrors the structure of the entire universe.</note>. $^3$ "Heaven (*Shang-te* or the Universe) is a Mold—a Great Man; Man is a little Heaven" (Microcosm). "Especially must this be done by us, whose ancestor (Confucius) and Heaven (*Shang-te* or the Universe) are one." $^4$ "The Sage is the <term>Active Principle</term> <note>original: 用 (yòng); the functional application or soul-like activity of a substance.</note> of Heaven and Earth." $^5$ <note>original title: 性理 (Xìnglǐ); referring to the *Xingli Daquan*, a Neo-Confucian compendium on "Nature and Principle."</note> "Man's head is round like Heaven, and his feet are square like Earth." $^6$ <note>original: Choo-tsze (Zhu Xi), the influential 12th-century Neo-Confucian scholar.</note> According to the *Yih King*, Heaven or *Shang-te* or the Universe is but a Man. Plato also held that "the skull (in Man) was made spherical in exterior form like the Universe," that is, "Heaven" or Jupiter. $^7$

(22, 23.) The period during which Mind or *Shang-te* is still, is when he is shut up in the <term>World Egg</term> <note>original: *Ovum Mundi*</note>, to preserve him during the period of the Flood. From

<summary>This page explains the Neo-Confucian and Classical concept of the universe as a divine organism, where the deity Shang-te acts as the "Mind" or soul inhabiting the "Body" of the physical world. It emphasizes the "Man as Microcosm" theory, comparing Chinese texts like the I Ching and the works of Zhu Xi to the philosophies of Plato and ancient Egyptian religion.</summary>

<keywords>Shang-te, Microcosm, World Egg, I Ching, Zhu Xi, Plato, Cosmology, Demiurge, Ether, Neo-Confucianism</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Neo-Confucian concept of the universe—specifically the interaction between the active "Mind" (Khëen) and the passive "Body" (Khwăn)—is functionally identical to the Stoic and Platonic systems of the West. It focuses on the second deity, the world-soul, who organizes matter under the direction of a higher, impersonal Fate.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>NOTES.</header>

this he comes forth to generate all things. This <term>Khëen</term> <note>original: 乾; the "Creative" principle in the Yih King (I Ching), symbolizing Heaven and the active mind.</note> or Mind is the second god and pervades all things, being in fact the rational soul of the world. He is the Mind in Man, as well as the principle of life in the lower creation. That which pervaded the **universe** <note>original: "το παν" (to pan)</note> in the West was called **God** <note>original: "Θεος" (Theos) or "Deus"</note> or **Mind** <note>original: "Νους" (Nous) or "Mens"</note>, and was the second god, and this same thing precisely, the Confucianists call the **Spirit** <note>original: 神 (Shen)</note> or God *Shang-te* or Mind; the second God. Thus the soul of the <term>Kosmos</term> <note>The universe seen as an ordered and harmonious system.</note> is called "Mind" both in the Confucian and in the Western systems of Philosophy; in both also, he is the subtile ether or the Light, endowed with reason by the First God, or the Incorporeal Fate, to which latter, in both, precisely the same titles are given, and the same attributes ascribed. The parallel, both as to the First God and also as to the second, is beyond question. Of Jupiter, the rational soul of the *Kosmos*, Virgil says:

> "Mind moves the mass, and mingles itself with the great body. Thence comes the race of man and beast, the life of flying things, and the monsters that the sea bears beneath its marble surface."<note>1; original Latin: "Mens agitat molem, et magno se corpore miscet. Inde hominum pecudumque genus, vitæque volantum, Et quæ marmoreo fert monstra sub æquore pontus."</note>

Aristotle held "that animals have souls derived from that most subtile ether or soul which pervades Heaven, the Air, and the Stars."<note>2</note> "Chrysippus asserted the ether to be the principal god, and to pervade all things." "Souls are thus bound to and connected with God as being parts of and **fragments plucked** <note>original: "decerptions"</note> from himself."<note>3</note> Plato held that the soul in Man "is derived by emanation from God; but that this emanation was not immediate, but through the *intervention of the soul of the world*, which was itself debased by some material admixture," &c. (*i. e.* with the **Breath of Life** <note>original: "Anima"</note>; the <term>Khwăn</term> <note>original: 坤; the "Receptive" principle in the I Ching, representing Earth and physical matter.</note> of the <term>Yih King</term> <note>The I Ching or Book of Changes.</note>).<note>4</note> "Man, according to the Stoics, is an image of the world; one whole composed of body and Mind. The Mind of Man is a spark of that Divine fire which is the soul of the world."<note>5</note> Pythagoras, Empedocles, and the philosophers of the Italic school, held that "we men have not only a conjunction amongst ourselves with one another, but also with the gods above us, and with brute animals below us; because there is but one spirit, which, like a soul, pervades the whole world, and unites all the parts thereof together."<note>6</note> Hence as Heaven, Earth and all things are *Shang-te* in China, so were they Jupiter in the West. This explains the statement of <term>Mencius</term> <note>A famous 4th-century BCE Confucian philosopher who discussed human nature.</note>, that, "That whereby man differs from the lower animals is but small."<note>7</note>

This Mind or *Shang-te*, the animated Air, is generated by the First God, who is called Fate, and "The Good" (see Part I, above, par. 2, 7). So also Jupiter. Plato, for instance, held that this "Mind or Intellect" was generated by the first **Substance** <note>original: "Hypostasis"</note>, **the Good** <note>original: "τ'αγαθον" (to agathon)</note>,<note>8</note> and this Mind henceforth usurps the place, titles, and attributes of the First God, and is "generally by all men looked upon as the first and highest God."<note>9</note> So it is in China; although the Confucianists distinctly acknowledge an eternal, self-existent Unity or Divine Reason higher than Mind or *Shang-te*, who is but the second god and the <term>Demiurgus</term> <note>The artisan-deity who fashions the physical world.</note>, yet, all worship this second god, as being "the immediate architect of the world," and virtually exclude the first God altogether.<note>10</note>

(27, 28.) Hence, while <term>Choo-tsze</term> <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the primary philosopher of Neo-Confucianism.</note> holds that there must be a Ruler in the Kosmos, he denies, like all the rest of the non-Christian world, that this Ruler is a personal being separate from Matter. He is merely a Mind or soul who acts under a higher power, Fate, who "rides upon him as a man rides upon a horse."

It argues that while Neo-Confucianism recognizes a higher "Fate" or "Reason," it is the second god—the Mind—that acts upon matter to create the world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the Neo-Confucian worldview and Western classical philosophy, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of the divine. It draws parallels between the Confucian "First God" and Aristotle's "Unmoved Mover," and identifies the "Primordial Air" (Qi) with the World Soul of the Greeks and Romans.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>NOTES.</header>

(21.) The God of the Confucianists is identical to the First God of Western Philosophers; for example, the First God <note>original Greek: Θεός (Theos)</note> is designated by Aristotle as "the first unmoved mover" <note>original Greek: το πρῶτον κινοῦν ἀκίνητον</note>.¹ Hence, in the Confucian terminology, this God <note>original: 神 (Shén); spirit or divinity</note> "moves yet moves not, rests yet rests not"; that is, He bestows these powers upon <term>Shang-te</term> <note>The "Supreme Emperor," here identified with the animated Primordial Air or World Soul.</note> or the animated <term>Primordial Air</term> <note>The vital energy or matter (Qi) that constitutes the universe.</note> in whom He is inherent. Meanwhile, He Himself, as He occupies "the pivot" or center of motion, is not affected by either Motion or Rest.

(22.) Here again, the First God—Fate or Reason—is carefully distinguished from the second god, namely, the Primordial Air, or Monad, or *Shang-te*, in whom He is inherent. *Shang-te*, or the Air, is His Ethereal Receptacle, and He transforms this Air into a Mind by His inherent presence.

(23, 24.) The Primordial Air is not only Light and Darkness, or the animated Heaven and Earth, but these two Beings are styled astronomically as the Sun <note>original: 太陽 (Tàiyáng), the "Great Yang"</note> and the Moon <note>original: 太陰 (Tàiyīn), the "Great Yin"</note>.

(25.) In this paragraph <term>Choo-tsze</term> <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the influential Neo-Confucian philosopher whose commentaries became the basis of the imperial examinations.</note> gives a full description of the divided Primordial Air or the two great Principles of all things: Light and Darkness, the double soul or "Rational-sentient soul" <note>original Latin: *Animus-anima*</note> of the visible Heaven and Earth.² The Circle or Universe composed of these two is thus represented by the Chinese, with the exception of the cardinal points which are added here for the sake of clearness.

<columns>2</columns>

<image-desc>A circular diagram (the Taijitu or Yin-Yang symbol) divided by an S-shaped curve. The left side is white (Yang) and labeled "Light or Khëen." The right side is black (Yin) and labeled "Darkness or Khwǎn." The diagram is oriented with South at the top, North at the bottom, East on the left, and West on the right.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

*a.* The Light is the *Animus Mundi* <note>Latin: "Soul of the World"; the rational, active principle.</note>, or the Rational soul of the world; and the Darkness is the *Anima Mundi* <note>Latin: "Soul of the World"; the sentient, passive, or irrational principle.</note> or the sentient and Irrational soul of the world; for "The soul of the *Yang* (Heaven) is God (the Light), and the soul of the *Yin* (Earth) is Demon (the Darkness)." <note>The author uses "Demon" to translate the Chinese *Guǐ* (鬼), which refers to ancestral or earthly spirits, contrasting with *Shén* (神), the heavenly or bright spirits.</note>

*b.* This complete circle is the Demon-god *Shang-te*; for, "The Darkness is Demon, the Light is god <note>original: 神 (Shén)</note>." "Demon and god, are the Air."³

*c.* These are designated the Light *Khëen* <note>Qian; the hexagram representing Heaven and the creative.</note> and the Darkness *Khwǎn* <note>Kun; the hexagram representing Earth and the receptive.</note> in the <term>Yih King</term> <note>The I Ching, or Book of Changes.</note>; and the body of this double soul is the

<column-break/>

visible world or Heaven and Earth.⁴ These two souls "fill up the midst of Heaven and Earth" and animate them, so that these two Beings have the power "to make and transmute," and thus become the Great Father and Mother of all things. The animated Heaven or *Shang-te*, soul and body, is the first-born from the Chaotic egg—like the Love or Cupid of Western <term>Cosmogony</term> <note>Stories or theories about the origin of the universe.</note>—and the animated Earth (*Yin*, *Yoni*, or the goddess *Juno*) is his wife. These two Beings, according to the *Yih King*, emerge with "the six Children" (three sons and their three wives), constituting a family of eight deities—the "Gods of the Greater Nations" <note>original Latin: *Dii Majorum Gentium*</note> of the West—from the World-Egg <note>original Latin: *Ovum Mundi*</note> in which they are preserved from a fearful Deluge which utterly destroys a previous world in consequence of the degeneracy of its inhabitants. Heaven and Earth are, in fact, the *Cælus* <note>Latin: Sky</note> and *Terra* <note>Latin: Earth</note> of Western Cosmogony, and are the oldest of the gods; "Heaven," says

It interprets Chinese cosmology through the lens of a "World Egg" and a universal flood, identifying Chinese principles with Aristotle's "Unmoved Mover."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the comparative analysis of the Neo-Confucian concept of the "First God" and the "Divine Reason" (Tao) with Western classical philosophy. While the previous page focused on the identification of the Primordial Air with the World Soul, this page moves toward the moral implications of being "one with the universe" and draws parallels between Confucian trinities and those of the Egyptians, Platonists, and Zoroastrians.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>
### NOTE.

The former is exhorted to virtue; for example: "Man is one thing with Heaven and Earth, why then should he demean himself?"$^1$ "Heaven, Earth, and the myriad of things, are one substance with my body; when my Mind is properly adjusted, the Mind of Heaven and Earth is properly adjusted."$^2$ "The God <note>original Greek: Θεός (Theos)—Rational soul or Mind</note> <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shén)</note> in Man, is the God <note>original: 神 (Shén); Mind or the Supreme Emperor (Shang-te)</note> of Heaven and Earth; so that when Man demeans himself, he demeans Heaven and Earth. Can he then venture to do so?"$^3$ Hence also the First God or the Divine Reason, is inherent in Man’s Mind as well as in the Mind of the Universe <note>original: Kosmos</note>, and this is what Confucius means when he says, in the passage already quoted, "My Reason <note>original: 道 (Dào); Tao</note> is the all-pervading Unity." That Reason or Fate or God is an Indivisible Unity we have already seen. Hence Mencius <note>A follower of Confucius known for his belief in the innate goodness of human nature.</note> says that the sage "preserves the traces of God" <note>original: 神 (Shén)—the Divine Reason</note> in the Universe.

f. It is plain from what has been stated that there are three Hypostases <note>Fundamental underlying substances or realities.</note> in the animating and governing power of the Universe, according to the Confucian system, namely: 1. "Fate" or "Reason" or "The good" or "God," who is an Indivisible "Unity;" 2. "Mind" or Rational soul; and 3. "Sentient soul" or *vital breath* <note>original Latin: anima</note>. Hence, in forming the Universe, the First God uniting Himself with the subtile ether thus places Reason in that Rational soul; next He places that soul in body, namely, the visible Heaven and Earth, and thus completes the animated Great Ultimate <note>original: Great Extreme or Kosmos; the Taiji</note>, which Choo-tsze <note>Zhu Xi</note> pronounces to be a most Divine <note>original: 至神 (Zhìshén)</note> thing. Now, we have here clearly, the "three divine hypostases" of the Egyptians with the Pythagorean or Platonic trinity of first, "the One" <note>original Greek: τὸ ἕν (to hen)</note> or "the Good" <note>original Greek: τἀγαθόν (tagathon)</note>, unity and goodness itself; secondly, "Mind" <note>original Greek: νοῦς (nous)</note>; and thirdly, "Soul" <note>original Greek: ψυχή (psuchē); i.e. anima</note>."$^4$ Further, Plato describes the formation of the Universe <note>original: Kosmos</note> thus: "In pursuance of this reasoning, placing intellect in soul, and soul in body, he (that is, the First God <note>original Greek: Θεός (Theos)</note>) constructed the universe." "He generated the universe a blessed god."$^5$ The Mind <note>original Greek: Νοῦς (Nous)</note> of Plato was also a double soul; "Must we not necessarily say that the soul governing and residing in all things that move, governs also Heaven (that is, the universe)? Assuredly. One or more? At least more than one; nor ought we to lay down fewer than two, the one beneficent, the other working contrary things?"$^6$ Hence also, "Man, according to Plato bears the image of the whole world; both the world and Man being a compound of soul and matter, and the soul of both being partly rational and partly irrational."$^7$ "From this we see the nature, as well as the origin and birth of that soul which governs and rules the whole universe. That mundane soul consists of two parts etc. From this soul of the world were derived, according to Plato’s opinion, those souls by which our bodies are governed. What is said of it, therefore, we are to suppose as said of these also. As the soul of the world consists of two parts, one brute and irrational, the other rational and wise, so also our minds."$^8$ "This Ethiopian god (previously mentioned) is that twofold principle of good and evil, the worship of which was so *general* among the nations of antiquity."$^9$

"Zardusht (Zoroaster) affirmed Light and Darkness, Yezdan (Oromasdes) and Ahriman, to be the two contrary principles, which were the origin of every thing subsisting in the world; the forms of nature being produced from the combination of these principles." "Zoroaster, adopting the principle commonly held by the ancients that—

<summary>This page argues that Neo-Confucianism shares a common structural theology with Greek, Egyptian, and Persian philosophies, particularly through the concept of a "trinity" or "three hypostases" (Fate, Mind, and Soul). It highlights the moral imperative in Confucianism derived from the belief that the human mind and the universal mind are essentially one substance.</summary>
<keywords>three hypostases, Platonic trinity, World Soul, Zhu Xi, Zoroaster, Tao, Mencius, the One, Great Ultimate, Nature and Principle</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the Confucian concept of the Supreme Emperor (Shang-te) and the philosophical systems of the West. While the previous page established the "First God" as an indivisible unity present in both the universe and the human soul, this page explores the personification of this power in the celestial bodies (the Sun and Moon) and the human mind, drawing parallels with Stoic, Platonic, and ancient Near Eastern religions.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>NOTE. 159</header>

“...The God who rules within us [forbids us] to depart hence without his command.”¹ <note>original Latin: "dominans ille in nobis Deus, injussu hinc nos suo demigrare." — A quote from Cicero’s Tusculan Disputations, suggesting that our lives belong to God and we should not commit suicide.</note> “......it is a *common practice* with the Stoics <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that taught that virtue is the highest good and based on reason.</note> and Platonists <note>Followers of Plato who believed in a higher realm of perfect forms and a divine soul.</note> to call men gods, as supposing that the sovereign portion of man, namely, the mind and rational soul, emanated from God himself, and is a part of God, and that if man bestows due care on this part, and abstracts it from body, he then becomes wholly like unto God, nay altogether a god.”² “Wherefore in order to be consistent, they ought also to have placed all souls among the gods, and to have mutually paid divine honours to each other.”³

As the Light or Mind or the Supreme Emperor <note>original Chinese: Shang-te (上帝)</note> is the firstborn God from Chaos;⁴ so also is the Light or Mind or Jupiter of the west, according to Orpheus <note>A legendary figure in Greek myth associated with sacred poetry and the origins of the world.</note>; “But the Earth being then invisible by reason of the darkness, a light breaking out through the Ether illumined the whole creation; this light being said by him to be that highest of all beings (before mentioned) which is called also counsel and life.”⁵ Hence also the *Supreme Emperor* is the same as the Chaldean Bel or Baal <note>Ancient Mesopotamian and Levantine titles for "Lord" or chief deities.</note>, the Mind of the Universe <note>original: Kosmos</note>, from whom all other Minds are derived, and who is also said to have formed Light and Darkness or Heaven and Earth, by dividing himself in two.⁶ Confucius says of the Great One <note>original Chinese: 太一 (Taiyi)</note> or the *Supreme Emperor*, that “he *dividing*, became Heaven and Earth, and gyrating produced Light and Darkness,”⁷ &c.

Lastly, these two Principles are the Sun and Moon. Choo-tsze <note>Zhu Xi, the most influential Neo-Confucian philosopher (1130–1200 AD).</note> says, “The Sun and Moon placed in opposition are God and Demon.”⁸ “The Light and Darkness placed in opposition are Heaven and Earth, Sun and Moon.” “The Light and Darkness are synonymous with the Sun and Moon.”⁹ Ching-tsze <note>Cheng Yi, a key philosopher whose ideas formed the basis of Neo-Confucianism.</note> says, “The Light-Dark Air <note>The "Qi" or vital breath that makes up all things.</note> is Eternal and disperses not, it is the Sun and Moon.” He also says “Heaven and Earth, Sun and Moon are the same.”¹⁰ &c. “In offering the chief sacrifice, the Great Recompense is made to Heaven (the *Supreme Emperor*), and the Sun rules as Lord. He mates with the Moon.” “O Sun, O Moon, coming forth from the East, my Father and Mother, who unceasingly nourish me!”¹¹ The Sun and Moon therefore are the Great Father and Mother, or Heaven and his wife Earth, regarded astronomically; “By the seminal influence of the Sun and Moon the Stars were produced.”¹²

So also, “The Persians added the worship of the Moon to that of the Sun, and regarded the Moon as the Sun’s wife.” “As therefore the Sun was called the god Mithras, so his inseparable companion the Moon, seems to have been called by them the goddess Mithra.”¹³ “The prostrations and adorations that are used both by the Greeks and all Barbarians towards the rising and setting Sun and Moon (as well in their prosperities as adversities) declare them to be unquestionably esteemed gods.”¹⁴ Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who bridged Greek and Jewish thought.</note> says, “Wherefore some admiring with a kind of astonishment the nature of both these worlds (*i. e.* invisible ideas and visible bodies) have not only deified the whole of them, but also the most excellent parts in them, as the Sun, and the Moon, and the whole Heaven, which they scruple not at all to call gods.”¹⁵ Hammon <note>Amun, the chief Egyptian deity often associated with the Sun.</note> the chief god of the Egyptians, like the *Supreme Emperor*, was also the Sun,¹⁶ as, in fact, was also the chief god of every Pagan system throughout the world. Jupiter, like the *Supreme Emperor*, was the great Mind <note>original: Nous or Mens</note> of Heaven or the Universe, and resided in the Sun; and hence he was thus

<page-num>160</page-num>
# NOTES.

addressed in prayer, "‘O All-powerful Sun, the mind and spirit of the whole world.’"¹
"‘The Spirit <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shen)</note> of Heaven (the soul of the **Supreme Emperor** <note>original Chinese: Shang-te (上帝)</note>) resides in the Sun, just as the spirit of a man resides in his eye.’" — *Kangxi Dictionary* <note>The standard Chinese dictionary compiled under the order of the Kangxi Emperor in the early 18th century.</note>.

(27.) The Light and Darkness—that is to say, the <term>Spirit-god</term> <note>The original text uses "Demon-god," likely referring to the Greek *daimon*, a presiding spirit or divine power, rather than an evil entity.</note> **Supreme Emperor**—permeates everything in the whole universe, serving as the principle of life in each thing. As the twofold soul of every part of the <term>Cosmos</term> <note>The universe seen as a well-ordered whole.</note>, he is invisible and must not be confused with physical matter; nonetheless, he is inherent within it and cannot be separated from it.

(28.) The <term>Divine Reason</term>, or <term>Fate</term>, or <term>First God</term>, must not be confused with the **Supreme Emperor** or the <term>Spirit-god</term> (that is, the <term>Light and Darkness</term>). However, He is inherent within them, and therefore they should not be considered to exist separately from Him.

The latter sentences of this paragraph refer to the opinions of the <term>Taoists</term>, who hold, like the <term>Platonists</term>, that although God and Matter are eternally united together, this union is not absolutely necessary to the existence of the former. This the <term>Confucianists</term> deny; they hold, in common with the <term>Stoics</term>, that this connection is as necessary to the existence of God as the connection between body and soul is necessary to the existence of Man.

(29, 30, 31.) A reference to Part III, paragraphs 5 and 6, will make these paragraphs clear.

(33.) The <term>Light</term> or **Supreme Emperor** is God <note>original Chinese: 神 (Shen)</note> by virtue of the inherent <term>Fate</term> or Reason, who is the <term>First God</term>. This <term>Light</term> or **Supreme Emperor** is also the <term>Virtuous Nature</term> which Man receives at birth, created to be so by the inherent <term>Fate</term>; and he is the Great <term>Mind</term> <note>original Greek: Nous (Νους); original Latin: Mens (printed in text as "Meus")</note> who permeates every portion of his body—the world.²

<meta>This page concludes the series of notes on the theological and philosophical comparisons between Zhu Xi's Neo-Confucianism and Western classical thought. It follows a discussion on the role of the Sun and the relationship between the divine spirit and material reality.</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
# NOTES.

### SUMMARY.

To sum up: Zhu Xi <note>A prominent 12th-century philosopher whose interpretations of Confucianism became the foundation of Chinese official education for centuries.</note> and the Confucianists, along with the philosophers of ancient Greece and Rome, hold the following core beliefs:

1.º That the <term>Cosmos</term> <note>The universe seen as a well-ordered and harmonious system.</note> or Universe is a living, animated being, which they call "Heaven."

2.º This Heaven is viewed as a "Great Man," while an individual human is a <term>Microcosm</term> <note>A "little world" or miniature version of the entire universe.</note>. Both are composed of the same fundamental components: the divine inherent within physical matter.

3.º The <term>Cosmos</term> is made of both Body and Soul (or Mind). The visible world is its body, where Heaven acts as the head and Earth acts as the feet. This Soul or Mind is twofold—partly Rational (governed by reason) and partly Sentient (governed by feeling).

4.º The human mind is the same as the Mind of the <term>Cosmos</term>, consisting of a refined, intellectual energy or "ether." Like the universe, a human's head corresponds to Heaven and the feet to Earth.

5.º Heaven is the "First Man," and Man is Heaven. In other words, the First Ancestor, Sage, and Ruler of humanity possesses *a double character*: both worldly (divine) and human.

6.º All Gods are understood to be souls; for this reason, the use of physical images or idols is rejected. In these systems, one cannot find a *personal* God that exists entirely separate from material matter.

7.º These thinkers are simultaneously Monotheists and Pantheists; they believe the Divine Reason or First God is a perfect Unity that flows through and exists in all things.

8.º The <term>Demiurgic Mind</term> <note>The "craftsman" or secondary divine power that organizes and shapes the material world.</note> is considered the *second God* and must be carefully distinguished from the First God.

9.º They hold the doctrine that "nothing comes from nothing" <note>original Latin: *ex nihilo nihil fit*</note>, and consequently believe that Matter is eternal and has no beginning.

10.º All these systems assign nearly identical titles and attributes to the First God, who resides within eternal Matter. Finally, Zhu Xi derives his entire philosophical system from the <term>Book of Changes</term> <note>original Chinese: *Yih King* (I Ching), the oldest of the Chinese classic texts, used for divination and philosophical inquiry.</note>, the most ancient Chinese classic in existence.

-> T H E   E N D . <-

<summary>This page provides a ten-point summary comparing Neo-Confucian philosophy with Greco-Roman thought, emphasizing the unity of the divine and material worlds. It concludes the commentary by identifying the Book of Changes as the ultimate source of Zhu Xi's system.</summary>
